optics.bigb
= Optics
{wiki}

Optics studies <light>, its propagation, and instruments for forming images and measuring <optical spectra>.

The central question is how <light> carries information from a source to an observer. A useful description must explain both where the <light> goes and how its distribution changes. In <geometrical optics>, <light rays> locate paths and images; in <physical optics>, an <electromagnetic wave> has a <wave amplitude> and a <wave phase>, so overlapping contributions can produce <wave interference> and <diffraction>. These descriptions address different levels of detail within the same subject. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/1-1-the-propagation-of-light[OpenStax: The propagation of light]{ref}.

An image is especially interesting because a single object point emits <light> in many directions. An <optical lens> redirects part of that spreading bundle toward a corresponding image point; repeating this construction over the object preserves spatial information. Simply gathering more <light> is insufficient: <light rays> from distinct object points must arrive at distinct locations. This is why an <optical camera> needs both an <optical lens> and a correctly positioned receiving surface. The <thin lens> example below makes that correspondence quantitative. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-3-images-formed-by-refraction[OpenStax: Images formed by refraction]{ref}.

= Radiometry
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

<Radiometry> measures the <energy> and <power> carried by <electromagnetic radiation>, including <light>. It asks how much radiation a source supplies, where it travels, and how much reaches a receiving surface. The measurements are physical rather than weighted by the sensitivity of the human eye: equal <radiant fluxes> at different <wavelengths> need not look equally bright. https://www.nist.gov/publications/radiometry-and-photometry-review-vision-optics[Yoshihiro Ohno, Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics, 2000]{ref}.

The five quantities below answer different questions about the same radiation. The <International System of Units> makes the distinctions visible: a watt is a joule per second, while a steradian measures <solid angle>. https://www.nist.gov/pml/special-publication-811/nist-guide-si-chapter-4-two-classes-si-units-and-si-prefixes[NIST Guide to the SI, Chapter 4: derived units]{ref}.

|| Quantity
|| What is resolved?
|| Units

| <Radiant flux> $\Phi$
| Total radiation <power> in the specified beam or collection
| $\mathrm W$

| <Radiant intensity> $I$
| <Power> per outgoing <solid angle>
| $\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$

| <Radiance> $L$
| <Power> per <projected area> and per <solid angle>
| $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$

| <Irradiance> $E$
| Incident <power> per receiving <area>
| $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$

| <Radiant exitance> $M$
| Outward emitted <power> per emitting <area>
| $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$

Resolving <wavelength> or <frequency> adds a further “per unit interval”, as in <spectral radiance>. The notation is consistent with NIST's table of spectral quantities. In particular, <radiant flux> is <power>, whereas the existing astronomical <radiative flux> is <power> per <area>. https://tsapps.nist.gov/publication/get_pdf.cfm?pub_id=901354[NISTIR 6657, A Rational Methodology for Estimating the Luminous Flux Based upon Color Primaries from Digital Projection Displays, 2009, Table 1]{ref}.

This bookkeeping makes an <optical camera>'s collected signal calculable from a source's <radiance> and the geometry of the receiving aperture. It also lets radiation measurements from different instruments be compared. For example, NIST describes separate calibrations of <spectral radiance> and spectral <irradiance> for instruments used to observe Earth. https://tsapps.nist.gov/publication/get_pdf.cfm?pub_id=104550[Johnson, Brown and Rice, Metrology for remote sensing radiometry, §§3.4.1–3.4.2]{ref}.

= Radiant flux
{parent=Radiometry}
{title2=$\Phi=dQ/dt$}
{wiki}

= Radiant power
{synonym}

<Radiant flux> is the <power> carried by <electromagnetic radiation>: radiation <energy> transferred per unit <time>, measured in <watts>, $\mathrm W=\mathrm{J\,s^{-1}}$. Specify what is counted: all radiation emitted by a source, radiation within one beam, or radiation intercepted by a <photodetector>. Writing $Q(t)$ for the cumulative <energy> crossing the specified boundary gives
$$
\Phi_e(t)=\frac{dQ}{dt},\qquad
\overline\Phi_e=\frac{Q(t_2)-Q(t_1)}{t_2-t_1},\qquad
Q(t_2)-Q(t_1)=\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\Phi_e(t)\,dt.
$$
The first expression is an instantaneous <derivative>; the second is an average over the stated <time> interval. If $0.020\,\mathrm J$ arrives during $0.010\,\mathrm s$, the mean <radiant flux> is $2\,\mathrm W$. The <photodetector>'s absorbed <power> equals this incident <radiant flux> only if it absorbs all of the incident radiation.

Keeping <radiant flux> fixed while widening a beam reduces its <irradiance>, the incident <power> per receiving <area>. <Radiance> additionally resolves the propagation direction and has units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$; an <optical camera> can collect different <radiant fluxes> from sources with the same <radiance> but different apparent <solid angles>. <Luminous flux>, measured in lumens, weights the <optical spectrum> by the human eye's standardized response instead of counting <energy> equally at every <wavelength>. Equal <radiant fluxes> need not give equal <luminous fluxes>.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

<Radiant power> also differs from the <optical power> of an <optical lens>, which is reciprocal <focal length> in <dioptres>. Likewise, astronomical <radiative flux> is <power> per <area>. Stating the units prevents these shared words from hiding different physical quantities.

= Radiant flux through an oriented surface
{parent=Radiant flux}
{title2=$\Phi_{\rm net}=\Phi_+-\Phi_-$}

In <geometrical optics>, suppose radiation has a nonnegative <radiance> $L(\mathbf x,\mathbf s)$ whose directional contributions add as <power>. Here $\mathbf s$ is the actual propagation <unit vector>, $d\Omega$ is a <solid angle> element, and the collecting <oriented surface> $A$ has chosen <unit normal> $\mathbf n(\mathbf x)$. Assume the following <integrals> exist. The outward and inward <radiant fluxes> are
$$
\Phi_+=\int_A\!\int_{\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s>0}
L(\mathbf x,\mathbf s)(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s)\,d\Omega\,dA,
\qquad
\Phi_-=\int_A\!\int_{\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s<0}
L(\mathbf x,\mathbf s)(-\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s)\,d\Omega\,dA.
$$
Both quantities are nonnegative; their difference is the signed net <radiant flux>. The <dot product> supplies the <cosine> of the <angle> with the <unit normal>, hence the <projected area>. For incoming radiation the outward <unit normal> gives a negative <dot product>; the minus sign makes the collected <power> positive. Measuring the incoming <angle> from a source-facing <unit normal> instead gives the familiar positive $\cos\theta$. The units check is $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}\times\mathrm{m^2}\times\mathrm{sr}=\mathrm W$.

Equivalently, the incoming <surface integral> is $\Phi_-=\int_A E\,dA$ with local <irradiance> $E$; the emitted part of $\Phi_+$ is $\int_A M\,dA$ with <radiant exitance> $M$. For a small flat patch and a narrow accepted cone, nearly constant <radiance> and <angle> give $\Phi\simeq LA\cos\theta\,\Delta\Omega$. This approximation requires uniformity across the patch and cone. Direction-independent outgoing <radiance> over a whole <hemisphere> gives the different exact result $\Phi_+=\pi LA$, by <Lambert's cosine law> and the <spherical coordinates> calculation in <radiant exitance>. Replacing the <cosine>-weighted <integral> by the <hemisphere>'s $2\pi$ <solid angle> would double the answer.<Radiometry and Photometry>{ref}

These <radiance> formulas presume a description in additive directional <power>; they do not reconstruct arbitrary coherent <wave interference> from separate ray contributions. For general <electromagnetic fields>, the signed instantaneous <power> crossing an <oriented surface> is $\int_A\mathbf S\cdot\mathbf n\,dA$, using the <Poynting vector> $\mathbf S$; optical measurements commonly average over many <wave periods>. A zero net <radiant flux> can conceal equal nonzero incoming and outgoing <radiant fluxes>.

= Beam collection at fixed radiant flux
{parent=Radiant flux}
{title2=$\Phi_{\rm collected}=P\min(1,a^2/b^2)$}

Consider an ideal parallel circular beam with uniform <irradiance> inside radius $b$, zero outside, and total <radiant flux> $P=2\,\mathrm W$. A centred circular collector of radius $a$, perpendicular to the <light rays>, intercepts
$$
E_b=\frac{P}{\pi b^2},\qquad
\Phi_{\rm collected}=E_b\pi\min(a,b)^2
=P\min\!\left(1,\frac{a^2}{b^2}\right).
$$
For $b=1\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $a=0.5\,\mathrm{cm}$, $E_b=20000/\pi\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}\simeq6366.20\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ and the collected <radiant flux> is $0.50\,\mathrm W$. Expanding the beam to $b=2\,\mathrm{cm}$ while keeping $P$ fixed divides <irradiance> and the same collector's <radiant flux> by four: $E_b\simeq1591.55\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ and $\Phi_{\rm collected}=0.125\,\mathrm W$. A collector covering either entire beam still receives $2\,\mathrm W$.

Tilting a flat collector of actual <area> $A$ by an <angle> $\theta$ to the beam direction gives $\Phi_{\rm collected}=E_b A\cos\theta$ if its entire <projected area> remains inside the uniform beam. At $60^\circ$ the small collector's <radiant flux> halves. A collector large enough to intercept the whole beam after tilting still receives $P$: the footprint on its surface grows as $1/\cos\theta$ and cancels the <irradiance> factor. This model ignores <diffraction>, beam-edge structure, and changes in absorption with incidence <angle>; it counts incident rather than absorbed <power>.

\Image[radiant-flux-beam-collection-geometry.png]
{id=radiant-flux-beam-collection-geometry}
{title=Fixed <radiant flux>, different beam widths and collecting <projected areas>}
{description=Original ideal geometry, not measurements. The beam cross-sections use the same centimetre scale and total <radiant flux> of $2\,\mathrm W$; both collectors have radius $0.5\,\mathrm{cm}$. The side view shows a collector's cross-section at $60^\circ$: its projection perpendicular to the <light rays> has half the width, with depth unchanged. The stated half-power result requires the projected collector to stay inside the uniform beam.}

= Spectral radiant flux
{parent=Radiant flux}
{title2=$\Phi_{e,\lambda},\ \Phi_{e,\nu}$}

<Spectral radiant flux> resolves <radiant flux> by <wavelength> or <frequency>:
$$
\Phi_e=\int_0^\infty\Phi_{e,\lambda}(\lambda)\,d\lambda
=\int_0^\infty\Phi_{e,\nu}(\nu)\,d\nu.
$$
Its units are $\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}}$ per metre of <wavelength> or $\mathrm{W\,Hz^{-1}}$ per hertz of <frequency>. A density quoted per nanometre must be integrated with <wavelength> intervals in nanometres; its numerical value is $10^{-9}$ times the per-metre density. The <frequency and wavelength spectral radiance conversion> applies equally to <spectral radiant flux>, through the <change of variables formula>:
$$
\Phi_{e,\nu}(\nu)=\Phi_{e,\lambda}(c/\nu)\frac{c}{\nu^2},
$$
with $c$ the <speed of light> in <vacuum> and $\Phi_{e,\lambda}$ expressed per metre. The <Jacobian determinant> preserves integrated <power> rather than the height or location of an <optical spectrum>'s peak.

For two explicitly synthetic <optical spectra>, let $x=\lambda/(1\,\mathrm{nm})$ and quote the following densities in $\mathrm{mW\,nm^{-1}}$:
$$
f_A(x)=\begin{cases}0.020(1-|x-500|/100),&400\leq x\leq600,\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
\qquad
f_B(x)=\begin{cases}0.010,&600\leq x\leq800,\\0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
$$
The <triangle> under $f_A$ and the <rectangle> under $f_B$ both have <area> $2\,\mathrm{mW}$: $200\times0.020/2=200\times0.010=2$. Their band <radiant fluxes> differ: $450$–$550\,\mathrm{nm}$ contains $1.5\,\mathrm{mW}$ of $A$, while $650$–$750\,\mathrm{nm}$ contains $1.0\,\mathrm{mW}$ of $B$. These are specified functions, not measured source spectra.

\Image[radiant-flux-synthetic-spectral-integrals.png]
{id=radiant-flux-synthetic-spectral-integrals}
{title=Equal total <radiant flux> can hide different band <radiant fluxes> and detector signals}
{description=Original synthetic <optical spectra> with exact total <radiant flux> $2\,\mathrm{mW}$ each. Darker shading marks the stated 100 nm bands. The lower panel uses a stipulated linear <spectral responsivity> $R(\lambda)=\bigl(0.2+0.001(x-400)\bigr)\,\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}}$; integrating it against the two <spectral radiant fluxes> gives dark-subtracted <electric currents> $0.60$ and $1.00\,\mathrm{mA}$. The response is illustrative and is not a calibration curve for a real <photodiode>.}

The dark-subtracted <electric current> of a linear <photodiode> is
$$
i=\int R(\lambda)\Phi_{e,\lambda}(\lambda)\,d\lambda,
\qquad [R]=\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}},
$$
using its <spectral responsivity>. The figure's stipulated $R$ gives $i_A=0.60\,\mathrm{mA}$ and $i_B=1.00\,\mathrm{mA}$ despite equal total <radiant flux>. Symmetry places $A$'s mean <wavelength> at $500\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $B$'s at $700\,\mathrm{nm}$; a linear $R$ averages to its value at that mean. A single <electric current> therefore cannot determine an unknown broadband <radiant flux> without additional spectral information or a sufficiently flat calibrated response.

= Calibrated radiant-flux measurement
{parent=Radiant flux}

An <optical power meter> makes the definition operational: collect a specified beam on a calibrated <photodiode>, measure its <electric current>, and infer incident <radiant flux> using <spectral responsivity>. In the manufacturer-authored <Model 1830-R Series Optical Power Meters User's Manual by Newport Corporation>, §5.5.1 specifies a concrete sequence: connect the detector and its calibration module, select watt mode and the source <wavelength>, block the beam and press ZERO, then unblock it and read the display. The stored background includes <dark current> and ambient <light> present during zeroing. For narrowband radiation,
$$
\Phi_{\rm indicated}=\frac{i_{\rm illuminated}-i_{\rm zero}}{R(\lambda)}.
$$
The <electric current> difference divided by <spectral responsivity> has units $\mathrm A/(\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}})=\mathrm W$. For a stipulated $R=0.40\,\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}}$ and difference $0.20\,\mathrm{mA}$, the inferred <radiant flux> is $0.50\,\mathrm{mW}$; these numbers illustrate the inference and are not reported measurements. The entire beam must hit the active <area>, and §5.6 calls for correct <wavelength>, background subtraction, and operation below detector saturation. If an <optical attenuator> is fitted, §5.4 requires the calibrated detector–attenuator pair and the matching meter setting.

The calibration itself must connect <electric current> to independently known incident <power>. NIST's <Spectroradiometric Detector Measurements: Ultraviolet, Visible, and Near-Infrared Detectors for Spectral Power by Thomas C. Larason and others> describes this chain. A <cryogenic radiometer>, an <electrical substitution radiometer>, compares <temperature> changes from absorbed <light> with those from known <Joule heating> (§5.2). Transfer and working detectors carry that <watt> scale to a spectral comparator (§5.1). A source, <monochromator>, focusing optics, shutter, <beam splitter>, and monitor <photodiode> illuminate a standard and test detector in turn; calibrated current-to-voltage electronics and voltmeters provide the readout (§§3.2.2–3.2.4, 4.3.1). At each <wavelength>, subtract blocked-beam signals and normalize each detector signal by the simultaneously sampled monitor signal. Comparison with the standard yields the test detector's <spectral responsivity>. NIST uses two working standards and alignment normal to the beam (§6.1.1).

Controls distinguish a smaller collected <radiant flux> from a faulty conversion: block and unblock the beam to check the baseline; check that its footprint fits the active <area>; verify the calibrated <optical attenuator> configuration; and test proportional response within the specified operating range. Source drift faster than monitor sampling is not removed. NIST discusses finite spectral bandwidth, stray <light>, <temperature>, and spatial response variations (§§3.1.1, 6.2, 7.2). A broadband <optical spectrum> requires the weighted <integral> in <spectral radiant flux>, rather than dividing by an arbitrary single <spectral responsivity>. These limitations explain why the meter's displayed <watts> need a stated collection boundary, spectral assumptions, and calibration conditions.

= Radiant intensity
{parent=Radiometry}
{title2=$I=d\Phi/d\Omega$}
{wiki}

<Radiant intensity> describes how a source distributes its <radiant flux> over directions. A small cone of <solid angle> $\Delta\Omega$ carries approximately $I\Delta\Omega$ when $I$ varies little across it; integrating $I$ over all emitting directions recovers the total <radiant flux>. For a source with <isotropy> emitting $4\pi\,\mathrm W$ over a full <sphere>, $I=1\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$ and a $0.01\,\mathrm{sr}$ cone carries $0.01\,\mathrm W$. A planar source satisfying <Lambert's cosine law> instead has direction-dependent <radiant intensity>, even though its <radiance> is direction-independent.

= Radiance
{parent=Radiometry}
{wiki}
{title2=$L$}

<Radiance> resolves both position and direction: it is <radiant flux> per <projected area> and per <solid angle>. For a surface element of <area> $dA$, and a small outgoing cone $d\Omega$ at <angle> $0\leq\theta<\pi/2$ to its outward <unit normal>,
$$
d^2\Phi=L(\mathbf x,\mathbf s)\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega,
\qquad [L]=\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}.
$$
Here $\mathbf x$ locates the patch and $\mathbf s$ is a <unit vector> along the outgoing direction. The factor $dA\cos\theta$ is the patch's apparent <area> viewed along that direction. The same local definition applies to transmitted or received radiation, choosing the positive <projected area> for the crossing direction. https://labs.phys.utk.edu/mbreinig/phys421/modules/m5/Radiometry.html[Marianne Breinig, University of Tennessee, Radiometry: radiance and Lambert's law]{ref}.

For a planar patch of <area> $A$ with spatially uniform, direction-independent <radiance> $L$, integrating over the patch gives
$$
I(\theta)=LA\cos\theta.
$$
Take $L=10\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$ and $A=0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}$. The <radiant intensities> are $I(0)=0.20\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$ and $I(60^\circ)=0.10\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$. A sufficiently narrow $0.010\,\mathrm{sr}$ cone therefore carries approximately $2.0\,\mathrm{mW}$ or $1.0\,\mathrm{mW}$, respectively. Both views have the same <radiance> $L$: the oblique view presents half the <projected area>. This is <Lambert's cosine law>, not a loss of <radiance> with viewing <angle>.

\Image[projected-area-radiance.png]
{title=One uniform patch: halving <projected area> halves <radiant intensity> while preserving <radiance>}
{description=The geometric panels show a cross-section of a rectangular patch with the same depth perpendicular to the page. Parallel projection onto a plane perpendicular to the viewing direction changes its width by $\cos\theta$. The graph gives $I(\theta)=LA\cos\theta$ for the stated patch and marks the exact $0^\circ$ and $60^\circ$ cases.}

<Radiance> is useful because it separates a source's local directional output from the geometry of an observation. Integrating over outgoing directions gives <radiant exitance>; integrating incoming <radiance> at a receiver gives <irradiance>. In the stationary <vacuum> setting of <vacuum conservation of specific intensity>, a resolved uniform source retains its <radiance> along each <light ray>, while the <radiant flux> received from the whole source can decrease as its apparent <solid angle> shrinks.

= Projected area
{parent=Radiance}
{title2=$A_\perp=A\cos\theta$}

The <projected area> of a planar patch viewed from a direction is the <area> of its perpendicular shadow onto a plane normal to that direction. If $\mathbf n$ and $\mathbf s$ are its <unit normal> and viewing <unit vector>, then $A_\perp=A|\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf s|$ by the <dot product>. On the outward side, this is $A\cos\theta$. For a <rectangle> tilted around one edge, the width perpendicular to that edge contracts by $\cos\theta$ and the edge length stays fixed, proving the formula. At grazing view $A_\perp=0$; interpret the <radiance> definition by a limit rather than dividing by zero.

= Spectral radiance
{parent=Radiance}
{title2=$L_\nu,\ L_\lambda$}
{wiki}

= Specific intensity
{synonym}

<Spectral radiance> is <radiance> per unit <frequency> or <wavelength> interval. Its astronomical synonym <specific intensity> is usually written $I_\nu=L_\nu$ or $I_\lambda=L_\lambda$; this $I_\nu$ is a spectral <radiance>, distinct from the <radiant intensity> $I$ in <radiometry>. For positive small intervals,
$$
d^3\Phi=L_\nu\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega\,d\nu
=L_\lambda\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega\,d\lambda.
$$
The two expressions refer to the same spectral slice, with the positive widths related through <frequency> $\nu$ and <wavelength> $\lambda$. For <frequency> measured in hertz, $\mathrm{Hz}=\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, the units are $[L_\nu]=\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,Hz^{-1}}$. For <wavelength> measured in metres, $[L_\lambda]=\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,m^{-1}}$. Integrating over a spectral band gives its <radiance>, and then multiplying by the collecting <projected area> and accepted <solid angle> gives a small uniform beam's <radiant flux>. A spectral measurement therefore retains information that the band-integrated <power> alone loses.

For an ideal <blackbody>, <Planck's law> gives $L_\nu=B_\nu(T)$ and $L_\lambda=B_\lambda(T)$ at <temperature> $T$, independent of outgoing direction. Its existing <frequency and wavelength peaks of Planck's law> illustrate why the coordinate matters. The simpler flat-band calculation below isolates that same <change of variables> without the thermal physics.

= Frequency and wavelength spectral radiance conversion
{parent=Spectral radiance}
{title2=$L_\nu=L_\lambda(c/\nu)\,c/\nu^2$}

In <vacuum>, $\nu=c/\lambda$, where $c$ is the <speed of light>. Increasing <wavelength> decreases <frequency>, so the ordinary signed <differentials> satisfy $d\nu=-c\lambda^{-2}d\lambda$. A spectral slice's positive contribution to <radiance> must be unchanged. The <change of variables formula> therefore gives the absolute <Jacobian determinant>
$$
L_\nu(\nu)=L_\lambda(c/\nu)\left|\frac{d\lambda}{d\nu}\right|
=L_\lambda(c/\nu)\frac{c}{\nu^2},
\qquad
L_\lambda(\lambda)=L_\nu(c/\lambda)\frac{c}{\lambda^2}.
$$
These formulas use $L_\lambda$ per metre and $L_\nu$ per hertz. The factor $c/\nu^2$ has units $\mathrm{m\,s}=\mathrm{m\,Hz^{-1}}$, which converts the former density into the latter. If $\ell_{\rm nm}$ denotes the numerical density per nanometre in $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,nm^{-1}}$, its per-metre value is $10^9\ell_{\rm nm}$. Conversely, the per-nanometre value is $10^{-9}$ times the per-metre value. Likewise, a density per terahertz is $10^{12}$ times its per-hertz value because $1\,\mathrm{THz}=10^{12}\,\mathrm{Hz}$.

For a concrete stipulated spectrum, let $L_\lambda=C=10^9\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,m^{-1}}$ between $\lambda_1=500\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $\lambda_2=600\,\mathrm{nm}$, and zero outside. This is exactly $1\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,nm^{-1}}$ throughout the band. Using the exact <International System of Units> value $c=299792458\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, the corresponding <frequency> bounds are
$$
\nu_1=\frac{c}{\lambda_2}=499.654096666\ldots\,\mathrm{THz},
\qquad
\nu_2=\frac{c}{\lambda_1}=599.584916\,\mathrm{THz}.
$$
The transformed <spectral radiance> is $L_\nu=Cc/\nu^2$ on this interval. At $\nu_1$ it is approximately $1.200830743\times10^{-12}\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}\,Hz^{-1}}$, and at $\nu_2$ it is $8.339102380\times10^{-13}$ in the same units. These are respectively $1.200830743$ and $0.833910238$ per terahertz. Thus a flat <wavelength> density becomes a decreasing <frequency> density; relabelling the horizontal axis alone would miss both the shape and the units. https://www.nist.gov/si-redefinition/meet-constants[NIST, Meet the Constants: exact speed of light]{ref}.

The equality of band <radiances> has a short exact proof:
$$
\int_{\lambda_1}^{\lambda_2}C\,d\lambda
=C(\lambda_2-\lambda_1)
=100\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}},
$$
$$
\int_{\nu_1}^{\nu_2}\frac{Cc}{\nu^2}\,d\nu
=Cc\left(\frac1{\nu_1}-\frac1{\nu_2}\right)
=C(\lambda_2-\lambda_1).
$$
The reversed bounds and the <Jacobian determinant> preserve the same spectral measure. Each half-band, $500$–$550\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $550$–$600\,\mathrm{nm}$, carries $50\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$, although the corresponding <frequency> widths are about $54.508$ and $45.423\,\mathrm{THz}$.

\Image[spectral-radiance-variable-change.png]
{title=A flat <wavelength> spectrum becomes curved in <frequency>, with equal band <radiances>}
{description=Matching colours identify the same two spectral half-bands. The shaded areas are $50\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$ each in either coordinate. The right-hand density is plotted per terahertz; the longer-wavelength half-band occupies the lower-frequency, narrower interval. Both total shaded areas are exactly $100\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$.}

A spectrum's peak is consequently a statement about a particular density and coordinate. The <frequency and wavelength peaks of Planck's law> differ for precisely this reason. Integrated <radiance> over a specified physical band supplies a comparison that survives the <change of variables>.

= Vacuum conservation of specific intensity
{parent=Spectral radiance}

In a transparent, stationary <vacuum> with <Euclidean geometry> and observers at rest in the same frame, the <radiative transfer equation> has zero absorption and emission, so $dI_\nu/ds=0$ along a <light ray>. The same <specific intensity> reaches the observer without geometric dilution. More generally, $I_\nu/\nu^3$ is a <Lorentz invariant>; collisionless propagation preserves this ratio along a ray even with gravitational or cosmological <redshift>, rather than preserving $I_\nu$ at shifted <frequencies>. https://www.roe.ac.uk/japwww/teaching/ram5/ram5_2018.pdf#page=27[John Peacock, Radiation and Matter, 2018–2019, §4.1, Conservation of intensity and equation (140)]{ref}.

The elementary geometry is instructive. Two facing surface elements separated by distance $s$ exchange spectral <radiant flux>
$$
d^3\Phi=I_\nu\frac{\cos\theta_1\cos\theta_2}{s^2}\,dA_1\,dA_2\,d\nu.
$$
The receiver subtends $d\Omega_2=\cos\theta_2\,dA_2/s^2$ at the source. Describing that same traveling beam at the receiver instead uses $d\Omega_1=\cos\theta_1\,dA_1/s^2$. Both descriptions have identical geometric factors, so conservation of its <radiant flux> gives equal <specific intensities> at the two ends. This compares the same propagation direction at both ends, not emission in the opposite direction.

A uniform source remains equally bright per resolved direction under these assumptions, but occupies a smaller apparent <solid angle> farther away. For a small source of <projected area> $A_\perp$ at distance $d$ and a small receiver facing it, <irradiance> is approximately $E=L\Omega_{\rm source}\simeq LA_\perp/d^2$. The <radiant flux> intercepted by receiver <area> $A_d$ is approximately $EA_d$. With $L=10\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$, $A_\perp=0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and $A_d=10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$, distances $10$ and $20\,\mathrm m$ give $E\simeq0.002$ and $0.0005\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$, and received <radiant fluxes> of $0.20$ and $0.050\,\mu\mathrm W$. The <inverse-square law> concerns collected radiation from the whole source; $L$ remains $10$ at either distance.

= Limb darkening
{parent=Spectral radiance}
{wiki}

Limb darkening is the decrease of emergent <specific intensity> toward the apparent edge of a stellar disc. A transit depth then depends on the intensity sampled by the planet as well as its projected area; the uniform-disc area ratio is only an approximation.

For a spherical <star> of <radius> $R_\star$, let $r$ be distance from the centre of its projected disk and let $\theta$ be the <angle> between the outward <unit normal> and the <line of sight>. The angular coordinate is
$$
\mu=\cos\theta=\sqrt{1-(r/R_\star)^2}.
$$
Disk centre has $\mu=1$; the apparent edge approaches $\mu=0$. A resolved image measures <specific intensity> in each direction. <Limb darkening> means that this <specific intensity> itself falls toward the edge; the <projected area> factor used when collecting <radiant flux> is a separate geometric effect.

A simple explanation assumes a horizontally uniform <plane-parallel atmosphere>, a sufficiently deep <optically thick medium>, negligible <scattering of light>, and thermal absorption and emission in <local thermodynamic equilibrium>. Let $\tau_\nu$ be inward vertical <optical depth> at fixed <frequency> $\nu$. An oblique <light ray> has <line of sight> <optical depth> $\tau_\nu/\mu$, so it encounters the same attenuation at a smaller vertical <optical depth>. The <formal solution of the radiative transfer equation> gives
$$
I_\nu(0,\mu)=\int_0^\infty S_\nu(\tau_\nu)e^{-\tau_\nu/\mu}\frac{d\tau_\nu}{\mu},\qquad 0<\mu\le1.
$$
Here $S_\nu$ is the <source function>; under these assumptions it is the <Planck function> $B_\nu[T(\tau_\nu)]$. If <temperature> increases inward, an oblique view preferentially samples cooler layers and hence lower <specific intensity>. <Radiative Transfer in Stellar Atmospheres by Robert J. Rutten (8th edition)>[§2.2.2, pp. 17–19, equations (2.37)–(2.44) and Figure 2.4]{ref}

The weight $e^{-\tau_\nu/\mu}/\mu$ is an <exponential distribution> in vertical <optical depth>, with <expected value> $\mu$ and <standard deviation> $\mu$. Thus “seeing down to <optical depth> one” refers to the <line of sight>, and represents a broad sampling of layers rather than a sharp emitting surface. For a <source function> that is linear throughout the atmosphere, the <Eddington-Barbier relation> is exact:
$$
S_\nu(\tau_\nu)=a_\nu+b_\nu\tau_\nu
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
I_\nu(0,\mu)=a_\nu+b_\nu\mu=S_\nu(\mu).
$$
This follows by integrating the constant and linear terms separately. A constant <source function> instead gives constant <specific intensity>, as in the <blackbody limit of an isothermal atmosphere>. An outward-rising <temperature> or important <scattering of light> changes the argument. Close enough to the actual edge, a <plane-parallel atmosphere> also loses validity because of curvature. Neither one <limb-darkening coefficient> nor a universal dependence on <wavelength> follows from this simple model.

= Linear limb-darkening law
{parent=Limb darkening}
{title2=$I(\mu)=I_c[1-u(1-\mu)]$}

The <linear limb-darkening law> parametrizes angular <specific intensity> by a stipulated <limb-darkening coefficient> $u$:
$$
I(\mu)=I_c[1-u(1-\mu)],\qquad I_c=I(1),\qquad 0\le u\le1.
$$
Here $I$ denotes <specific intensity> at one <frequency>, or <radiance> integrated over a specified interval of <frequency> or <wavelength>; it does not denote <radiant intensity>. Within this stipulated range, $u=0$ gives a uniform disk and $I(0)/I_c=1-u$. The <linear limb-darkening law> is linear in $\mu$, while its radial profile $I(r)=I_c[1-u+u\sqrt{1-(r/R_\star)^2}]$ is curved. In the linear <source function> example above, $u=b_\nu/(a_\nu+b_\nu)$ when $a_\nu,b_\nu\ge0$ and $a_\nu+b_\nu>0$.

For the illustrative choice $u=0.6$, the <specific intensity> at $r/R_\star=0$, $0.8$, and the limiting edge is respectively $I_c$, $0.76I_c$, and $0.4I_c$. These are synthetic values, not measurements of the <Sun>. Real <optical spectra> and <stellar atmospheres> need not obey a <linear limb-darkening law>; more flexible profiles can be necessary for accurate <exoplanet transit photometry>. <Analytic Light Curves for Planetary Transit Searches by Kaisey Mandel and Eric Agol>[§1, p. L171: the linear law is insufficient for the cited high-quality transit observations; §4 defines a quadratic law]{ref}

\Image[stellar-limb-darkening-disk-profiles.png]
{title=Synthetic stellar disk and angular and radial <limb darkening> profiles}
{description=The disk uses the stipulated <linear limb-darkening law> with $u=0.6$. The profiles compare four stipulated <limb-darkening coefficients> at equal central <specific intensity>. Shading represents relative brightness, without prescribing a <wavelength> or copying a solar measurement.}

= Limb-darkening coefficient
{parent=Linear limb-darkening law}
{title2=$u$}

The parameter $u$ in the <linear limb-darkening law> specifies its contrast between disk centre and the limiting edge. Its interpretation requires the chosen law and interval of <frequency> or <wavelength>. A <limb-darkening coefficient> fitted to an <exoplanet transit> may trade off against the <transit impact parameter> and <planet> <radius>; it is not a direct measurement of one layer's <temperature>.

= Limb-darkened disk flux
{parent=Limb darkening}
{title2=$\overline I=I_c(1-u/3)$}

For a circular stellar disk whose <specific intensity> is independent of <azimuth>, its mean <specific intensity> over <projected area> is
$$
\overline I=\frac{1}{\pi R_\star^2}\int_{\rm disk} I\,dA_{\rm proj}
=2\int_0^1 I(\mu)\mu\,d\mu.
$$
The <polar coordinates> element is $dA_{\rm proj}=2\pi r\,dr$ after angular integration, and $r\,dr=-R_\star^2\mu\,d\mu$. Substitution of the <linear limb-darkening law> gives
$$
\overline I=2I_c\int_0^1(1-u+u\mu)\mu\,d\mu=I_c(1-u/3).
$$
For a distant observer at distance $D\gg R_\star$ receiving <light> normally to the detecting plane, the <observed radiative flux> is
$$
f_\nu\simeq\frac{\pi R_\star^2}{D^2}\overline I_\nu
=\pi\left(\frac{R_\star}{D}\right)^2I_{\nu,c}(1-u_\nu/3).
$$
It is spectral <irradiance>, with units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,Hz^{-1}}$ when $I_\nu$ is per hertz. The local outward <radiative flux> per actual surface <area> is instead $F_\nu=2\pi\int_0^1 I_\nu(\mu)\mu\,d\mu=\pi\overline I_\nu$, a spectral <radiant exitance>. In a spherical model with the same <stellar atmosphere> everywhere, the <luminosity> is $L_\nu=4\pi R_\star^2F_\nu$ and $f_\nu=L_\nu/(4\pi D^2)$, consistent with the <inverse-square law>. Compare disks at fixed $I_c$ or fixed total <observed radiative flux> explicitly: increasing $u$ lowers the total in the first comparison, while the second requires a larger $I_c$.

= Small-planet transit weighting
{parent=Limb darkening}
{title2=$\delta\simeq k^2I(\mu_p)/\overline I$}

A dark, opaque circular <planet> of <radius> $R_p$ blocks <specific intensity> over its overlap with the projected <star>. With $k=R_p/R_\star$ and no <transit dilution>, the instantaneous <exoplanet transit depth> is exactly
$$
\delta=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\rm overlap}I\,dA_{\rm proj}}{\pi R_\star^2\overline I}.
$$
When $k\ll1$, the entire planetary disk is inside the stellar disk, and <specific intensity> varies little across that disk, <small-planet transit weighting> reduces to
$$
\delta\simeq k^2\frac{I(\mu_p)}{\overline I}
=k^2\frac{1-u+u\mu_p}{1-u/3},\qquad
\mu_p=\sqrt{1-z^2},\qquad z<1-k.
$$
Here $z$ is the projected centre separation divided by $R_\star$. The numerator samples the local <specific intensity>; the denominator normalizes by the whole <star>'s mean <specific intensity>. The small-size argument and the distinction between full overlap and ingress are explicit in the original analytic treatment. <Analytic Light Curves for Planetary Transit Searches by Kaisey Mandel and Eric Agol>[§2, p. L171, geometry; §5, p. L174, small-planet approximation restricted to the interior $z<1-p$]{ref}

For a short approximately straight crossing at constant transverse <velocity> $v_\perp$, write $x=v_\perp t/R_\star$ with $t=0$ at mid-transit, and let $b$ be the <transit impact parameter>. Then $z^2=x^2+b^2$ and $\mu_p=\sqrt{1-b^2-x^2}$. With stipulated $u=0.6$ and $k=0.05$, a central crossing has mid-transit <exoplanet transit depth> approximately $0.003125$, whereas $b=0.8$ gives $0.002375$. A uniform disk would give $k^2=0.0025$ for either fully overlapping crossing. The brighter central chord produces a deeper dip and the off-centre chord a shorter crossing at the same $v_\perp$.

\Image[limb-darkened-transit-chord-weighting.png]
{title=Two transit chords across the same synthetic <limb-darkened disk flux> distribution}
{description=Stipulated $u=0.6$ and $k=0.05$; the coloured curves integrate the occulted <specific intensity> over the finite planetary disk, including ingress and egress. Dashed curves show <small-planet transit weighting> only where the whole planetary disk is inside the <star>. Horizontal dotted segments give the uniform-disk full-overlap depth $k^2$. The chord panel shows mid-transit positions; the horizontal coordinate in the <light curve> is distance travelled divided by stellar <radius>, equivalent to scaled <time> at constant transverse <velocity>.}

At fixed $z<1$ away from the edge, a two-dimensional <Taylor's theorem> expansion over the symmetric planetary disk cancels the first <derivatives>; the first correction to the blocked <radiant flux> is of order $k^4$ if the second <derivatives> remain bounded. This error estimate is not uniform at the edge, where the radial <derivative> of the <linear limb-darkening law> diverges. Ingress, egress, a grazing <exoplanet transit>, and a crossing of a <starspot> require the overlap <integral> or a suitable more complete model. Inferring $k=\sqrt\delta$ without the local-to-mean <specific intensity> ratio can bias the estimated <planet> <radius>.

= Solar limb-darkening drift-scan experiment
{parent=Limb darkening}

A motivating measurement used the McMath solar <optical telescope> at Kitt Peak. The <Sun>'s image drifted across a $10\,\mathrm{mm}$-high, $0.2\,\mathrm{mm}$-wide <optical slit>; a double-pass <spectrograph> selected <wavelength> intervals from $0.0015\,\mathrm{nm}$ wide at $303\,\mathrm{nm}$ to $0.007\,\mathrm{nm}$ at $1099\,\mathrm{nm}$. An EMI 9750 QB <photodetector> or <silicon> <photodiode> supplied a digitized signal: $1\,\mathrm{kHz}$ samples were summed into 4096 positions per crossing, including dark and off-limb sky readings. <Solar limb darkening 1986–1990 (λλ303 to 1099 nm) by Heinz Neckel and Dietrich Labs>[§2, p. 93, instrumental compilation: slit dimensions, double pass, detectors, sampling and drift-curve layout; p. 113, acknowledgements identifying the telescope]{ref}

Controls included daily realignment of <optical telescope> mirrors, <wavelength> settings between <spectral lines> insensitive to the rotational <Doppler shift>, selection of detectors without detectable <hysteresis>, and measured electronic nonlinearity corrections below $0.1\%$ of maximum signal. Limb-position calibration combined centres of gravity of the edge <derivative> profiles with almanac semidiameters; the preliminary edge shifts were consistent with <astronomical seeing> of approximately $5$ <arcseconds> <full width at half maximum>. <Solar limb darkening 1986–1990 (λλ303 to 1099 nm) by Heinz Neckel and Dietrich Labs>[§2, pp. 93–94, precautions (1), (6)–(8); §3.1, p. 94, two-step limb positions and seeing estimate]{ref}

The decreasing centre-to-limb readout gives a <specific intensity> profile, constraining stratification of the <photosphere> under a <radiative transfer> model. <Astronomical seeing>, instrumental <scattering of light>, and <solar granulation> limit this inference; brightness alone does not uniquely determine <temperature> versus geometric height. These observations need not follow the stipulated <linear limb-darkening law>.

= Radiant exitance
{parent=Radiometry}
{title2=$M=M_e=d\Phi_{\rm emitted}/dA$}
{wiki}

<Radiant exitance> is total outward emitted <radiant flux> per actual emitting <area>, measured in $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. Integrate <radiance> over the outward <hemisphere>, keeping the <projected area> factor:
$$
M=\int_{\Omega_{\rm outward}}L(\theta,\varphi)\cos\theta\,d\Omega.
$$
If the <radiance> is direction-independent, the <spherical coordinates> element is $d\Omega=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi$, so
$$
M=L\int_0^{2\pi}\!d\varphi\int_0^{\pi/2}\cos\theta\sin\theta\,d\theta=\pi L.
$$
The weighted <solid angle> is $\pi\,\mathrm{sr}$, although the whole <hemisphere> covers $2\pi\,\mathrm{sr}$. Directions near grazing contribute less because of the <projected area>. This geometric factor is the bridge between <Lambert's cosine law> and the total output of a diffuse patch. https://labs.phys.utk.edu/mbreinig/phys421/modules/m5/Radiometry.html[Breinig, Radiometry: hemispherical integration of radiance]{ref}.

For the uniform patch with $L=10\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$, <radiant exitance> is $M=10\pi\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. Its <area> $0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}$ gives total <radiant flux> $\Phi=MA=0.20\pi\,\mathrm W\approx0.628319\,\mathrm W$ over the outward <hemisphere>. This total differs from its <radiant intensity> $I(0)=0.20\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$, which describes only one direction.

For an ideal <blackbody>, integrate <Planck's law> over <frequency> as well: $M=M_e=j^*=\sigma T^4$ by the <Stefan–Boltzmann law>. An ideal $6000\,\mathrm K$ emitting patch of <area> $1\,\mathrm{mm^2}$ emits about $73.4881\,\mathrm W$ outward. <Irradiance> counts incoming radiation separately; for purely emitted output the net outward exchange per <area> is $M-E$. Reflected output must also be included if the surface reflects incident radiation.

= Irradiance
{parent=Radiometry}
{title2=$E=d\Phi_{\rm incident}/dA$}
{wiki}

<Irradiance> measures incident <radiant flux> per actual receiving <area>. Its units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ match <radiant exitance>, but it counts the incoming side of the radiation exchange. Measure incoming <angles> from the <unit normal> pointing toward the incident hemisphere. Then
$$
E=\int_{\Omega_{\rm incident}}L_{\rm incoming}(\theta,\varphi)\cos\theta\,d\Omega.
$$
For $2\,\mathrm W$ distributed uniformly over $0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$, $E=200\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. If the receiving plane is tilted $60^\circ$ in the same broad uniform parallel beam, the incident <power> per actual <area> is halved to $100\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. This receiving-surface <cosine> factor is geometric and does not assume <Lambertian reflectance>. Together, <irradiance> and <radiant exitance> describe how radiation supplies and removes <energy> at a surface, while <radiance> preserves the directional information.

= Electrical substitution radiometer
{parent=Radiometry}

= Cryogenic radiometer
{parent=Electrical substitution radiometer}

= Photometry
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

= Photometry
{synonym}

<Photometry> weights visible <electromagnetic radiation> by standardized visual response functions; <radiometry> measures its <energy> and <power> without visual weighting.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

The question is how much <light> a source supplies under a stated visual weighting. A beam containing $1\,\mathrm{mW}$ at the defining <frequency> $540\,\mathrm{THz}$ has <luminous flux> $0.683\,\mathrm{lm}$. Moving the same <radiant flux> to a region of smaller <spectral luminous efficacy> reduces its <luminous flux>. Changing the <optical spectrum> changes this conversion even when its total <power> stays fixed. This makes <photometry> useful for comparing lighting, while <radiometry> remains necessary for an <energy> budget. The <lumen> counts <luminous flux>; the <candela> additionally specifies its distribution over <solid angle>.

Two measurement routes implement the weighting: measure an <optical spectrum> and evaluate a weighted <integral>, or use a <photometer> whose <spectral responsivity> approximates the prescribed weighting. Both require a specified collection geometry. Neither is a complete prediction of an individual's perception of a scene.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

= Photopic luminous efficiency function
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$V(\lambda)$}

The <photopic luminous efficiency function> $V(\lambda)$ is dimensionless and describes standardized relative sensitivity for <photopic vision>. It is normalized to $V(555\,\mathrm{nm})=1$ and tabulated over $360$–$830\,\mathrm{nm}$. The <wavelength> coordinate is in standard air, rather than <vacuum>; changing coordinates requires converting the <spectral radiant flux> density as well. The official prescription uses linear <interpolation> between the specified 1 nm entries.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure/Mise en pratique for the definition of the candela and associated derived units for photometric and radiometric quantities in the SI>[§3.1, printed pp. 2–3: wavelength convention, normalized functions and interpolation]{ref}

Write $\lambda_a$ for the standard-air <wavelength> at $540\,\mathrm{THz}$. With <spectral radiant flux> $\Phi_{e,\lambda}$ in $\mathrm{W\,nm^{-1}}$ and $d\lambda$ in nanometres, the <luminous flux> is
$$
\Phi_v=K_m\int_{360\,\mathrm{nm}}^{830\,\mathrm{nm}}V(\lambda)\Phi_{e,\lambda}(\lambda)\,d\lambda,
\qquad K_m=\frac{K_{\mathrm{cd}}}{V(\lambda_a)},\qquad K_{\mathrm{cd}}=683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}.
$$
The <integral> has units of <watts>, and $K_m$ converts them to <lumens>. For radiation at a single <wavelength> $\lambda_0$ with total <radiant flux> $P$, this reduces to $\Phi_v=K_mV(\lambda_0)P$. For a sufficiently narrow finite band, this is an approximation requiring nearly constant $V$ across the band.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure/Mise en pratique for the definition of the candela and associated derived units for photometric and radiometric quantities in the SI>[§3.1, equation (2), printed p. 3: spectral weighting normalized at the defining frequency]{ref}

= Photopic vision
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{wiki}

<Photopic vision> means vision under light-adapted conditions. Standard <photometry> here uses the <photopic luminous efficiency function>; <scotopic vision> uses a different weighting for dark-adapted conditions. The response function and adaptation conditions must be stated when comparing <luminous fluxes>.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure/Mise en pratique for the definition of the candela and associated derived units for photometric and radiometric quantities in the SI>[§3.1, printed p. 3: photopic and scotopic luminous efficiency functions]{ref}

= Scotopic vision
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{wiki}

= Spectral luminous efficacy
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$K(\lambda)=K_mV(\lambda)$}

<Spectral luminous efficacy> converts <radiant flux> at one <wavelength> to <luminous flux>. The <candela> convention fixes $K_{\mathrm{cd}}$ exactly at $540\times10^{12}\,\mathrm{Hz}$, not at an exactly specified <wavelength> of $555\,\mathrm{nm}$. <SI base unit: candela (cd)>{ref}

In <vacuum>, that <frequency> corresponds to $c/(540\times10^{12}\,\mathrm{Hz})\simeq555.1712\,\mathrm{nm}$, using the <speed of light>. In standard air it is approximately $555.017\,\mathrm{nm}$, close to the $555\,\mathrm{nm}$ maximum of the <photopic luminous efficiency function>. Thus $K_m$ is slightly larger than $683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}$; using $K_m\simeq683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}$ in ordinary calculations is a rounded convention. The exact anchor is $K(\lambda_a)=683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}$. <luminous efficacy of radiation, for a specified photometric condition>{ref}

= Luminous efficacy
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$K=\Phi_v/\Phi_e$}
{wiki}

The <luminous efficacy> of radiation is <luminous flux> divided by total <radiant flux>, in $\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}$, for the specified visual conditions. For nonzero <radiant flux>, it is a spectral <weighted mean> of <spectral luminous efficacy>. Specify the denominator: a lamp's <luminous efficacy> relative to electrical input <power> also includes conversion losses and is a different ratio. <luminous efficacy of radiation, for a specified photometric condition>{ref}

= Equal radiant power under visual weighting
{parent=Photometry (optics)}

An original declared model makes the distinction visible without substituting invented numbers for the <photopic luminous efficiency function>. Define a triangular weight
$$
w(\lambda)=\max\!\left(0,1-\frac{|\lambda-555\,\mathrm{nm}|}{150\,\mathrm{nm}}\right),\qquad
\widetilde\Phi_v=(683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}})\int w(\lambda)\Phi_{e,\lambda}(\lambda)\,d\lambda.
$$
This $w$ is an illustrative function, not a fit or table of $V$. Three ideal spectral lines, each carrying <radiant flux> $1\,\mathrm{mW}$, at $450$, $555$ and $650\,\mathrm{nm}$ have weights $3/10$, $1$ and $11/30$. Their model weighted outputs are respectively $0.2049$, $0.6830$ and approximately $0.25043\,\mathrm{lm}$. These are model calculations, not standard <luminous fluxes> or measured data. For mixtures of these lines the model outputs add, just as the <integral> adds independent spectral contributions.

\Image[photometry-declared-spectral-weighting.png]
{id=photometry-declared-spectral-weighting}
{title=Equal <radiant fluxes> give different outputs under a declared spectral weight}
{description=Original triangular weighting model, not CIE data. Each ideal line carries $1\,\mathrm{mW}$; its drawn height represents line <power>, not a finite spectral density. The lower bars apply the stipulated $683\,\mathrm{lm\,W^{-1}}$ model scale. The model demonstrates the mechanism without supplying calibration data for actual <photometry>.}

= Luminous intensity
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$I_v=d\Phi_v/d\Omega$}
{wiki}

<Luminous intensity> is <luminous flux> per outgoing <solid angle>, measured in <candelas>. It is the visually weighted counterpart of <radiant intensity>. The same total <luminous flux> concentrated into a narrower cone can give a greater <luminous intensity> within that cone.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

= Candela
{parent=Luminous intensity}
{title2=$\mathrm{cd}=\mathrm{lm\,sr^{-1}}$}
{wiki}

At exactly $540\,\mathrm{THz}$, <radiant intensity> $1/683\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$ corresponds to <luminous intensity> $1\,\mathrm{cd}$. This convention specifies a direction, rather than the source's total <luminous flux>. <SI base unit: candela (cd)>{ref}

= Illuminance
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$E_v=d\Phi_{v,\mathrm{incident}}/dA$}
{wiki}

<Illuminance> is incident <luminous flux> per receiving <area>, measured in <lux>. It is the visually weighted counterpart of <irradiance>. Uniform illumination of an aperture of <area> $A$ gives collected <luminous flux> $AE_v$. An aperture measurement alone does not give a source's total emitted <luminous flux>.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

= Lux
{parent=Illuminance}
{title2=$\mathrm{lx}=\mathrm{lm\,m^{-2}}$}
{wiki}

= Photometer
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{wiki}

A <photometer> measures a visually weighted radiation quantity. A filtered <photodiode> approximates the <photopic luminous efficiency function> with its <spectral responsivity>; calibration establishes how its <electric current> maps to <illuminance> or collected <luminous flux>. NIST's detector-based realization traces the <spectral responsivity> to a <cryogenic radiometer> and the aperture <area> to length measurements. <Realization of the candela>{ref}

= Photometric matching filter
{parent=Photometer}

A <photometric matching filter> shapes a <photodiode>'s combined <spectral responsivity> toward the <photopic luminous efficiency function>. Matching is imperfect, so <photometric calibration> must retain spectral information. <Realization of the candela>{ref}

= Photometric calibration
{parent=Photometer}

<NIST Measurement Services: Photometric Calibrations by Yuqin Zong and others> describes a concrete experiment (§§3.2–3.3.2): a <silicon> <photodiode>, multilayer <photometric matching filter>, precision aperture, <temperature> sensor and current-to-voltage converter. A <monochromator> supplies a small beam; scanning <wavelength> yields <spectral responsivity>, and scanning position checks response across the aperture. Finite spectral bandwidth requires correction. The average aperture response converts uniform illumination to <illuminance>.

On the photometric bench (§§3.4.1, 3.5.3, 3.5.6), align lamp and aperture reference planes, set the lamp's <electric current>, stabilize, and compare a check lamp with standard <photometers>. The readout is a <voltmeter> signal. Subtract the shutter-closed reading (§3.4.4), divide by converter gain to infer <electric current>, and use calibrated <illuminance> responsivity $S_A$ to obtain $E_v=i/S_A$. For a distant source, <luminous intensity> follows from $I_v=E_vd^2$, with $d$ between reference planes.

Controls include repeat lamp operations, distance changes to test the <inverse-square law>, <temperature> corrections and gain <linearity> checks. <Spectral mismatch correction> is source dependent (§3.4.3). Unknown <optical spectra>, spatial nonuniformity and incorrect reference planes remain limitations.<NIST Measurement Services: Photometric Calibrations by Yuqin Zong and others>[§§3.2–3.5.6, printed pp. 16–19, 22–28, 33–37: apparatus, dark subtraction, readout, corrections and check-lamp procedure]{ref}

= Spectral mismatch correction
{parent=Photometric calibration}
{title2=$F=\Phi_v/\Phi_{v,\mathrm{indicated}}$}

For collected <spectral radiant flux> $f(\lambda)$ and a linear <photodiode> with <spectral responsivity> $r(\lambda)$, the dark-subtracted <electric current> is $i=\int r f\,d\lambda$. A reference <optical spectrum> $f_A$ sets the conversion $C_A$ from <electric current> to collected <luminous flux>. Applying it to another <optical spectrum> $f_T$ gives
$$
C_A=\frac{K_m\int V f_A\,d\lambda}{\int r f_A\,d\lambda},\qquad
\Phi_{v,\mathrm{indicated}}=C_A\int r f_T\,d\lambda,
\qquad
F=\frac{\int V f_T\,d\lambda}{\int r f_T\,d\lambda}
\frac{\int r f_A\,d\lambda}{\int V f_A\,d\lambda}.
$$
Assume nonzero calibration and test signals. Then $F\Phi_{v,\mathrm{indicated}}$ is the corrected <luminous flux>. Multiplying either <optical spectrum> by an overall positive scale leaves $F$ unchanged. If $r$ is proportional to $V$, $F=1$ for every <optical spectrum>. Uniformly overfilled aperture measurements use <wavelength>-resolved <irradiance> instead, multiplying the <electric current> expression by aperture <area>; the same ratio cancellation gives the <illuminance> correction. This is the algebra behind source-dependent calibration, rather than a new measured correction curve. <Realization of the candela>{ref}

= Calibrated photometer model
{parent=Spectral mismatch correction}

Use the triangular $w$ in <equal radiant power under visual weighting> and stipulate the detector's <spectral responsivity>
$$
r(\lambda)=(0.5\,\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}})w(\lambda)
\left(1+\frac{\lambda-555\,\mathrm{nm}}{150\,\mathrm{nm}}\right).
$$
This model has nonnegative response: $w$ is zero outside $405$–$705\,\mathrm{nm}$. For a $1\,\mathrm{mW}$ ideal calibration line at $555\,\mathrm{nm}$, $i_A=0.5\,\mathrm{mA}$ and $\widetilde\Phi_{v,A}=0.683\,\mathrm{lm}$, so $C_A=1366\,\mathrm{lm\,A^{-1}}$. An equal-<radiant flux> test line at $630\,\mathrm{nm}$ gives $w=1/2$, $r=0.375\,\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}}$ and $i_T=0.375\,\mathrm{mA}$. The unchanged conversion indicates $0.51225\,\mathrm{lm}$, whereas the model's intended weighted result is $0.3415\,\mathrm{lm}$. The required <spectral mismatch correction> is $F=2/3$: the uncorrected result is 50% too large. All these numbers are declared model outputs, not instrument measurements or standard <photometry> data.

\Image[photometry-calibrated-detector-spectral-mismatch.png]
{id=photometry-calibrated-detector-spectral-mismatch}
{title=Calibration at one <wavelength> leaves a spectral mismatch at another}
{description=Original schematic and analytic model. The apparatus shows the signal path of a filtered <photodiode> <photometer>, with shutter-closed subtraction; it is not a drawing to scale of a specific instrument. The lower panels use the declared triangular weighting and detector response, not CIE tables. The two ideal lines carry the same collected <radiant flux>; the source-dependent factor $2/3$ corrects the test indication within this model only.}

= Luminous flux
{parent=Photometry (optics)}
{title2=$\Phi_v$}
{wiki}

<Luminous flux> is the visually weighted counterpart of <radiant flux>, measured in lumens. Its value depends on the <optical spectrum> and the specified visual response; it is distinct from <radiant flux> in <watts>.<OSA Handbook of Optics, Volume III Visual Optics and Vision/Radiometry and Photometry: Review for Vision Optics>{ref}

= Lumen
{disambiguate=unit}
{parent=Luminous flux}
{title2=$\mathrm{lm}=\mathrm{cd\,sr}$}
{wiki}

= Lumen
{synonym}

The <lumen> is the <International System of Units> unit of <luminous flux>: $1\,\mathrm{lm}=1\,\mathrm{cd}\,\mathrm{sr}$. A source with constant <luminous intensity> $1\,\mathrm{cd}$ within a cone of <solid angle> $1\,\mathrm{sr}$ supplies $1\,\mathrm{lm}$ into that cone. A source with the same <luminous intensity> in every direction supplies $4\pi\,\mathrm{lm}$ over a full <sphere>. <SI base unit: candela (cd)>{ref}

= Lambertian reflectance
{c}
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

<Lambertian reflectance> is the ideal diffuse-reflection model with direction-independent outgoing <radiance>, the <frequency>-integrated <specific intensity>. If a fraction $0\leq\rho\leq1$ of incident <radiant flux> is reflected, a surface receiving <irradiance> $E$ has reflected <radiance> $L=\rho E/\pi$. The reflected outward <power> per <area> is $\pi L=\rho E$ by the same hemispherical integral used for <radiant exitance>. Its <radiant intensity> still acquires the <projected area> factor in <Lambert's cosine law>.

For $E=100\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ and $\rho=0.80$, the reflected <radiance> is $80/\pi\approx25.4648\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$. A patch of <area> $0.020\,\mathrm{m^2}$ reflects $1.6\,\mathrm W$ in total, and its <radiant intensities> at $0^\circ$ and $60^\circ$ are respectively $1.6/\pi$ and $0.8/\pi\,\mathrm{W\,sr^{-1}}$. The <radiance> stays fixed as the view changes, provided the illumination stays fixed. Changing the incident <angle> can change <irradiance> and therefore the reflected <radiance>.

= Lambert's cosine law
{c}
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}
{title2=$I(\theta)=I(0)\cos\theta$}

For an ideal diffuse emitting or reflecting planar surface with uniform direction-independent <radiance>, the <radiant intensity> in a direction at <angle> $\theta$ to its <unit normal> is $I(\theta)=I(0)\cos\theta$. This follows immediately from $I=LA\cos\theta$ and the <projected area> formula. The worked <radiance> example and diagram show the $0^\circ$ and $60^\circ$ cases. <Lambertian reflectance> supplies one physical idealization realizing the law; an ideal <blackbody> provides an emitting example.

The historical setting is Johann Heinrich Lambert's \i[Photometria sive de mensura et gradibus luminis, colorum et umbrae], published in Augsburg in 1760. Its Part I, §139, explicitly compares normal and oblique illumination using the sine of the source's elevation, equivalently the <cosine> of its <angle> from the receiving <normal vector>. This is an early receiving-surface projection calculation; the modern outgoing-patch statement above keeps <radiance>, <radiant intensity> and <irradiance> distinct. https://www.e-rara.ch/zut/content/titleinfo/2814923[Lambert, Photometria, 1760, digitized original held by ETH-Bibliothek Zürich]{ref} https://www.e-rara.ch/zut/download/ftpack/plain/2814923[Photometria, searchable text, Part I, §139]{ref}.

= Optical lattice
{parent=Optics}
{title2=$V(x)=sE_R\sin^2(\pi x/d)$}
{wiki}

An optical lattice is a periodic <optical trap> produced by interfering light fields. Its <periodic potential> gives atomic <Bloch states> and <energy bands>. For the displayed one-dimensional lattice, the <recoil energy> is $E_R=\hbar^2\pi^2/(2md^2)$ and $s$ measures the depth in recoil units.

= Condensate compressibility in an optical lattice
{parent=Optical lattice}
{title2=$\kappa^{-1}=n(\partial\mu/\partial n)_s$}

Here $n$ is average <number density> and $\mu$ the ground-state <chemical potential>. This commonly used condensate response has units of inverse energy; thermodynamic <isothermal compressibility> is instead $\kappa/n$. In a weakly interacting repulsive condensate, the normalized one-cell ground state $f_s$, with average $|f_s|^2=1$, gives $\mu=E_0(s)+Un I(s)+\cdots$, $I(s)=d^{-1}\int_0^d|f_s|^4dx$. Localization raises $I$ and reduces $\kappa$ at fixed $n,U,d$. In deep harmonic wells $I\propto s^{1/4}$, hence $\kappa\propto s^{-1/4}$. This is a ground-band, repulsive-superfluid statement with the held variables specified, not a theorem for arbitrary signs of the interaction or arbitrary excited branches.

= Recoil energy
{parent=Optical lattice}
{title2=$E_R=p_R^2/(2m)$}

Recoil energy is the <kinetic energy> gained by an initially stationary atom absorbing a photon of momentum $p_R$. In a standing-wave <optical lattice> of spacing $d$, the photon wavenumber is $\pi/d$, giving $E_R=\hbar^2\pi^2/(2md^2)$.

= Refraction
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

Refraction changes the direction of a transmitted <wave> when it crosses between media with different <phase velocities>. For <light> at a planar interface, <Snell's law> relates the angles measured from the <normal vector> through the <refractive indices>. At normal incidence the direction remains unchanged even though the <wavelength> and <phase velocity> can change. The worked example under <geometrical optics> distinguishes transmission from <total internal reflection>.

= Image formation
{parent=Optics}

<Image formation> assigns each object point a corresponding image point: outgoing <light rays> from that object point either converge there, giving a <real image>, or diverge as though they originated there, giving a <virtual image>. The requirement applies separately to each point. Bringing unrelated <light rays> together makes a bright patch, but does not preserve the object's pattern. This point-by-point correspondence explains how an <optical camera> records a scene. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-4-thin-lenses[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 3, §2.4: Image formation by thin lenses]{ref}.

Use the same convention as <thin lens>: place the <optical lens> at $x=0$, with incident <light> travelling to the right and a real object at $x=-u$, $u>0$. Write the image coordinate as $x=v$, so $v>0$ for a <real image> and $v<0$ for a <virtual image>. Heights $h_o,h_i$ are positive above the <optical axis> and negative below it. In air, within the <paraxial approximation>, a <thin lens> obeys
$$
\frac1u+\frac1v=\frac1f,\qquad v=\frac{fu}{u-f},\qquad m=\frac{h_i}{h_o}=-\frac vu.
$$
Here $f$ is the signed <focal length>, positive for a converging <optical lens> and negative for a diverging <optical lens>; the expression for $v$ assumes $u\ne f$. Object distance $u$ is positive even though the object's coordinate is negative. These coordinate rules describe transmission through the <optical lens>; a mirror reverses the outgoing direction, so do not identify its image distance with this same rightward coordinate. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/25-6-image-formation-by-lenses[Urone and Hinrichs, College Physics 2e, §25.6: Lens equation and sign conventions]{ref}.

The beauty of <geometrical optics> is that two convenient <light rays> can locate a point, while a whole admitted bundle forms its image. For a converging <thin lens>, an incident <light ray> parallel to the <optical axis> exits through the rear <optical focus>; a <light ray> through the centre continues undeviated. Real <optical lens> elements do not focus every admitted <light ray> exactly: <optical aberrations> and <diffraction> limit the point correspondence. The diagrams below are original ideal-model constructions, not measured data. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-4-thin-lenses[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, §2.4: Ray tracing and imaging limits]{ref}.

= Conjugate optical plane
{parent=Image formation}

Two <conjugate optical planes> are object and image planes related by an imaging system: points in one are imaged onto corresponding points in the other in the <paraxial approximation>. For a converging <thin lens> forming a <real image> in air, their distances $u,v>0$ obey $1/u+1/v=1/f$. Exchanging $u$ and $v$ preserves this relation; this symmetry gives the two settings of the <Bessel lens displacement method>.

= Optical bench
{parent=Image formation}

An <optical bench> is a rail or aligned platform carrying an object, <optical lens> elements and a screen or detector at readable positions. It turns <image formation> into measurements of position and <transverse magnification>. Read distances between the optical planes rather than arbitrary holder boundaries; keep heights and <optical axis> alignment controlled. https://www.pasco.com/resources/lab-experiments/484[PASCO, Object and Image Distances Thin Lens: bench apparatus and measurements]{ref}.

= Real image
{parent=Image formation}
{wiki}

A <real image> lies where the outgoing <light rays> from each object point actually intersect in <geometrical optics>. A screen or detector placed at that image plane receives the focused pattern. The screen reveals the <real image>; it does not create the intersection. A viewer can also see a <real image> without a screen if their eye receives the diverging <light rays> beyond the intersection. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/25-7-image-formation-by-mirrors[Urone and Hinrichs, College Physics 2e, §25.7: Real images and their observation]{ref}.

For a converging <thin lens> and real object with $u>f>0$, the <real image> has $v>0$ and signed <transverse magnification> $m<0$. Inversion is a property of this particular setup, not the definition of a <real image>. With $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $h_o=0.50\,\mathrm{cm}$, the following are predictions:

|| Object distance $u$
|| Image distance $v$
|| <Transverse magnification> $m$
|| Signed image height $h_i$

| $30\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $15\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $-1/2$
| $-0.25\,\mathrm{cm}$

| $20\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $20\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $-1$
| $-0.50\,\mathrm{cm}$

| $15\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $30\,\mathrm{cm}$
| $-2$
| $-1.00\,\mathrm{cm}$

\Image[image-magnification-real-rays.png]
{title=A converging <thin lens> forms reduced and enlarged <real images>}
{description=Original ideal-model <ray tracing> for $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $h_o=0.50\,\mathrm{cm}$. Solid coloured <light rays> travel rightward and meet at the image tip on the screen. Both panels use the same coordinate scales, with the vertical scale enlarged for readability. Values are predictions, not measured data.}

To test both image position and <transverse magnification>, use an <optical bench> with an illuminated arrow or millimetre target, a converging <optical lens> of nominal <focal length> $10\,\mathrm{cm}$, a movable white screen and a ruler. Measure corresponding target and image intervals, rather than guessing the whole image size when it exceeds the screen. PASCO's Experiment 12 compares distance-based and size-based <magnification>; the following procedure uses several object distances, as in the Northern Virginia laboratory. https://tecnoedu.com/recursos/UNLCHidrologia/ManualesOriginalesPasco/Basic-Optics-System-Manual-OS-8515C.pdf[PASCO, Basic Optics System manual, Experiment 12, pp. 33–36]{ref}; https://www.pasco.com/resources/lab-experiments/484[PASCO, Object and Image Distances Thin Lens]{ref}.

* Align target centre, <optical lens> centre and screen along one <optical axis>, with target and screen perpendicular to it. Record bench positions of the actual target plane, <optical lens> plane and screen surface; distances between holder edges introduce an offset.
* Start at $u=30\,\mathrm{cm}$. Move the screen through the expected $v=15\,\mathrm{cm}$ region until the target's central detail is sharpest. Record $u$, measured $v$, the corresponding target and image intervals, and whether the arrow is inverted. Give the measured size ratio a negative sign if inverted.
* Repeat at $u=20\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $u=15\,\mathrm{cm}$. Compare the measured signed size ratio with $-v/u$, and calculate $f_{\mathrm{est}}=uv/(u+v)$ for each pair. The three ideal rows above are predictions to compare against, not entries to copy into the measurement record. https://sites.nvcc.edu/alphy/_files/Manuals/Physics%20242%20Lab%2011%20-%20Thin%20Lenses.pdf[Northern Virginia Community College, Physics 242 Lab 11, pp. 2–4: screen focusing, orientation and size measurements]{ref}.

Keep the <optical lens>, illumination and central target detail fixed between trials. Approach best focus from both sides and record the interval over which sharpness is difficult to distinguish; repeat focusing to estimate its reproducibility. A modest central <aperture stop> tests whether peripheral <light rays> change the result, but also reduces brightness. <Spherical aberration>, <chromatic aberration> and <diffraction> can broaden the optimum; a thick <optical lens> also makes the chosen reference plane imperfect. Treat nominal $f$ as an initial prediction, and report position and interval-reading limits instead of forcing exact agreement. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_27.html[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, §§27-5–27-7: principal planes and imaging limits]{ref}.

Moving the object toward the front <optical focus> from outside makes the <real image> larger and farther away. A failed screen search near $u=f$ can therefore mean that the <real image> is beyond the bench, rather than that a <virtual image> has formed. At exactly $u=f$, the outgoing bundle from each point is parallel and no finite screen position focuses it.

= Virtual image
{parent=Image formation}
{wiki}

A <virtual image> is the apparent origin of an outgoing diverging bundle: the backward extensions of its <light rays> intersect at the image point. Those extensions are geometric constructions, not backward-travelling <light>. A screen placed there cannot intercept the outgoing bundle to display that <virtual image>. An eye or a second <optical lens> receiving the actual outgoing <light rays> can nevertheless form a subsequent <real image>. “Virtual” describes this imaging stage, not whether the image can be seen or photographed. https://tecnoedu.com/recursos/UNLCHidrologia/ManualesOriginalesPasco/Basic-Optics-System-Manual-OS-8515C.pdf[PASCO, Basic Optics System manual, Experiment 14, pp. 41–43: viewing and reimaging virtual images]{ref}.

For a converging <thin lens> with $0<u<f$, the same equations give $v<0$ and $m=f/(f-u)>1$: the <virtual image> is upright and enlarged. For the illustrative choice $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$, $u=7.5\,\mathrm{cm}$, $h_o=0.30\,\mathrm{cm}$,
$$
v=-30\,\mathrm{cm},\qquad m=4,\qquad h_i=1.20\,\mathrm{cm}.
$$
The image appears farther from the <optical lens> than the object. A diverging <thin lens> with a real object instead has $v<0$ and $0<m<1$, so a <virtual image> need not be enlarged. A plane mirror provides another <virtual image>, located behind the reflecting surface by backward extensions of the reflected <light rays>. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/25-7-image-formation-by-mirrors[Urone and Hinrichs, College Physics 2e, §25.7: Plane-mirror images]{ref}.

\Image[image-magnification-virtual-rays.png]
{title=Backward ray extensions locate a <virtual image>; $u=f$ gives parallel outgoing <light rays>}
{description=Original ideal-model diagrams for a converging <thin lens> with $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $h_o=0.30\,\mathrm{cm}$. Above, solid <light rays> travel rightward while dashed backward extensions meet at $(-30,1.20)\,\mathrm{cm}$. Below, each object point at $u=f$ sends out a parallel bundle, so there is no finite image position. Vertical distances are enlarged; both panels share coordinate scales. These are predictions, not measurements.}

For a direct viewing test, bring the target to $u=7.5\,\mathrm{cm}$, search for a sharp screen image on the outgoing side, then remove the screen and look through the <optical lens> toward the target. Expect an upright enlarged view if the eye receives the bundle and can focus. Record the eye position: $5\,\mathrm{cm}$ beyond the <optical lens> gives a predicted apparent distance $35\,\mathrm{cm}$. This adapts the Northern Virginia laboratory's comparison. https://sites.nvcc.edu/alphy/_files/Manuals/Physics%20242%20Lab%2011%20-%20Thin%20Lenses.pdf[Northern Virginia Community College, Physics 242 Lab 11, pp. 4–5: Virtual images with a converging lens]{ref}.

For a position measurement, use <parallax localization of a virtual image>. Place a movable pencil tip or narrow comparison marker beside the apparent image, and view that marker directly beside or above the <optical lens>, while viewing the image through it. Move the head a little sideways and move the marker along the bench until relative lateral motion vanishes. Read its coordinate $x_m$; the estimate is $v=x_m<0$. Deliberately place the marker nearer and farther to check that relative motion reverses on opposite sides of the match. This follows Orhan Aytür's lens laboratory, which uses a pencil to locate a <virtual image>. https://www.ee.bilkent.edu.tr/~ee428/manual/html/node6.html[Orhan Aytür, Bilkent University, Experiment 2: Imaging with mirrors and lenses, §2]{ref}.

Keep target and <optical lens> fixed, use one eye and the central image detail, and ensure the marker itself is not viewed through the <optical lens>. A marker blocked by the target, or a head movement that loses the bundle through the <aperture>, invalidates that trial. Repeat the match from both directions and record its depth range. This measures apparent depth; comparing apparent sizes at different depths does not directly measure <transverse magnification>. Near $u=f$, apparent depth becomes very large and the match is poorly constrained. A failed screen search alone therefore needs the distance check and the viewing test.

= Parallax localization of a virtual image
{parent=Virtual image}

<Parallax localization of a virtual image> matches the apparent depth of a <virtual image> to a directly viewed physical marker. A sideways change of viewpoint changes their relative apparent positions if their depths differ. Adjust the marker until that relative motion vanishes, and use its coordinate to estimate image position. The marker must be viewed directly; viewing it through the same <optical lens> compares two images rather than an image and a known physical depth. https://www.ee.bilkent.edu.tr/~ee428/manual/html/node6.html[Orhan Aytür, Bilkent University, Experiment 2, §2: Pencil parallax test]{ref}.

For two nearly aligned points at distances $L_i,L_m$ from an eye displaced sideways by $\delta$, the <small-angle approximation> gives a change of relative viewing <angle> proportional to $\delta(1/L_i-1/L_m)$. It vanishes when $L_i=L_m$, and changes sign across that match. When both distances are large, the difference is small, explaining the poor depth precision near the <thin lens> condition $u=f$.

= Magnification
{parent=Image formation}
{wiki}

<Magnification> compares an image's scale with a specified reference. <Transverse magnification> compares signed lengths in object and image planes; <angular magnification> compares viewing <angles> at an observer. Both are <dimensionless quantities>, but they answer different questions. A projected <real image> twice as tall has $|m|=2$; an <optical telescope>'s advertised viewing enlargement normally concerns <angular magnification>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-8-microscopes-and-telescopes[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 3, §2.8: Angular magnification and telescopes]{ref}.

Always specify the sign and the reference arrangement. For signed <transverse magnification>, $m<0$ means inverted and $m>0$ means upright; $|m|>1$ means enlarged and $0<|m|<1$ means reduced. A larger <virtual image> can subtend a modest viewing <angle> if it is farther away. At $u=f$, an ideal converging <thin lens> has no finite image height and hence no finite <transverse magnification>, while <angular magnification> can remain finite. Enlargement also does not guarantee extra detail: <diffraction-limited resolution> and <optical aberrations> limit the information available to enlarge. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_27.html[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, §§27-6–27-7]{ref}.

= Transverse magnification
{parent=Magnification}
{title2=$m=h_i/h_o$}

<Transverse magnification> is the ratio of corresponding signed lengths perpendicular to the <optical axis>. For an ideal image of a small arrow of nonzero height,
$$
m=\frac{h_i}{h_o}.
$$
Use the same upward-positive height convention in both planes. Thus an upright $0.50\,\mathrm{cm}$ arrow imaged as $-0.25\,\mathrm{cm}$ has $m=-1/2$: inverted and half as tall. The sign describes orientation; <real image> versus <virtual image> describes ray intersections. They are separate properties, even when a particular <thin lens> setup correlates them.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[§H: Magnification]{ref}

For a <thin lens> with object coordinate $-u$ and image coordinate $v$, the undeviated centre <light ray> is the <straight line> through $(-u,h_o)$ and $(0,0)$, so its height at $x=v$ is $h_i=-h_ov/u$. This is the signed <similar triangles> argument. Combining it with the <thin lens> equation gives
$$
m=-\frac vu=-\frac{f}{u-f}\qquad (u\ne f).
$$
For $f>0$, $u>2f$ gives $-1<m<0$; $u=2f$ gives $m=-1$; $f<u<2f$ gives $m<-1$; and $0<u<f$ gives $m>1$. At $u=f$, the image is conventionally “at infinity”: the divergent expressions for $v$ and $m$ describe the limiting behaviour, not a finite image of infinite height. Different object points produce parallel bundles in different directions, which is why <angular magnification> remains meaningful.

The <real image> experiment tests $m$ in two independent ways: signed ratios of measured corresponding intervals, and $-v/u$ from measured longitudinal distances. As a further prediction, moving from $u=30\,\mathrm{cm}$ to $u=15\,\mathrm{cm}$ for $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$ swaps $u,v$, changing $m=-1/2$ to $m=-2$. The two image sizes differ by a factor of four, while both images remain inverted. This reversal follows from the symmetric <thin lens> equation and connects to the <Bessel lens displacement method>.

= Angular magnification
{parent=Magnification}
{title2=$M_\theta=\theta_i/\theta_o$}

<Angular magnification> is the ratio of the signed viewing <angle> with an <optical instrument> to the signed viewing <angle> in a stated reference arrangement. Measure both as apparent directions from the eye, positive toward the same side of the <optical axis>; then an inverted view has negative $M_\theta$. If only positive angular sizes are compared, the result is $|M_\theta|$ and carries no orientation information. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/26-5-telescopes[Urone and Hinrichs, College Physics 2e, §26.5: Telescope angular magnification and inversion]{ref}.
