{title=<Wavefronts> and normal <light rays> at an interface: an original <plane wave> model of <refraction>}

The diagram shows incident and transmitted branches separately, with the reflected branch omitted. Its stipulated <refractive indices> are $n_1=1$ and $n_2=3/2$, and its incident <angle> is $30^\circ$ to the interface normal. At a stationary interface, the <frequency> and tangential <wavevector> agree on the two sides: <Snell's law> gives $\sin\theta_2=1/3$, hence $\theta_2\approx19.4712^\circ$. The <wavefronts> turn while their normal spacing decreases from $\lambda_0$ to $2\lambda_0/3$, where $\lambda_0$ is the <vacuum> <wavelength>. More explicitly, with $x$ normal to the interface and $y$ tangential, take $S_1=\sqrt3x/2+y/2$ for $x<0$ and $S_2=\sqrt2x+y/2$ for $x>0$. These functions agree at $x=0$; the drawn <wavefronts> are $S_j=m\lambda_0$ at $t=0$ with $\phi_0=0$. This is an exact piecewise <plane wave> phase construction for the stipulated transparent media, not measured material data. <Electromagnetism lecture notes by Richard Fitzpatrick/Laws of Geometric Optics>{ref}

The normal-to-<wavefront> rule requires its assumptions. In a medium with <anisotropy>, the <wavevector> remains normal to a smooth <wavefront>, but the <group velocity> and <energy flux> need not follow it. <RP Photonics Encyclopedia by Rüdiger Paschotta/Wavefronts>{ref} Near a <wave caustic>, where multiple <light rays> meet, one smooth <phase function> may no longer describe the field; <diffraction> and the full <wave amplitude> matter. A <wavefront> is also not generally a surface of equal pulse arrival time. In a medium with <optical dispersion>, a monochromatic <wave phase> moves with the <phase velocity>, while a sufficiently narrow <wave packet> follows the <group velocity>. Even identical <optical path lengths> at one <frequency> can give different <group delays>. The usual equal-travel-time picture needs a common launch reference and a nondispersive ray model; an arbitrary broadband disturbance has no single monochromatic <wavefront>.<The Feynman Lectures on Physics, Volume I by Richard Feynman and others>{ref}

= Optical path length
{parent=Wavefront}
{wiki}
{title2=$\int n\,ds$}

= OPL
{c}
{synonym}

An <optical path length> measures how much propagation <wave phase> a path accumulates, expressed as the distance that would accumulate the same <wave phase> in <vacuum>. A centimetre in a uniform medium with <refractive index> $3/2$ has <optical path length> $1.5\,\mathrm{cm}$, although its geometric length remains $1\,\mathrm{cm}$. This makes <optical path length> useful whenever two contributions must arrive with matching <wave phases>, from an <optical lens> to a <Michelson interferometer>. <RP Photonics Encyclopedia by Rüdiger Paschotta/Optical Path Length>{ref}

For a specified path $\gamma$ at fixed <angular frequency> $\omega$, the <optical path length> is the <line integral>
$$
\operatorname{OPL}_\gamma(\omega)=\int_\gamma n(\mathbf r,\omega)\,ds,\qquad
ds=|d\mathbf r|,\qquad
\operatorname{OPL}=\sum_j n_jL_j\ \text{for uniform segments}.
$$
Here $s$ is geometric <arc length>, and $n$ is the phase <refractive index>, evaluated at the same <frequency> everywhere. The propagation interpretation assumes a stationary linear medium with <isotropy>, a real positive scalar <refractive index> and negligible loss, on a valid <geometrical optics> branch. The path can curve; at sharp interfaces apply the appropriate matching conditions to each segment. A prescribed curve has a mathematical <line integral>, but it predicts a propagation <wave phase> only when it represents the physical branch. <MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>{ref} <Optical fibers> can require a mode <wavenumber> rather than the bulk <refractive index> along a guessed <light ray>. <RP Photonics Encyclopedia by Rüdiger Paschotta/Optical Path Length>{ref}

Fix the <vacuum> <wavelength> $\lambda_0=c/f=2\pi c/\omega$, with <frequency> $f=\omega/(2\pi)$ and <vacuum> <wavenumber> $k_0=2\pi/\lambda_0$. In a locally uniform medium the <wavelength> is $\lambda=\lambda_0/n$, while the <frequency> stays unchanged at a stationary interface. With the convention $\Phi=k_0S-\omega t+\phi_0$, propagation from a specified starting <wave phase> contributes
$$
\phi_{\mathrm{prop}}=k_0\operatorname{OPL},\qquad
\delta=\delta_{\mathrm{ref}}+k_0\bigl(\operatorname{OPL}_2-\operatorname{OPL}_1\bigr)\pmod{2\pi}.
$$
The relative reference $\delta_{\mathrm{ref}}$ includes launch <wave phases> and any extra <wave phase> from mirrors, <beam splitters> or coatings. Thus $k_0\operatorname{OPL}$ is a propagation contribution, not an absolute detector <wave phase> independent of the apparatus. If the reference contributions stay fixed, a change in <optical path difference> $h$ changes the <phase difference> by $2\pi h/\lambda_0$. Using the material <wavelength> again in this formula would count the <refractive index> twice. <Electromagnetism lecture notes by Richard Fitzpatrick/Laws of Geometric Optics>{ref}

\Image[optical-path-michelson-phase-readout.png]
{id=optical-path-michelson-phase-readout}
{title=<Optical path difference> in a <Michelson interferometer>: apparatus schematic and a calculated <phase difference> readout}

A motivating measurement turns invisible optical oscillations into a slowly changing electrical signal. MIT's teaching experiment uses a <laser>, a <beam splitter>, two returning mirrors, an amplified <photodiode>, an oscilloscope and a calibrated mirror actuator driven by a function generator. The returning beams overlap on the <photodiode>; one channel records detector voltage and another the actuator drive. The measured observable is the changing <light intensity>, including its maxima and minima, rather than direct timing of an optical cycle. A calibrated displacement per drive voltage and the spacing of the extrema relate geometric motion to the <phase difference>. The guide infers the <laser> <wavelength> from known actuator calibration; conversely, with independently known <vacuum> <wavelength> and ambient <refractive index>, the same relation calibrates mirror motion. These two unknowns cannot both be inferred from fringe spacing alone. <MIT 8.13–14 Experimental Physics I & II, Junior Lab, Fall 2016–Spring 2017 by MIT Department of Physics/Optical Interferometry>{ref}

For a proposed change-of-medium measurement, keep the mirrors fixed and put a transparent cell of internal length $L$ in one arm. Compare its contents at <refractive indices> $n_a$ and $n_b$, leaving its windows in place. At normal incidence the two traversals give
$$
\Delta\operatorname{OPL}=2L(n_b-n_a),\qquad
\Delta\delta=\frac{4\pi L(n_b-n_a)}{\lambda_0},\qquad
N=\frac{\Delta\delta}{2\pi}=\frac{2L(n_b-n_a)}{\lambda_0}.
$$
Here $N$ is the signed number of whole <interference fringe> cycles plus any resolved fractional cycle, counted continuously. A mirror displacement $x$ instead gives $\Delta\operatorname{OPL}=2n_{\mathrm{amb}}x$; distinguish this double-pass factor from a single pass in a <Mach-Zehnder interferometer>. OpenStax's gas-cell worked example provides the same double-pass reasoning; it is a teaching calculation rather than a published measurement dataset.<University Physics Volume 3 by Samuel J. Ling and others/3.5 The Michelson Interferometer>[Example 3.6: mirror displacement and a gas cell]{ref}

An exact toy calculation makes the sensitivity visible. Stipulate $\lambda_0=600\,\mathrm{nm}$, $L=10\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $n_b-n_a=3/100000=0.00003$. Then $\Delta\operatorname{OPL}=600\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $\Delta\delta=2\pi$: a full <interference fringe> cycle, despite unchanged geometric lengths. For a continuous change $n_b-n_a=q(3/100000)$, let $0\leq q\leq2$ and choose the initial detector <phase difference> to be zero. Equal coherent detected arm contributions of <light intensity> $I_0$ predict
$$
\frac{I(q)}{4I_0}=\frac{1+\cos(2\pi q)}{2}=\cos^2(\pi q).
$$
The plot is this stipulated model: $q=1/2$ gives a minimum, $q=1$ restores a maximum, and the reference level is chosen by adjustment of the apparatus. It contains no measured values or assigned properties of a real gas. The equivalent mirror travel in <vacuum> is $300\,\mathrm{nm}$ per cycle. A single endpoint <light intensity> cannot distinguish $q=0$ from $q=1$; the continuous fringe count carries the missing information.

For an interpretable cell measurement, establish the initial <phase difference> with a small calibrated mirror scan, then track slow changes through successive <interference fringes>. Measure the dark signal and each arm separately, keep <photodiode> gain within its linear range, align both beams on the same region and match their <linear polarizations>. Monitor <interferometric visibility> and keep arm delays within the useful <optical coherence time>. Record cell length, contents, <pressure> and <temperature>; return to the starting condition to check drift. Changes in window deformation, cell length, external air or mirror position also change <optical path length>, so a reference cell or an independently monitored mechanical path is needed to isolate the contents' <refractive index>. These controls are deductions from the model, not a claim that the illustrated apparatus was operated. Loss of fringe count leaves a whole-cycle ambiguity; at an intensity extremum the first <derivative> with respect to <phase difference> vanishes, so a scan around <phase quadrature> is more sensitive to small changes. Broadband illumination, unstable <optical coherence>, vibration and strong loss can invalidate a simple one-<frequency> inference.

= Optical path difference
{parent=Optical path length}
{title2=$h=\operatorname{OPL}_2-\operatorname{OPL}_1$}

= OPD
{c}
{synonym}

An <optical path difference> subtracts two <optical path lengths> at the same <frequency> and with specified endpoints and sign. Its <phase difference> contribution is $k_0h$, in addition to any apparatus reference <wave phase>. For <wavefront error>, the comparison is usually between an actual <wavefront> and an ideal reference <wavefront>.

= Group refractive index
{parent=Optical path length}
{title2=$n_g=n+\omega\,\partial_\omega n$}

= Group index
{synonym}

The <group refractive index> is $n_g=c/v_g$ for a transparent <isotropic> propagation branch with forward <group velocity> $v_g=d\omega/dk>0$. Differentiating the local <wavenumber> $k=n(\omega)\omega/c$ gives
$$
n_g=n+\omega\frac{\partial n}{\partial\omega}=n-\lambda_0\frac{\partial n}{\partial\lambda_0},\qquad
\tau_g=\frac{\partial\phi_{\mathrm{prop}}}{\partial\omega}=\frac1c\int_\gamma n_g(\mathbf r,\omega)\,ds.
$$
The last formula gives the propagation <group delay> along a path whose geometry is fixed as <frequency> varies; additional apparatus <wave phases> require their own <frequency> derivatives. For a narrow <wave packet> with little loss and negligible reshaping, it approximates the transit of its <wave envelope>. In the nondispersive case $\partial_\omega n=0$, it reduces to $\operatorname{OPL}/c$. An <optical path length> at one <frequency> contains no information about $\partial_\omega n$, so in a medium with <optical dispersion> it cannot by itself determine the <group delay>. <RP Photonics Encyclopedia by Rüdiger Paschotta/Group Index>{ref}

For an original local model near $\omega_*$, choose two equal geometric paths of length $L$, with $n_A(\omega)=3/2$ and $n_B(\omega)=3/2+(\omega-\omega_*)/(10\omega_*)$. At $\omega_*$ both <optical path lengths> equal $3L/2$, and matched launch and interface references give equal carrier <wave phases>. But $n_{g,A}=3/2$ and $n_{g,B}=8/5$, so their <group delays> differ by $L/(10c)$. This local stipulated model is a counterexample to identifying equal <wave phase> with equal pulse arrival; it asserts no material response outside the narrow band considered. Strong loss or pulse reshaping requires a fuller propagation model, and <group delay> need not time the earliest causal response.

= Group delay
{parent=Group refractive index}
{title2=$\tau_g=\partial\phi/\partial\omega$}

The <group delay> is the <frequency> derivative of an unwrapped transfer <wave phase>, using the $e^{-i\omega t}$ convention. It predicts the leading delay of a narrow <wave envelope> when the transfer <wave amplitude> and the delay vary little across its band; it is not generally a signal-front arrival time.

= Wavefront error
{parent=Wavefront}
{title2=$h$}

A wavefront error is the difference in <optical path length> from a reference wavefront, often an ideal plane or sphere. Its <root mean square> should specify the illuminated pupil and which modes, such as constant phase or image displacement, have been removed.

= Modal amplitude norm versus wavefront RMS
{parent=Wavefront error}

For a real <wavefront error> $h=\sum_k a_k s_k$, its squared spatial <root mean square> is $\langle h^2\rangle=\sum_{k,l}a_ka_l\langle s_ks_l\rangle$. Orthogonal unit-peak <sine> modes have mean square $1/2$, so $h_{\rm rms}^2=\frac12\sum_k a_k^2$, whereas the coefficient norm is $\sqrt{\sum_k a_k^2}$. On a finite <optical pupil> <orthogonality> needs checking; independent uniform random <wave phases> give the same $1/2$ diagonal after <wave phase> averaging. This normalization matters when a <speckle contrast of a sinusoidal wavefront error> is specified by peak <wave amplitude>.

= Interference
{disambiguate=wave propagation}
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}

= Wave interference
{synonym}

= Interference
{synonym}

<Wave interference> is the reinforcement or cancellation that occurs when overlapping <wave amplitudes> add by the <superposition principle>. For two <monochromatic waves> of the same <frequency>, mutual <optical coherence> means that their relative <wave phase> is stable during the observation. Equal <wave phases> give <constructive interference>; a half-cycle <phase difference> gives the <destructive interference intensity> minimum. <Wave amplitudes> add before their squares determine <wave intensity>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/3-1-youngs-double-slit-interference[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 3, §3.1: coherent waves and interference]{ref}.

Consider two overlapping beams in a homogeneous medium, with the same <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> and individual local time-averaged <light intensities> $I_1,I_2$. Their <phase difference> $\delta$ is measured in <radians>. In the scalar detection convention, the combined local time-averaged <wave intensity> is
$$
I=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\cos\delta.
$$
The last term records <wave interference>; adding $I_1+I_2$ alone misses it. The formula describes <irradiance> when both fields propagate along the same direction, or when the same detector geometry factor is included in all three intensities. For crossing beams it describes the squared <electric field> response, proportional to <scalar wave intensity>; a specified component of the <Poynting vector> also requires the propagation geometry. The ideal crossing <plane waves> below make that distinction explicit.

For equal individual <light intensities> $I_1=I_2=I_0>0$, the <cosine> half-angle identity gives
$$
I=2I_0(1+\cos\delta)=4I_0\cos^2(\delta/2).
$$
The <constructive interference intensity> is $4I_0$ at $\delta=0$ modulo $2\pi$, and the <destructive interference intensity> is zero at $\delta=\pi$ modulo $2\pi$. At <phase quadrature>, $\delta=\pi/2$, it is $2I_0$. With $I_1=1$ and $I_2=0.25$ in the same arbitrary <wave intensity> unit, the maximum is $2.25$, the minimum is $0.25$, and the <phase quadrature> value is $1.25$: unequal <wave amplitudes> cannot cancel completely.

\Image[interference-intensity-versus-phase.png]
{title=Two-beam <wave intensity> versus <phase difference>: equal beams reach 4 and 0; unequal beams reach 2.25 and 0.25}

Both curves use the same <wave intensity> unit and a horizontal <angle> in <radians>. The dashed levels are the averages over a full <phase difference> cycle. Equal beams have <interferometric visibility> one; the unequal example has <interferometric visibility> $0.8$.

A spatially varying <phase difference> turns the same law into <interference fringes>. Take two ideal <plane electromagnetic waves> with common <wavelength> $\lambda$, equal individual <light intensities> $I_0$, and propagation directions at <angles> $+\theta$ and $-\theta$ from the $z$ axis in the $xz$ plane, with $0<\theta<\pi/2$. Both have <linear polarization> along $y$, perpendicular to both propagation directions. Choose equal initial <wave phases>; their <wavevectors> and <phase difference> are
$$
\mathbf k_\pm=k(\pm\sin\theta,0,\cos\theta),\qquad k=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda},\qquad
\phi_\pm=\pm kx\sin\theta+kz\cos\theta-\omega t,\qquad
\delta=\phi_+-\phi_-=2kx\sin\theta.
$$
Here $k$ is the <wavenumber> and $\omega$ the <angular frequency>. The common $z$ and time terms cancel, so the <interference fringes> stay at fixed $x$ for every $z$. Their <interference fringe spacing> is
$$
\Lambda=\frac{2\pi}{2k\sin\theta}=\frac{\lambda}{2\sin\theta},\qquad
\frac{I(x,z)}{I_0}=4\cos^2(kx\sin\theta).
$$
Set the length unit to $\lambda$, so that $\lambda=1$, and take $\theta=30^\circ=\pi/6$: then $\Lambda=1$. The <constructive interference intensity> bands lie at integer $x/\lambda$; the <destructive interference intensity> bands lie at half-integer $x/\lambda$.

\Image[two-beam-interference-fringes.png]
{title=Ideal crossing <plane waves> at $\pm30^\circ$: stationary <interference fringes> with spacing one <wavelength>}

The grid covers $-3\leq x/\lambda\leq3$ and $0\leq z/\lambda\leq4$; both axes are dimensionless lengths in <wavelength> units. The colour scale is $I/I_0$ from zero to four, using the same single-beam normalization as the equal-beam curve above. This is an ideal <plane wave> superposition, with no slits or point sources and no near-field approximation. For these $y$-polarized <plane electromagnetic waves> in <vacuum>, the longitudinal time-averaged <Poynting vector> obeys $\langle S_z\rangle=I\cos\theta$; the plotted scalar $I$ therefore differs from the <irradiance> through a plane normal to $z$ by that common factor. This relation follows by adding the <magnetic fields> as well as the <electric fields>.

The bright peaks do not create <energy>. Averaging over one <interference fringe spacing>, or equivalently over one full <phase difference> cycle, gives
$$
\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}I(\delta)\,d\delta=I_1+I_2,
$$
because the <integral> of the <cosine> is zero, with <sine> as its <antiderivative>. Equal beams thus average to $2I_0$, despite bright bands at $4I_0$. For the crossing-wave geometry, the average longitudinal <energy flux> is correspondingly $2I_0\cos\theta$. Bright and dark regions redistribute the incoming <energy>; <conservation of energy> concerns the full <energy flux>, rather than the height of one peak.

<Wave interference> is useful because <phase differences> convert small changes in <optical path length> into measurable changes of <wave intensity>, as in a <Mach-Zehnder interferometer>. Its beauty is that the same <superposition principle> produces both complete cancellation and fourfold brightening; adding more coherent contributions also produces the narrow peaks of a <diffraction grating>. Feynman's treatment connects this many-source pattern directly to addition of <complex amplitudes>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_30.html#Ch30-S1[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics, I §30–1: the resultant amplitude of identical oscillators]{ref}.

A short proof outline uses local real fields $E_j(t)=A_j\cos(\omega t+\phi_j)$ with parallel <linear polarizations>. Write $I=C\langle(E_1+E_2)^2\rangle_t$ and $I_j=CA_j^2/2$ for the same positive conversion factor $C$. Averaging over many <wave periods> while the <phase difference> stays fixed uses $\langle\cos^2(\omega t+\phi_j)\rangle_t=1/2$ and $\langle\cos(\omega t+\phi_1)\cos(\omega t+\phi_2)\rangle_t=\cos\delta/2$, giving the stated law. Equivalently, add the <complex amplitudes> and take the squared <complex modulus>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_29.html#Ch29-S5[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics, I §29–5: the mathematics of interference]{ref}.

For different fixed <linear polarizations> in a common transverse plane, the cross term acquires the <dot product> of their real <unit vectors>, equal to $\cos\alpha$ for relative <angle> $\alpha$. Orthogonal <linear polarizations> remove the cross term in polarization-insensitive scalar detection; different orientations generally only reduce it. A common <polarizer> can select overlapping components and recover <wave interference>. Likewise, rapid random variation of the <phase difference> removes the observed cross term only when the detector's time average has $\langle e^{i\delta(t)}\rangle_t=0$. Slowly varying <wave phases> can instead move resolved <interference fringes>, and biased or incompletely sampled <wave phases> can leave residual contrast. Finite <optical coherence> therefore depends on the observation time as well as the fields.

= Constructive interference intensity
{parent=Interference (wave propagation)}
{title2=$I_{\max}=(\sqrt{I_1}+\sqrt{I_2})^2$}

For mutually coherent <monochromatic waves> with the same <polarization of waves> in the scalar convention of <wave interference>, <constructive interference> reaches its maximum at <phase difference> $\delta=2\pi m$, with <integer> $m$. The <wave amplitudes> add, giving $I_{\max}=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}$. Equal individual <wave intensities> $I_0$ give $4I_0$; individual <wave intensities> $1$ and $0.25$ give $2.25$ in their common unit. This maximum assumes the <optical coherence> and detection conditions stated in <wave interference>.

= Destructive interference intensity
{parent=Interference (wave propagation)}
{title2=$I_{\min}=(\sqrt{I_1}-\sqrt{I_2})^2$}

For the same mutually coherent, equally polarized <monochromatic waves>, the <wave interference> minimum occurs at <phase difference> $\delta=(2m+1)\pi$, with <integer> $m$. The <wave amplitudes> subtract, giving $I_{\min}=I_1+I_2-2\sqrt{I_1I_2}$. Complete cancellation requires equal individual <wave intensities>; $1$ and $0.25$ leave $0.25$ in their common unit. A dark band is a local minimum, and the average across an <interference fringe spacing> still obeys <conservation of energy>.

= Interference fringe
{parent=Interference (wave propagation)}

An <interference fringe> is a spatial band of enhanced or reduced <wave intensity> caused by a spatially varying <phase difference>. Uniform crossing <plane waves> produce straight parallel <interference fringes>; curved <wavefronts> can give curved bands. The <interference fringes> mark the relative <wave phase>, rather than the individual instantaneous <wavefronts>.

= Interference fringe spacing
{parent=Interference fringe}
{title2=$\Lambda$}

The distance between adjacent bright <interference fringes>, or between adjacent dark <interference fringes>, measured along a specified direction. For the symmetric crossing <plane waves> in <wave interference>, measured along $x$, $\Lambda=\lambda/(2\sin\theta)$. Bright-to-dark separation is $\Lambda/2$. As $\theta$ tends to zero the spacing diverges; at $\theta=0$ the <phase difference> is spatially constant and there are no periodic bands.

= Which-path information
{parent=Interference (wave propagation)}

If the two arms have internal marker states $|a\rangle,|b\rangle$, an interference cross term is multiplied by $\langle a|b\rangle$. Orthogonal markers remove interference from polarization-insensitive detection even if nobody reads the markers. Coherently making the two markers identical erases that distinguishing information and restores the cross term.

// Target: quantum-measurement.bigb

= Interferometric visibility
{parent=Interference (wave propagation)}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathcal V=(I_{\max}-I_{\min})/(I_{\max}+I_{\min})$}

= Interference visibility
{synonym}

<Interferometric visibility> measures the contrast of a scanned <interference fringe>. For the mutually coherent, equally polarized two-beam model in <wave interference>, with fixed individual <wave intensities> and $I_1+I_2>0$, the <constructive interference intensity> and <destructive interference intensity> give
$$
\mathcal V=\frac{I_{\max}-I_{\min}}{I_{\max}+I_{\min}}=\frac{2\sqrt{I_1I_2}}{I_1+I_2}.
$$
Equal nonzero <wave intensities> give $\mathcal V=1$; $I_1=1,I_2=0.25$ give $\mathcal V=0.8$. Unequal <wave intensities> can therefore reduce <interferometric visibility> even with perfect mutual <optical coherence>. Random <phase differences> or distinguishable <wave polarizations> can reduce it further; zero contrast alone does not identify the cause. Both intensities zero leave the ratio undefined.

For normalized paths with <complex amplitudes> $\alpha,\beta$ and identical internal states, the output <probability> is $1/2+\operatorname{Re}(\alpha^*\beta e^{i\theta})$, so $\mathcal V=2|\alpha\beta|$. A <photon polarization> fringe alone determines transverse <quantum coherence> but not the sign of the population difference.

// Target: optics.bigb

= Fraunhofer diffraction
{c}
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}

<Fraunhofer diffraction> is the far-field approximation for <diffraction>: contributions from an <aperture> reach a selected observation direction with approximately linear path-dependent <wave phases>. Adding their <complex amplitudes> gives a <Fourier transform> of the field across the <aperture>. It also describes the appropriate rear <focal plane> of an ideal <optical lens> under coherent plane-wave illumination. The <diffraction grating> examples use this regime. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waves/node98.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Wave Optics, §10.9: Two-dimensional Fourier optics]{ref}; https://web.mit.edu/2.710/Fall06/2.710-wk10-a-sl.pdf[MIT 2.71/2.710 Optics, The ideal thin lens as a Fourier transform engine, slides 12–14: back focal plane diffraction]{ref}.

For a slit of width $a$, <wavelength> $\lambda$ and screen distance $L$, sufficient conditions near the forward direction are
$$
L\gg a,\qquad L\gg\frac{a^2}{\lambda},\qquad \frac{a^2}{\lambda L}\ll1.
$$
The last two statements are equivalent: the important comparison is with $a^2/\lambda$, rather than just with the slit width. For a finite rectangular <aperture>, check the corresponding condition for both transverse dimensions. The <single-slit diffraction> calculation treats the transverse cut associated with width $a$. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waves/node99.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, Wave Optics, §10.10, equations 10.93–10.96: far-field conditions]{ref}.

A short explanation is that the distance from slit coordinate $x$ to an observation point at distance $R$ and <angle> $\theta$ is
$$
r(x)=\sqrt{R^2-2Rx\sin\theta+x^2}=R-x\sin\theta+\frac{x^2\cos^2\theta}{2R}+O(a^3/R^2).
$$
The linear term supplies the <phase difference>; the quadratic term supplies a <wave phase> error of order $a^2/(\lambda R)$, since the <wavenumber> is $2\pi/\lambda$. Near the forward direction $R\approx L$, so the stated condition makes this error small. This is why a distant screen permits the simple <single-slit diffraction> formula. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waves/node98.html[Fitzpatrick, §10.9, equations 10.70–10.74: path expansion and aperture superposition]{ref}.

= Single-slit diffraction
{parent=Fraunhofer diffraction}
{title2=$I(\theta)/I(0)=(\sin u/u)^2,\quad u=\pi a\sin\theta/\lambda$}

<Single-slit diffraction> is the spreading of <light> transmitted by one slit. Consider an ideal rectangular slit of width $a$, illuminated normally by a uniform <plane wave> with a single <wavelength> $\lambda$ in the surrounding medium. This <monochromatic wave> has uniform <wave amplitude> and equal incident <wave phases> across the slit, providing the required <optical coherence>. In the scalar <Fraunhofer diffraction> model, the normalized angular <wave intensity> is
$$
\frac{I(\theta)}{I_0}=\left(\frac{\sin u}{u}\right)^2,\qquad u=\frac{\pi a\sin\theta}{\lambda},\qquad I_0=I(0),\qquad \lim_{u\to0}\frac{\sin u}{u}=1.
$$
Here $\theta$ is measured from the forward normal in the plane across the slit width. The <sinc function> uses the unnormalized convention $\operatorname{sinc}u=\sin u/u$. The <limit> makes the central value one. This ideal envelope neglects additional direction-dependent propagation and detector factors; near the forward direction it describes the normalized screen <irradiance>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/4-2-intensity-in-single-slit-diffraction[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 3, §4.2: single-slit intensity]{ref}.

The noncentral zeros satisfy
$$
a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\qquad m\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\},\qquad \left|\frac{m\lambda}{a}\right|\leq1.
$$
The restriction follows from the range of the <sine> of a real <angle>. The first minima have $\sin\theta=\pm\lambda/a$ when $\lambda/a\leq1$. If $a<\lambda$, this model has no noncentral zero in the forward hemisphere; equality puts the first zeros at grazing directions, outside the <small-angle approximation> and off any finite perpendicular screen. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/4-1-single-slit-diffraction[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, University Physics Volume 3, §4.1: positions of diffraction minima]{ref}.

For $0<\lambda/a<1$, define the central width as the separation between the two first minima. Its angular width and its width on a perpendicular screen are
$$
\Delta\theta=2\arcsin(\lambda/a)\approx\frac{2\lambda}{a},\qquad y=L\tan\theta,\qquad W=2L\tan\!\bigl(\arcsin(\lambda/a)\bigr)\approx\frac{2L\lambda}{a}.
$$
The angular approximation uses <radians> and requires $\lambda/a\ll1$; the screen approximation uses $y\approx L\theta$. The <tangent> relation is exact straight-line geometry, even though the underlying <Fraunhofer diffraction> field is approximate. This width is distinct from the <full width at half maximum>.

For $\lambda=500\,\mathrm{nm}$, $a=0.1\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $L=1\,\mathrm m$, the positive first minimum is
$$
\theta_1=\arcsin(0.005)\approx0.286480^\circ,\qquad y_1=\frac{L(0.005)}{\sqrt{1-0.005^2}}\approx5.00006\,\mathrm{mm},\qquad W\approx10.0001\,\mathrm{mm}.
$$
The dimensionless far-field parameter is $a^2/(\lambda L)=0.02$. Doubling the slit width to $a=0.2\,\mathrm{mm}$ gives $W\approx5.00002\,\mathrm{mm}$ and parameter $0.08$; both curves below use the <Fraunhofer diffraction> approximation. Choosing $L=2\,\mathrm m$ halves those parameters and doubles both screen widths.

\Image[single-slit-diffraction-width.png]
{title=Normalized <single-slit diffraction> on a screen: a narrower slit produces a wider central maximum}

Each curve is normalized to its own $I_0$; the arrows measure between first minima. The calculation uses $\theta=\arctan(y/L)$ before evaluating the <sinc function>, rather than replacing $\sin\theta$ by $y/L$. The lower diagram shows the exact screen geometry with the vertical scale enlarged. Normalization removes the change in peak brightness when the slit width changes.

The <wave interference> explanation is especially simple: divide the slit into equal narrow strips and add their <complex amplitudes>, rather than their <wave intensities>. With <wavenumber> $k=2\pi/\lambda$, the continuous slit gives
$$
\frac{A(\theta)}{A(0)}=\frac1a\int_{-a/2}^{a/2}e^{ikx\sin\theta}\,dx=\frac{e^{iu}-e^{-iu}}{2iu}=\frac{\sin u}{u}.
$$
Integrating the <complex exponential> gives the endpoint difference; its continuous value at zero is one. Taking the squared <complex modulus> gives the stated <wave intensity>. This is the <Fourier transform of an interval indicator> expressed in angular coordinates. Feynman's continuous-source argument obtains the same pattern by letting the number of sources tend to infinity. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_30.html#Ch30-S2[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, I §30–2: continuous distributions of oscillators]{ref}.

At the positive first minimum, the <wave phases> span exactly $2\pi$ across the slit. Every strip in one half has a partner $a/2$ away, with equal <wave amplitude> and <phase difference> $\pi$, so the pairs cancel. At $\theta=0$, all the <wave phases> align instead. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/4-1-single-slit-diffraction[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, §4.1: cancellation between the slit halves]{ref}.

\Image[single-slit-aperture-phase-cancellation.png]
{title=<Complex amplitudes> across a slit: aligned contributions at broadside and cancellation at the first minimum}

The drawing uses $N=8$ equally weighted midpoint samples $x_j/a=-1/2+(j+1/2)/8$, with $j=0,\ldots,7$. At the first minimum their <complex amplitudes> are a common rotation of the eight <roots of unity>, so their head-to-tail polygon closes exactly. This finite sum illustrates cancellation; the continuous <integral>, rather than eight point sources, defines the slit pattern at general <angles>. The circle shows the continuous range of <wave phases>; coloured arrows show the eight samples.

A narrower <aperture> requires a larger <angle> to accumulate the cancelling <phase difference>, so narrowing the slit widens the beam. This is a useful limit on <geometrical optics>: confining <light> more tightly at an opening increases its subsequent angular spread. At fixed incident <irradiance> and slit height, reducing $a$ also reduces transmitted <power>, exposing a practical balance between <diffraction-limited resolution> and light throughput. In a <diffraction grating>, this <single-slit diffraction> envelope modulates the <wave interference> between different slits.

= Optical coherence
{parent=Physical optics}

<Optical coherence> describes correlations between the <wave amplitudes> and <wave phases> of <light> sampled at different places or times. Its most direct classical test is <wave interference>: combine two samples of a field and see how much the detected <light intensity> changes when their relative <wave phase> is scanned. <First-order optical coherence> makes this statement quantitative; <temporal optical coherence> compares delayed samples, while <spatial optical coherence> compares different positions. These properties are relative to the samples and averaging procedure, rather than a single yes-or-no label for a source. https://www.rp-photonics.com/coherence.html[Rüdiger Paschotta, RP Photonics Encyclopedia, Coherence: field correlations and the two kinds of coherence]{ref}.

A useful surprise is that two copies of a fluctuating source can interfere strongly when they have matched delays: the copies share the fluctuations. Conversely, two individually steady-looking beams need not have correlated <wave phases>. The ideal <diffraction grating> calculation requires <spatial optical coherence> across its illuminated elements; unequal-arm measurements with a <Michelson interferometer> test <temporal optical coherence>.

= First-order optical coherence
{parent=Optical coherence}
{title2=$g^{(1)}_{12}(\tau)$}

Consider one selected <polarization of waves>, represented by complex fields $E_1(t)$ and $E_2(t)$ at two sampling positions. Suppose their second moments are finite and stationary: $J_j=\langle|E_j(t)|^2\rangle$ and the following uncentered <wave-field correlation> do not depend on the absolute time $t$. Define
$$
\Gamma_{12}(\tau)=\langle E_1^*(t)E_2(t+\tau)\rangle,
\qquad g^{(1)}_{12}(\tau)=\frac{\Gamma_{12}(\tau)}{\sqrt{J_1J_2}},
\qquad J_1,J_2>0.
$$
The star denotes the <complex conjugate>; brackets denote an <ensemble average>. A long detector average estimates this quantity only if it adequately samples the fluctuations. The superscript $1$ identifies a field correlation: it does not mean the <autocorrelation> of the measured <light intensity>. The one-position version is $g^{(1)}(\tau)=\langle E^*(t)E(t+\tau)\rangle/\langle|E|^2\rangle$, so $g^{(1)}(0)=1$ for every nonzero field covered by this definition; this alone says nothing about its correlation at nonzero delay. https://www.rp-photonics.com/coherence_time.html[Rüdiger Paschotta, RP Photonics Encyclopedia, Coherence Time: stationary complex field correlation]{ref}.

The <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> gives $0\leq|g^{(1)}_{12}(\tau)|\leq1$. At a specified delay, magnitude one means $E_2(t+\tau)=aE_1(t)$ in mean square for a fixed nonzero complex $a$: the samples have a reproducible relation in both <wave amplitude> and <wave phase>. Magnitude zero means the averaged field cross term vanishes. It need not mean statistical <independence>. A deterministic <phase difference> changes the argument of $g^{(1)}$, not its <complex modulus>; a distorted but fixed <wavefront> can therefore remain fully coherent. https://www.rp-photonics.com/coherence.html[Paschotta, Coherence: static distortions versus fluctuating distortions]{ref}.

To see the observable, recombine the samples in the same detected spatial mode with matched <wave polarizations>. With a common positive conversion factor $C$, let $I_j=CJ_j$ be the single-arm <light intensities> reaching that detector. Adding $E_1(t)+e^{i\phi}E_2(t+\tau)$ gives
$$
I(\phi,\tau)=I_1+I_2+2\sqrt{I_1I_2}\,\operatorname{Re}\!\left[e^{i\phi}g^{(1)}_{12}(\tau)\right],
\qquad \mathcal V(\tau)=\frac{2\sqrt{I_1I_2}}{I_1+I_2}|g^{(1)}_{12}(\tau)|.
$$
This follows simply by expanding the squared <complex modulus> and averaging, then taking the maximum and minimum as $\phi$ spans a full cycle. The extra <wave phase> scan must leave $I_1,I_2$ and the magnitude of the correlation effectively unchanged. Thus <interferometric visibility> equals $|g^{(1)}|$ only for equal nonzero <light intensities> and ideal mode matching. For $I_2/I_1=1/4$, perfectly coherent beams have <interferometric visibility> $0.8$; a measured value $0.4$ would imply $|g^{(1)}|=0.5$ under these assumptions. Orthogonal <linear polarizations>, background light, unresolved <interference fringes> or vibrations can reduce measured contrast further. One absent arm gives zero contrast and makes the normalized two-sample correlation undefined.

= Temporal optical coherence
{parent=First-order optical coherence}
{title2=$g^{(1)}(\tau)$}

= Temporal coherence
{synonym}

<Temporal optical coherence> compares a field at one position with a delayed copy. In a <Michelson interferometer>, unequal round-trip lengths supply this delay. Even a source with rapidly changing <wave phase> can give high <interferometric visibility> close to zero delay, because both arms then deliver nearly the same source history. A source narrowed in <optical spectrum> generally retains correlation over larger delays; a single width does not describe sources with several separated lines. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-71-optics-spring-2009/bebe78c6a2ae2603a0d1a3ea85e0b4ca_MIT2_71S09_lec22.pdf[MIT 2.71/2.710, lecture 22, slides 2–5: temporal and spatial coherence]{ref}.

For a stationary scalar field, choose the carrier convention $e^{-i2\pi\nu t}$, with <frequency> $\nu$ in cycles per second. Let $S(\nu)\geq0$ be its <spectral density> per unit <frequency>, with $0<\int S(\nu)\,d\nu<\infty$. Its normalized field correlation is the <Fourier transform>
$$
g^{(1)}(\tau)=\frac{\int S(\nu)e^{-i2\pi\nu\tau}\,d\nu}{\int S(\nu)\,d\nu}.
$$
The <optical spectrum> weights the rotating contributions; a broad spread of <frequencies> allows them to cancel at short delays. This is the stationary field correlation–spectrum relation. The relevant spectrum is that of the optical field in the detected mode, rather than the low-frequency fluctuations of a detector's <light intensity>. https://www.rp-photonics.com/optical_spectrum.html[Rüdiger Paschotta, RP Photonics Encyclopedia, Optical Spectrum: optical field spectrum and temporal coherence]{ref}.

Here are two stipulated line profiles, each centered at $\nu_0$ with <full width at half maximum> $\Delta\nu>0$. Write $f=\nu-\nu_0$ and use normalized profiles on the whole detuning line:
$$
p_{\mathrm L}(f)=\frac{\Delta\nu/2}{\pi[f^2+(\Delta\nu/2)^2]},
\qquad p_{\mathrm G}(f)=\frac{2\sqrt{\ln2}}{\sqrt\pi\,\Delta\nu}\exp\!\left(-4\ln2\frac{f^2}{\Delta\nu^2}\right).
$$
The first is a Lorentzian, mathematically a <Cauchy distribution>; the second is a <Gaussian function>. Taking their <Fourier transforms> gives, with the carrier factor $e^{-i2\pi\nu_0\tau}$ omitted from the magnitudes,
$$
|g^{(1)}_{\mathrm L}(\tau)|=e^{-\pi\Delta\nu|\tau|},
\qquad |g^{(1)}_{\mathrm G}(\tau)|=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\pi^2\Delta\nu^2\tau^2}{4\ln2}\right).
$$
These are calculated model results, not measured line profiles. The detuning approximation treats the narrow line as extending to arbitrarily large positive and negative $f$; for optical applications require $\Delta\nu\ll\nu_0$. Exact spectra of the complex positive-frequency field have no negative absolute <frequencies>. Changing to <angular frequency> replaces $\Delta\nu$ by $\Delta\omega/(2\pi)$; it must not silently preserve the same numerical constant.

\Image[optical-coherence-delay-visibility.png]
{title=<Michelson interferometer> geometry and calculated <interferometric visibility> versus delay for stipulated line profiles}

Equal detected single-arm <light intensities> give <interferometric visibility> equal to the correlation magnitude. Doubling the Gaussian <full width at half maximum> from $1$ to $2\,\mathrm{GHz}$ halves every fixed-contrast delay. At $\tau=0.5\,\mathrm{ns}$ the model gives Gaussian <interferometric visibility> approximately $0.410686$ at $1\,\mathrm{GHz}$ and $0.0284471$ at $2\,\mathrm{GHz}$; the $1\,\mathrm{GHz}$ Lorentzian gives $0.207880$. The dashed curve instead keeps the $1\,\mathrm{GHz}$ Gaussian correlation and changes $I_2/I_1$ to $1/4$, multiplying all <interferometric visibilities> by $0.8$. Its lower contrast at zero delay therefore comes entirely from intensity imbalance.

= Optical coherence time
{parent=Temporal optical coherence}
{title2=$\tau_c$}

= Coherence time
{synonym}

An <optical coherence time> summarizes the delay scale of <temporal optical coherence>, but requires a convention. Two useful choices are the first positive delay $\tau_{1/e}$ where $|g^{(1)}|=e^{-1}$, for a monotonically decaying envelope, and the integral definition
$$
\tau_{\mathrm{int}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g^{(1)}(\tau)|^2\,d\tau.
$$
The latter counts correlation over both signs of delay and may diverge for ideal discrete lines. https://www.rp-photonics.com/coherence_time.html[Paschotta, Coherence Time: exponential time constant and integral definition]{ref}.

For the stipulated Lorentzian and Gaussian profiles in <temporal optical coherence>, direct substitution and a <Gaussian integral> give
$$
\begin{array}{c|cc}
\text{profile}&\tau_{1/e}&\tau_{\mathrm{int}}\\
\hline
\text{Lorentzian}&1/(\pi\Delta\nu)&1/(\pi\Delta\nu)\\
\text{Gaussian}&2\sqrt{\ln2}/(\pi\Delta\nu)&\sqrt{2\ln2/\pi}/\Delta\nu
\end{array}
$$
The respective dimensionless coefficients are $0.318310$ for both Lorentzian conventions, and $0.530021$ and $0.664282$ for the Gaussian conventions. The familiar scale $1/\Delta\nu$ is consequently an estimate, not a convention-independent equality. A source with narrow discrete lines can have repeated revivals instead of one monotonically decaying envelope, so recording the full $|g^{(1)}(\tau)|$ can be more informative than one <optical coherence time>.

= Optical coherence length
{parent=Optical coherence time}
{title2=$L_c=c\tau_c$}

= Coherence length
{synonym}

In this temporal sense, the <optical coherence length> is $L_c=c\tau_c$, where $c$ is the <vacuum> <speed of light> and $\tau_c$ is an explicitly chosen <optical coherence time>. It measures a delay as a vacuum-equivalent path difference; it does not measure transverse <spatial optical coherence>. https://www.rp-photonics.com/coherence_length.html[Rüdiger Paschotta, RP Photonics Encyclopedia, Coherence Length: temporal definition and path difference]{ref}.

For the stipulated $1\,\mathrm{GHz}$ profiles, the $1/e$ convention gives $L_c\approx9.54269\,\mathrm{cm}$ for the Lorentzian and $15.8896\,\mathrm{cm}$ for the Gaussian. In a <vacuum> <Michelson interferometer>, changing one mirror position by $x$ changes the round-trip delay by $2x/c$, so the mirror travel to either of these delays is $L_c/2$. In a medium, convert physical travel using the actual group delay, which can depend on <frequency>. For a narrow line centered at <vacuum> <wavelength> $\lambda_0$, differentiating $\nu=c/\lambda$ gives $\Delta\nu\approx c\Delta\lambda/\lambda_0^2$; this small-width approximation must use consistently defined spectral widths.

= Michelson interferometer
{c}
{parent=Temporal optical coherence}
{wiki}

A <Michelson interferometer> divides <light> at a <beam splitter>, reflects the two arms at mirrors and recombines the returning fields. For <vacuum> arm lengths $\ell_1,\ell_2$, the delay magnitude is $|\tau|=2|\ell_2-\ell_1|/c$. At each coarse delay, a small mirror scan produces maxima and minima of the detected <light intensity>. A <photodiode> and its electrical readout resolve these changes; the scan should cover several carrier cycles while changing $|g^{(1)}|$ negligibly. MIT's laboratory guide uses a mirror actuator, <photodiode> and electrical drive to scan <interference fringes> and extract <interferometric visibility>.<MIT 8.13–14 Experimental Physics I & II, Junior Lab, Fall 2016–Spring 2017 by MIT Department of Physics/Optical Interferometry>[§§II–IV: apparatus and visibility measurement]{ref}

A verified motivating experiment is Shaoul Ezekiel's MIT demonstration of source-spectrum control. A <polarizer> selects one or several lines from a <laser> whose adjacent lines have orthogonal <linear polarizations>. A sampled beam goes to a scanning <Fabry–Pérot interferometer> to check the <optical spectrum>; the main beam enters a <Michelson interferometer>, and a lens projects its <interference fringes> onto a screen. With one selected line the fringes retain good contrast through the demonstrated delay range. With several lines their contrast falls and returns as the arm length changes. At a fixed delay with poor contrast, selecting one line restores it; at matched arms both selections give good contrast. This control isolates the role of spectral composition rather than simply the arm position. The presentation is a qualitative demonstration, not a calibrated linewidth measurement. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/res-6-006-video-demonstrations-in-lasers-and-optics-spring-2008/203f69980203861f374be32d61f9940e_LixwAXsN8vg.pdf[Shaoul Ezekiel, MIT, Coherence length and source spectrum, demonstration transcript]{ref}.

A deliberately simpler two-line model explains the return of contrast. Take two stationary, mutually uncorrelated monochromatic contributions of equal power at $\nu_0\pm\delta\nu/2$. Their normalized <wave-field correlation> is
$$
g^{(1)}(\tau)=e^{-i2\pi\nu_0\tau}\cos(\pi\delta\nu\tau),
\qquad \mathcal V(\tau)=|\cos(\pi\delta\nu\tau)|
$$
for equal detected single-arm <light intensities>. At $\tau=1/(2\delta\nu)$ the two contributions' <interference fringes> cancel on averaging; at $\tau=1/\delta\nu$ they agree again. This is a deduction from the stipulated spectrum, not a fit to Ezekiel's two- or three-line source. Finite individual linewidths eventually damp these revivals.

For a quantitative version of the experiment, the visibility law suggests explicit controls. Measure each arm separately at every coarse delay, subtract the detector's dark signal and any measured background, then normalize by $2\sqrt{I_1I_2}/(I_1+I_2)$. Keep the returning beams aligned on the same detector region with parallel <linear polarizations>; keep detector gain fixed and avoid saturation. Resolve the small mirror scan electrically, rather than averaging over its moving <interference fringes>. Repeat scans to identify vibration or drift. These controls follow from the averaging and mode-matching assumptions in <first-order optical coherence>; loss of contrast alone cannot distinguish source decorrelation from apparatus motion, misalignment or insufficient spatial resolution. The finite travel range gives access only to a finite range of delays.

The practical gain is that <wave interference> measures field correlations even when the detector cannot follow the optical oscillation itself. A long <optical coherence length> allows unequal-arm measurements; a short one localizes high contrast near matched delays. The beautiful feature is that both fading and reappearance of <interference fringes> are sums of the same rotating <complex amplitudes>.

= Spatial optical coherence
{parent=First-order optical coherence}
{title2=$g^{(1)}_{12}(0)$}

= Spatial coherence
{synonym}

<Spatial optical coherence> compares field samples at different positions, usually across a transverse plane at equal time. Two small openings separated by $d$ sample this correlation: recombine their <light> with nearly matched travel times and measure <interferometric visibility> near the central <interference fringes>. A narrow opening in front of a broad-spectrum lamp can improve transverse <spatial optical coherence> while leaving its short <temporal optical coherence>. These are separate controls: narrow spectral selection by itself need not make an extended source spatially coherent. MIT's lecture notes illustrate the two-opening test and these contrasting source preparations. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-71-optics-spring-2009/bebe78c6a2ae2603a0d1a3ea85e0b4ca_MIT2_71S09_lec22.pdf[MIT 2.71/2.710, lecture 22, slides 3–5: spatial sampling and source examples]{ref}.

To isolate source width, stipulate a distant, narrowband source with uniform angular brightness on $-\Theta/2\leq\alpha\leq\Theta/2$, and mutually uncorrelated emission from distinct source directions. Here $\Theta\ll1$ is the full angular width in <radians>, $\lambda$ the common <wavelength>, and the two openings are separated by $d$ along the same transverse direction as the source extent. Neglect amplitude differences across the openings. Each source direction contributes a <phase difference> approximately $2\pi d\alpha/\lambda$. Averaging its complex cross term gives
$$
g^{(1)}_{12}(0)=\frac1\Theta\int_{-\Theta/2}^{\Theta/2}e^{i2\pi d\alpha/\lambda}\,d\alpha
=\frac{\sin(\pi d\Theta/\lambda)}{\pi d\Theta/\lambda},
\qquad \mathcal V=\left|\frac{\sin(\pi q)}{\pi q}\right|,
\qquad q=\frac{d\Theta}{\lambda}.
$$
The <sinc function> is extended continuously to one at $q=0$. The sign convention in the exponential depends on which opening is numbered first; the symmetric model gives the same real result either way. Equal detected single-opening <light intensities> make the magnitude equal to <interferometric visibility>. A displaced source center adds a deterministic <wave phase>, shifting the <interference fringes> without changing their contrast.

\Image[optical-coherence-source-width.png]
{title=Calculated <spatial optical coherence> for a stipulated uniform incoherent angular source: wider sources wash out and invert <interference fringes>}

The strips show $I/(I_1+I_2)=1+g^{(1)}_{12}(0)\cos\phi$ with equal single-opening <light intensities>, a common <phase difference> coordinate $\phi$ and no <single-slit diffraction> envelope. At $q=1/2$, contrast is $2/\pi\approx0.636620$; at $q=1$, it vanishes. At $q=3/2$, the correlation is $-2/(3\pi)\approx-0.212207$: weak fringes return with bright and dark positions exchanged. For $\lambda=500\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $d=0.5\,\mathrm{mm}$, the first zero occurs at $\Theta=1\,\mathrm{mrad}$. This is a calculated example, not an experimental measurement or a universal source-width law.

A proposed source-width control follows directly from this model: illuminate two fixed openings with a distant adjustable luminous slit of full width $w$ at distance $R$, so $\Theta\approx w/R$ under the <small-angle approximation>, and hold $d$ and the spectral selection fixed. Increasing $w$ overlays more mutually incoherent, displaced <interference fringe> patterns. Record profiles over several near-central fringes, measure each opening separately and correct intensity imbalance as in <first-order optical coherence>. Restrict the recorded region so the path difference stays small compared with the <optical coherence length>, and resolve the fringes with the detector. Real source nonuniformity, finite opening widths and spectral spread modify the stipulated <sinc function> law. This protocol is a model-based extension of the verified two-opening method, not a claim that the plotted curves were measured.

Thus an extended incoherent source can give locally excellent <wave interference> when the sampled separation is small, and almost none when it is larger. The useful scale $d\Theta\sim\lambda$ expresses how source extent limits the aperture over which coherent addition is available. The same angular averaging explains why narrowing a source opening can recover visible <interference fringes>, even without narrowing its <optical spectrum>.

= Diffraction grating
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}

A diffraction grating is a periodic optical structure whose repeated slits or grooves direct <light> into <diffraction orders> through <wave interference>. Its beauty is that many individually broad contributions cooperate to select narrow directions: the spacing determines where the principal peaks occur, while the number of illuminated elements determines how sharply they are selected. This makes a <diffraction grating> useful in a <spectrometer>, where separating <wavelengths> spatially lets us examine an <optical spectrum>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-007-electromagnetic-energy-from-motors-to-lasers-spring-2011/4835d62777f42e82a40c319defcc80a6_MIT6_007S11_lec28.pdf[MIT 6.007: Multiple-slit diffraction and spectrometer application, slides 5-6]{ref}.

Consider a transmission <diffraction grating> in air, illuminated at normal incidence by a <plane wave>. Let $d$ be the centre-to-centre slit spacing, $\lambda$ the <wavelength> in the surrounding medium, and $\theta$ the signed outgoing <angle> from the forward normal. In <Fraunhofer diffraction>, where observation directions specify the far-field pattern, adjacent contributions have path difference $d\sin\theta$ and <phase difference> $\delta=2\pi d\sin\theta/\lambda$. Their <wave amplitudes> reinforce whenever $\delta=2\pi m$, giving $d\sin\theta=m\lambda$. This normal-incidence geometry should be kept distinct from the incident and reflected <angle> convention in the <grating equation> below. https://www.mit.edu/course/other/viz/EM/visualizations/notes/modules/guide14.pdf[MIT: Interference and diffraction, section 14.8]{ref}.

Angular colour separation follows immediately. At fixed nonzero <diffraction order>, increasing the <wavelength> moves the peak farther from the normal. With $d=2\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, first-order <light> at $450\,\mathrm{nm}$ leaves at $13.003^\circ$, whereas $650\,\mathrm{nm}$ leaves at $18.966^\circ$: a separation of $5.963^\circ$. The zero <diffraction order> stays at $0^\circ$ for both <wavelengths>. Thus <grating dispersion> comes from an orderly dependence of <wave interference> on <wavelength>, rather than an arbitrary assignment of colours to directions.

\Image[diffraction-grating-orders.png]
{title=Normal-incidence <diffraction orders> at $600\,\mathrm{nm}$ and first-order angular colour separation}

For $N$ identical slits with uniform coherent illumination, meaning stable relative <wave phases> across the array, the normalized <wave interference> factor is
$$
F_N(\theta)=\left|\frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ij\delta}\right|^2=\left[\frac{\sin(N\delta/2)}{N\sin(\delta/2)}\right]^2,\qquad \delta=\frac{2\pi d\sin\theta}{\lambda}.
$$
The apparent $0/0$ at a principal peak has limiting value one. A short explanation uses the <geometric series>: equal <wave phases> add $N$ equal <complex amplitudes>; a small directional change distributes those contributions around the complex plane and allows cancellation. With each slit's contribution held fixed, peak <scalar wave intensity> scales as $N^2$. The normalized plots remove that height change to make the narrowing visible. Stable relative <wave phases>, or <optical coherence>, are essential; a sum of independent intensities would lose this array structure. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waveshtml/node93.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, University of Texas: Multi-slit interference, equations 10.38-10.40]{ref}.

For finite slit width $a$, the ideal rectangular-slit model multiplies $F_N$ by a broad <single-slit diffraction> envelope:
$$
\frac{I(\theta)}{I(0)}=\left[\frac{\sin u}{u}\right]^2F_N(\theta),\qquad u=\frac{\pi a\sin\theta}{\lambda}.
$$
The <sinc function> factor, extended continuously at $u=0$, controls the brightness available to the narrow peaks. For example, $a=0.5\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ gives envelope values about $0.811$, $0.405$, and $0.090$ at <diffraction orders> $1$, $2$, and $3$ in the $600\,\mathrm{nm}$ example. If an envelope zero coincides with an allowed <diffraction order>, that peak disappears. A permitted direction therefore need not produce a visible bright spot. https://www.mit.edu/course/other/viz/EM/visualizations/notes/modules/guide14.pdf[MIT: Single-slit envelope and absent interference maxima, sections 14.6-14.7]{ref}.

Historically, <Joseph von Fraunhofer> used fine parallel wires stretched between threaded rods to make <diffraction gratings>. Kenyon's historical apparatus account describes both this construction and a later reconstruction with visible <light>. The example makes the governing idea tangible: regular spacing can turn a simple array of obstacles into an instrument for distinguishing <wavelengths>. https://physics.kenyon.edu/EarlyApparatus/Optics/Diffraction_Grating/Diffraction_Grating.html[Thomas B. Greenslade Jr., Kenyon College: Diffraction grating]{ref}.

= Diffraction order
{parent=Diffraction grating}
{title2=$m$}

A diffraction order is the <integer> $m$ labeling a principal <constructive interference> direction of a <diffraction grating>: adjacent elements differ in path length by $m$ <wavelengths>. In the normal-incidence transmission geometry described above,
$$
\sin\theta_m=\frac{m\lambda}{d},\qquad \left|\frac{m\lambda}{d}\right|\leq1.
$$
Positive and negative <diffraction orders> lie on opposite sides of the forward normal. The condition expresses the range of the <sine> of a real <angle>, so it also limits the propagating <diffraction orders>.

For $\lambda=600\,\mathrm{nm}$ and $d=2\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, $\sin\theta_m=0.3m$. Taking the <inverse sine> gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
m=0 &: \quad \theta_0=0^\circ,\\
m=\pm1 &: \quad \theta_m=\pm17.458^\circ,\\
m=\pm2 &: \quad \theta_m=\pm36.870^\circ,\\
m=\pm3 &: \quad \theta_m=\pm64.158^\circ.
\end{aligned}
$$
These are seven allowed propagating <diffraction orders>. Order $m=4$ would require $\sin\theta_4=1.2$, and $m=-4$ would require $-1.2$, so neither has a propagating far-field direction. The allowed <diffraction orders> are equally spaced in $\sin\theta$, while their actual <angles> become farther apart toward grazing propagation.

Each principal peak has finite width. For the ideal uniform coherent array, the nearest zeros around order $m$ obey
$$
\sin\theta_{m,\pm}=\frac{m\lambda}{d}\pm\frac{\lambda}{Nd}.
$$
Thus the one-sided distance from the order direction to the first zero is $\Delta(\sin\theta)=\lambda/(Nd)$. Near the first <diffraction order> in our example, $N=10$ puts the zeros at $0.27$ and $0.33$, while $N=40$ puts them at $0.2925$ and $0.3075$. The respective one-sided widths are $0.03$ and $0.0075$; the full zero-to-zero widths are $0.06$ and $0.015$. These are calculated coherent peak widths, with the slit envelope omitted, and do not describe a measured instrument response. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waveshtml/node93.html[Richard Fitzpatrick: Principal maxima and their neighbouring zeros, equations 10.39-10.42]{ref}.

\Image[finite-grating-interference.png]
{title=<Single-slit diffraction> envelope and calculated first-order peak widths for $N=10$ and $N=40$}

Four times as many illuminated slits therefore give a fourfold narrowing in this coordinate without moving the <diffraction order>. That sharper directional selection helps distinguish nearby <wavelengths> in a <spectrograph>. Actual performance also depends on illumination, <optical coherence>, and source width; the separate <slit-limited resolving power of a grating> describes the contribution of the entrance slit.

= Grating equation
{parent=Diffraction grating}
{title2=$m\lambda=d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta)$}

For a reflection <diffraction grating> with both ray directions measured on the same side of its normal, adjacent grooves have path difference $d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta)$. <Constructive interference> requires this to be an integer multiple $m\lambda$. Signed-angle conventions can turn the sum into a difference; the physical path difference is unchanged.

= Grating dispersion
{parent=Grating equation}
{title2=$d\beta/d\lambda=m/(d\cos\beta)$}

At fixed incidence angle, differentiating the <grating equation> gives $d\beta/d\lambda=m/(d\cos\beta)$. A camera with <focal length> $f$ therefore has local focal-plane dispersion $q=fm/(d\cos\beta)$ near its optical axis. With coordinate $X=f\tan(\beta-\beta_0)$, the full derivative is $q=f\sec^2(\beta-\beta_0)m/(d\cos\beta)$.

= Anamorphic magnification of a grating
{parent=Grating equation}
{title2=$|d\beta/d\alpha|=\cos\alpha/\cos\beta$}

At fixed <wavelength>, the <grating equation> gives $d\beta/d\alpha=-\cos\alpha/\cos\beta$. Thus an incident angular width is magnified by $\cos\alpha/\cos\beta$ in the dispersion direction. Including the ratio of camera and <collimator> <focal lengths> gives the projected slit width. The https://www.eso.org/sci/libraries/historicaldocuments/Operating_Manuals/Operating_Manual_No.9_A1b.pdf[ESO B&C operating manual, Appendix A] explicitly includes this anamorphic factor.

= Slit-limited resolving power of a grating
{parent=Anamorphic magnification of a grating}
{title2=$R=m\lambda f_{\rm coll}/(xd\cos\alpha)$}

A slit of width $x$ at the focus of a <collimator> subtends $x/f_{\rm coll}$. Its projected width is $x f_{\rm cam}\cos\alpha/(f_{\rm coll}\cos\beta)$. Dividing this width by the <grating dispersion> gives $\Delta\lambda=xd\cos\alpha/(mf_{\rm coll})$. Thus the <spectral resolving power> is $R=m\lambda f_{\rm coll}/(xd\cos\alpha)$. Omitting the anamorphic factor gives a denominator $\cos\beta$ instead; these agree in the <Littrow configuration>.

= Littrow configuration
{c}
{parent=Diffraction grating}

In the Littrow configuration the selected diffracted ray retraces the incident ray, so $\alpha=\beta$ and the <grating equation> becomes $m\lambda=2d\sin\alpha$.

= Echelle grating
{parent=Diffraction grating}
{wiki}

An echelle grating works in high <diffraction orders>. A separate <cross-disperser> separates their overlapping <optical spectra> on a two-dimensional detector.

= Free spectral range
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta\lambda_{\rm FSR}$}

The free spectral range is the wavelength or frequency spacing between adjacent orders or resonances of an optical instrument. It identifies when different orders can overlap.

= Free spectral range of an echelle grating
{parent=Free spectral range}

At fixed incident and central outgoing angles, the <grating equation> gives $m\lambda_m=K=d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta_0)$. Adjacent central wavelengths are separated by
$$
\lambda_m-\lambda_{m+1}=\frac K{m(m+1)}\sim\frac{\lambda_m}m.
$$
This is the usual adjacent-order <free spectral range>. A detector width of approximately $q\Delta\lambda_{\rm FSR}$, where $q$ is the <grating dispersion>, covers one such interval.

= Adjacent-order spacing versus nearest-axis echelle interval
{parent=Free spectral range of an echelle grating}
{title2=$S_{\rm centres}=K/[m(m+1)]$}

At a fixed camera-axis direction, the <grating equation> gives $m\lambda_m=K=d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta_0)$, so adjacent central <wavelengths> differ by $K/[m(m+1)]$. This differs from the exact <wavelength> cell where an order is nearest the <optical camera> axis. For $\beta_0=0$ and $x=f\tan\beta$, equal distances of orders $m$ and $m+1$ mean opposite <diffraction> <angles>; adding their <diffraction grating> equations gives $\lambda=K/(m+1/2)$. The full order-$m$ cell is bounded by $K/(m+1/2)$ and $K/(m-1/2)$, hence has width $K/(m^2-1/4)$ for $m\ge2$ and accessible neighbouring orders. Both widths approach $K/m^2$ at high order.

= Fluorescence
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}

<Fluorescence> is <photon emission> following <photon absorption> by matter: an <excited state> releases some of its <energy> as <light>. A <fluorophore> can therefore mark where something is, while its <fluorescence emission spectrum> and <fluorescence lifetime> report different aspects of its environment. <Fluorescein> is one example; <FRET> exploits a competing route for excitation <energy> rather than requiring a second emitted <photon>.

The usual molecular sequence is excitation, relaxation, then emission, as in the <Jablonski diagram>. The emitted <photon> commonly has lower <energy> and longer <wavelength>, giving a <Stokes shift>; the exceptions are discussed below.<What is the Stokes Shift? by Edinburgh Instruments>[Stokes Shift in Fluorescence Spectroscopy, Figure 4 and relaxation discussion]{ref}

= Fluorophore
{parent=Fluorescence}
{wiki}

A <fluorophore> is a fluorescent <molecule> or fluorescent part of a <molecule>. Its usefulness as a label depends on how efficiently it absorbs the chosen excitation <light>, its <fluorescence quantum yield>, and its resistance to <photobleaching>. A large <fluorescence quantum yield> alone does not guarantee a bright image if few excitation <photons> are absorbed or collected.

= Jablonski diagram
{parent=Fluorescence}
{c}
{wiki}

A <Jablonski diagram> arranges molecular <energy eigenstates> vertically and draws arrows for changes between them. In the usual closed-shell molecular example, $S_0$ is the electronic <ground state>, $S_1$ and $S_2$ are electronic <excited states> with <singlet state> spin, and $T_1$ has <triplet state> spin. Each electronic state also has levels of <molecular vibration>. The diagram records possible processes, not molecular paths in space.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§1.1.1, Figure 2B; §1.1.2, Figure 4: singlet and triplet levels]{ref}

<Photon absorption> can populate an upper level of <molecular vibration> in $S_1$. Relaxation removes excess <energy> without <photon emission>; <fluorescence> commonly starts from relaxed $S_1$.<What is the Stokes Shift? by Edinburgh Instruments>[Stokes Shift in Fluorescence Spectroscopy, Figure 4 and its discussion]{ref} The drawing also includes relaxation from a higher electronic <excited state> and a competing spin-changing route to a <triplet state>.

\Image[fluorescence-jablonski-level-processes.png]
{id=fluorescence-jablonski-level-processes}
{title=Molecular excitation, relaxation and <fluorescence> in a <Jablonski diagram>}
{description=Original schematic for a usual molecular sequence. Straight coloured arrows represent <photon absorption> and <photon emission>; dashed arrows show routes without <photon emission>. Vertical position indicates <energy>, while horizontal spacing only separates processes. The level spacings and arrows are illustrative, not measurements or a prediction of transition rates. The selected emission ends above the lowest $S_0$ level, so subsequent relaxation releases additional <energy> without another fluorescent <photon>.}

= Stokes shift
{parent=Fluorescence}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Delta\lambda=\lambda_{\rm em,max}-\lambda_{\rm abs,max}$}

The <Stokes shift> compares the lowest absorption-band maximum with the <fluorescence emission spectrum> maximum. Relaxation often makes $\Delta\lambda>0$. This comparison differs from using an arbitrary excitation <wavelength>. A spectral-density maximum can move when changing from per unit <wavelength> to per unit <spectroscopic wavenumber>; specify the spectral convention.<What is the Stokes Shift? by Edinburgh Instruments>[Stokes Shift in Fluorescence Spectroscopy, definition, spectral-coordinate caveat, Figures 2 and 4]{ref}

An original illustrative <energy> budget makes the distinction concrete. Excitation at $450\,\mathrm{nm}$ and emission at $520\,\mathrm{nm}$ correspond to about $2.76$ and $2.38\,\mathrm{eV}$ per <photon>, using $E=h c/\lambda$ with <Planck constant> $h$ and <speed of light> $c$ in <vacuum>. The difference, about $0.37\,\mathrm{eV}$, must go elsewhere in the complete <energy> balance. These chosen <wavelengths> are not measured band maxima for a particular <fluorophore>.

Anti-Stokes emission has shorter <wavelength> than excitation. Additional <energy>, for example from a thermally populated level of <molecular vibration> or multiple excitation <photons>, makes it possible. The one-photon, initially relaxed drawing does not cover these cases.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§1.1.1, final paragraphs: anti-Stokes emission and multiphoton excitation]{ref}

= Fluorescence excitation spectrum
{parent=Fluorescence}
{title2=$F_{\rm ex}(\lambda_{\rm ex})$}

A <fluorescence excitation spectrum> scans excitation <wavelength> while detecting <fluorescence> in a fixed emission band. A <fluorescence emission spectrum> does the converse. The inspected spectrofluorometer example uses an excitation <monochromator> scan with the emission <monochromator> fixed, and reverses their roles for emission.<What are Absorption, Excitation and Emission Spectra? by Edinburgh Instruments>[What are Absorption, Excitation and Emission Spectra?, Excitation Spectra, PDF pp. 2–3, Figures 3–4; Emission Spectra, p. 4, Figures 5–6]{ref}

An operational model explains why this need not reproduce absorption. For one dilute, unsaturated emitting species, write $a(\lambda)$ for absorbed fraction, $R_{\rm in}(\lambda)$ for incident <photon> rate, $\Phi(\lambda)$ for <fluorescence quantum yield>, and $\eta_{\rm band}(\lambda)$ for the fraction of emitted <photons> counted in the selected band. The background-subtracted count rate is
$$
C_{\rm ex}(\lambda)=R_{\rm in}(\lambda)a(\lambda)\Phi(\lambda)\eta_{\rm band}(\lambda).
$$
Only after correcting incident <photon> rate, and with constant yield and emission-band collection, is this proportional to absorbed fraction. A nonfluorescent absorber contributes to absorption but not this readout. Thick samples, multiple emitting species, and reabsorption of <fluorescence> complicate the model.

= Fluorescence emission spectrum
{parent=Fluorescence}
{title2=$F_{\rm em}(\lambda_{\rm em})$}

A <fluorescence emission spectrum> resolves emitted <photons> by <wavelength> at fixed excitation settings. Subtract a matched sample-container and solvent background, and correct the wavelength-dependent throughput and <photodetector> sensitivity when estimating the emitted distribution. A camera image through one <fluorescence emission filter> instead measures a weighted band <integral>, not a whole <fluorescence emission spectrum>. A <fluorescence lifetime> resolves delay after excitation; its horizontal axis is <time>, not <wavelength>.

= Fluorescence quantum yield
{parent=Fluorescence}
{title2=$\Phi$}

<Fluorescence quantum yield> counts emitted fluorescent <photons> per absorbed excitation <photon>. It differs from <quantum efficiency>, which describes the <photodetector>.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§1.1.2, Quantum yield, equation 2 and the quantum-efficiency distinction]{ref}

For an original constant-rate model, let $k_{\rm r}$ be the radiative decay rate and $k_{\rm nr}$ the sum of competing rates without fluorescent <photon emission>. An initial population $N_0$ in the emitting <excited state> obeys the <rate equation>
$$
\frac{dN}{dt}=-(k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr})N,\qquad
N(t)=N_0e^{-t/\tau},\qquad
\tau=\frac{1}{k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr}}.
$$
Integrating $k_{\rm r}N(t)$ over all <time> gives
$$
\Phi=\frac{1}{N_0}\int_0^\infty k_{\rm r}N(t)\,dt
=\frac{k_{\rm r}}{k_{\rm r}+k_{\rm nr}}=k_{\rm r}\tau.
$$
Here every absorbed excitation is assumed to reach this emitting population; additional losses before that stage require another factor. Under steady weak excitation, absorbed <photon> rate $R_{\rm abs}$ and total detection fraction $\eta$ give $C=\eta\Phi R_{\rm abs}$. The count rate therefore mixes excitation, yield, and detection, whereas $\tau$ specifies a decay timescale.

= Photobleaching
{parent=Fluorescence}
{wiki}

<Photobleaching> is an irreversible loss of fluorescent capacity caused by illumination. It changes the number of active <fluorophores> over experimental <time>; <fluorescence lifetime> concerns the delay after an individual excitation. Reversible dark states and ordinary return to the <ground state> are different processes.

In a stipulated population-loss model, let $T$ denote elapsed illumination <time> and $t$ denote delay after a pulse. If active population declines as $e^{-k_{\rm b}T}$ while surviving <fluorophores> retain the same decay rates, then $I(t;T)=A_0e^{-k_{\rm b}T}e^{-t/\tau}$. Brightness falls while $\tau$ stays fixed. This example does not assert that all bleaching follows <exponential decay>: chemical changes and selective loss from a mixture can also change the observed decay shape. The existing <FRET photobleaching controls> illustrate why deliberate bleaching requires careful interpretation.

= Fluorescence lifetime
{parent=Fluorescence}
{title2=$\tau$}

For the constant-rate model in <fluorescence quantum yield>, <fluorescence lifetime> is the mean residence <time> in the emitting <excited state> and the $1/e$ timescale of its <exponential decay>. The detected fluorescent <photon> rate after an ideal instantaneous pulse is $I(t)=\eta k_{\rm r}N_0e^{-t/\tau}$ for $t\geq0$. Its amplitude and its decay timescale are separate quantities.

For a stipulated example, rates $(k_{\rm r},k_{\rm nr})=(0.05,0.20)\,\mathrm{ns}^{-1}$ give $\tau=4\,\mathrm{ns}$ and $\Phi=0.20$. Rates $(0.20,0.05)\,\mathrm{ns}^{-1}$ give the same $\tau$ but $\Phi=0.80$. Conversely, doubling $N_0$ doubles the ideal amplitude without changing $\tau$. Equal <fluorescence lifetimes> need not imply equal brightness or equal <fluorescence quantum yields>.

Different environments can supply several populations, giving $I(t)=\sum_j A_j e^{-t/\tau_j}$ rather than one <exponential decay>. A fitted single timescale then depends on the selected model and detection band. <FRET donor lifetime> uses the change in a donor's decay when transfer introduces an additional loss route; the <FRET> branch develops that interpretation.

= Time-correlated single-photon counting
{parent=Fluorescence lifetime}
{wiki}

= TCSPC
{synonym}
{c}

<TCSPC> accumulates a <histogram> of detected <photon> delays relative to repeated excitation pulses. Each cycle supplies a timing reference; many cycles provide the decay distribution. The <fluorescence instrument response> broadens that distribution. In conventional first-event acquisition, losing later <photons> in a cycle preferentially preserves early arrivals: this pile-up can falsely shorten the inferred <fluorescence lifetime>. Keep detection probability per cycle low and check stability when the illumination is attenuated.<Time-Correlated Single Photon Counting (TCSPC) by PicoQuant>[How Does TCSPC Work?, delay histogram, instrument response and first-photon pile-up paragraphs]{ref}

= Fluorescence instrument response
{parent=Time-correlated single-photon counting}
{title2=$R(t)$}

A <fluorescence instrument response> is the distribution of recorded delays for an effectively prompt optical signal. Finite excitation-pulse width and timing variation in the <photodetector> and electronics contribute. With a normalized response $R$, intrinsic fluorescent <photon> rate $I$, and background rate $B$, the expected trace is
$$
M(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}R(u)I(t-u)\,du+B(t),\qquad
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}R(u)\,du=1.
$$
Fit a candidate decay after this <convolution> with the measured response; treating the broadened leading edge as the intrinsic decay can bias <fluorescence lifetime>. The accessible technical note describes measuring prompt scattered excitation with a nonfluorescent scattering sample.<Time-Correlated Single Photon Counting (technical note v. 2.1) by Michael Wahl>[Michael Wahl, Time-Correlated Single Photon Counting, v. 2.1, p. 3: scattering-medium response measurement and iterative reconvolution]{ref}

\Image[fluorescence-decay-instrument-convolution.png]
{id=fluorescence-decay-instrument-convolution}
{title=Synthetic <fluorescence lifetime> decay broadened by an instrument response}
{description=Original stipulated model, not measurements: $I(t)=e^{-t/(4\,\mathrm{ns})}$ for $t\geq0$ and zero otherwise; $R$ is a normalized <Gaussian function> of <standard deviation> $0.6\,\mathrm{ns}$ centred at zero; the background is $B=0.025$ in the common signal units. The left panel compares intrinsic and convolved traces; the right shows how an unremoved background flattens the tail on a logarithmic scale. Negative delays come from the stipulated timing response about the pulse reference, not emission before excitation. Only the instrument-response curve is rescaled to unit peak for display.}

= Fluorescence lifetime measurement with pulsed excitation
{parent=Time-correlated single-photon counting}

The inspected apparatus sends picosecond diode-<laser> pulses into a sample cuvette. A neutral attenuation filter keeps detected events rare; an emission cut-off filter rejects scattered excitation. Collection optics deliver <fluorescence> to a <photodetector>; detector pulses and the laser-driver synchronization signal feed the timing electronics. The illustrated arrangement uses a PDL 800-B driver and TimeHarp 200 electronics. The note's recorded comparison uses two dye solutions and a prompt instrument-response trace; the dye tails have distinct slopes on a logarithmic display. Its Figure 9 measurement specifies an 80 MHz source, separately from the Figure 8 driver-setting example.<Time-Correlated Single Photon Counting (technical note v. 2.1) by Michael Wahl>[Michael Wahl, Time-Correlated Single Photon Counting, v. 2.1, pp. 7–9, Figures 8–9: cuvette/filter/detector setup; Toluidine Blue and Oxazin 4 curves; explicit 80 MHz measurement setting]{ref}

For a defensible new measurement, separately acquire detector background with excitation blocked, a solvent/container blank under excitation, and a prompt response appropriate to the timing channel. Compare successively attenuated acquisitions to expose pile-up; compare early and late acquisition intervals to expose <photobleaching>. Choose a repetition period long enough for the relevant decay or include residual population from earlier pulses in the model. These are proposed checks, not additional controls claimed for the published curves. <Photon shot noise>, background mismatch, and uncertainty in the <fluorescence instrument response> limit how uniquely multiple decay components can be inferred.

= Fluorescence microscope
{parent=Fluorescence}
{wiki}

A <fluorescence microscope> maps <photon emission> to an image. Excitation <light> passes through a <fluorescence excitation filter> and reflects from a wavelength-selective <beam splitter> into the objective <optical lens>.
