Returning <fluorescence> passes through that <beam splitter> and a <fluorescence emission filter> to the <photodetector>. Rejecting excitation makes faint emission visible.<Advanced Fluorescence Microscopy Techniques—FRAP, FLIP, FLAP, FRET and FLIM by Hellen C. Ishikawa-Ankerhold and others>[§1.2.1, Figure 5 and excitation/emission-filter paragraphs]{ref}

An ordinary image records emission-band counts at each <pixel>. Adding pulse-delay measurements gives a <fluorescence lifetime> map; scanning emission <wavelength> gives spectral information instead. <Confocal microscopy> adds spatial rejection and <confocal optical sectioning>, as developed in its own branch. An unlabeled specimen under the same illumination reveals intrinsic <fluorescence> and excitation leakage; excitation-blocked frames reveal detector background. A blank cannot perfectly predict background in a differently prepared specimen.

= Fluorescence excitation filter
{parent=Fluorescence microscope}

A <fluorescence excitation filter> selects the illumination <wavelength> band reaching the specimen. Its finite transmission changes the absorbed <photon> rate and thus image brightness. Specify that band when interpreting a <fluorescence excitation spectrum> or comparing images.

= Fluorescence emission filter
{parent=Fluorescence microscope}

A <fluorescence emission filter> transmits the chosen emitted <wavelength> band while rejecting excitation leakage. Finite rejection, overlap of the <fluorescence emission spectrum> with excitation, and the <photodetector>'s wavelength-dependent response can all affect the count. A darker filtered image alone does not establish a changed <fluorescence lifetime> or increased <photobleaching>.

= Confocal microscopy
{parent=Fluorescence microscope}
{wiki}

<Confocal microscopy> forms an image by scanning a localized illumination spot and preferentially detecting <light> from the same small region. In a thick specimen emitting <fluorescence>, a conventional <fluorescence microscope> also collects a haze from material away from the selected <optical focus>. <Confocal optical sectioning> suppresses that haze, revealing structure at a chosen depth without cutting the specimen. The useful idea is a paired selection: where excitation occurs, and which emitted <photons> reach the <photodetector>. <Introductory Confocal Concepts by Nikon Instruments Inc.>[Introductory Confocal Concepts, opening paragraphs and Fig. 2: out-of-focus fluorescence and optical sections]{ref}

The scope here is single-point, linear <fluorescence> imaging. <Confocal microscopy> can also use reflected <light>; its broader implementations need not all have the same scanning mechanism. The patent https://patents.google.com/patent/US3013467A/en[Microscopy apparatus, US3013467A, by Marvin Minsky] describes a point source imaged onto a specimen, a second pinhole receiving its image, specimen scanning, and a synchronized display. It was filed on 7 November 1957 and published on 19 December 1961; the filing date should not be described as the grant date. This is evidence for that apparatus and those dates, rather than a claim that all later <fluorescence> instruments were already present in it. <Microscopy apparatus by Marvin Minsky>[Front-page filing/publication metadata; description, Optical System, Fig. 1; Stage Scanning System]{ref}

= Conjugate pinholes in confocal microscopy
{parent=Confocal microscopy}

Two planes are optically conjugate when an <optical lens> system images one onto the other. In <confocal microscopy>, an illumination pinhole is imaged onto the selected specimen point, and that specimen point is imaged onto the detection pinhole. Thus the two <apertures> select corresponding positions in different planes; they need not occupy the same physical plane. Modern <laser> instruments can supply the illumination point with an <optical fiber> or a suitable focused <laser> mode instead of a separate metal illumination pinhole. <Confocal microscopy (chapter manuscript) by Brad Amos and others>[Amos, McConnell and Wilson, Confocal microscopy, manuscript pp. 5, 7–8: conjugate foci, Fig. 3, fiber-delivered illumination]{ref}

A fluorescent emitter at the selected <optical focus> forms a compact <point spread function> at the detection <aperture>. An emitter displaced axially produces a broadened spot there through <defocus>; a smaller fraction of its collected <light> passes. The emitter still exists and still emits <photons>. Rejected <photons> strike the opaque mask or other parts of the instrument instead of contributing to the accepted detector channel. Neither a finite pinhole nor <diffraction> makes the accepted out-of-focus contribution exactly zero.

\Image[confocal-conjugate-pinhole-ray-geometry.png]
{id=confocal-conjugate-pinhole-ray-geometry}
{title=Conjugate illumination and detection pinholes in <confocal microscopy>}
{description=Original unfolded <geometrical optics> schematic, not a scale drawing of an instrument. The top panel images an illumination pinhole onto the specimen; the lower panel images the selected specimen point onto the detection pinhole. Dashed orange <light rays> from an axially displaced point strike the mask, while an axial orange <light ray> passes. Real spots have finite <point spread functions> through <diffraction>; the drawing's perfect green intersection is only a ray construction. A <fluorescence> instrument may fold both paths through the same objective <optical lens> using a wavelength-selective <beam splitter>.}

= Confocal optical sectioning
{parent=Confocal microscopy}

At one depth, scan the illumination spot across $x$ and $y$ and assign the detector reading during each dwell interval to a <pixel>. Move the specimen or objective <optical lens> axially to acquire the next depth. A stack of these optical sections samples three-dimensional structure. With beam scanning, the returning <fluorescence> commonly retraces the scanning optics: this descanning holds the image of the currently illuminated point at the detection pinhole. With stage scanning, the fixed spot and pinhole remain aligned while the specimen moves. <Confocal microscopy (chapter manuscript) by Brad Amos and others>[Introduction, manuscript p. 2; Fig. 3 and caption, pp. 7–8: scanning and stationary return beam]{ref}

An optical section is a depth-weighted image of an intact specimen, with a finite axial response and tails. A physical cut actually separates a slice of material; its thickness and cutting damage are properties of the preparation. Changing <optical focus> selects another optical section without removing a slice, although illumination can still damage a specimen. Neither a thin physical slice nor a sharply displayed optical section makes the imaging <point spread function> infinitely narrow. <Introductory Confocal Concepts by Nikon Instruments Inc.>[Fig. 2 discussion: focused-light sections rather than physical sectioning]{ref}

Point response and section thickness answer different questions. For a spatially uniform, very thin fluorescent sheet, its response versus axial displacement is proportional to the lateral <integral> of the three-dimensional confocal response,
$$
R_{\rm sheet}(z)\propto\int_{\mathbb R^2}H_a(x,y,z)\,dx\,dy.
$$
Consequently a trace through a single bead, $H_a(0,0,z)$, does not by itself give the sheet's optical-section thickness. State which object and which <full width at half maximum> are being measured. A smaller axial sampling step gives more samples of the same response, rather than automatically improving <diffraction-limited resolution>. <Confocal microscopy (chapter manuscript) by Brad Amos and others>[Appendix §6, manuscript pp. 42–43: thin fluorescent sheet translated through focus]{ref}

= Confocal excitation and detection point spread function
{parent=Confocal microscopy}
{title2=$H_a(\mathbf r)=E(\mathbf r)D_a(\mathbf r)$}

For an isolated fluorescent molecule at displacement $\mathbf r$ from the scanning <optical focus>, write $E(\mathbf r)$ for relative excitation <light intensity> and $D_a(\mathbf r)$ for the fraction of its collected emission passed by a centred detection pinhole of radius $a$. The expected response, apart from a common brightness and collection factor, is
$$
H_a(\mathbf r)=E(\mathbf r)D_a(\mathbf r),\qquad
D_a(\mathbf r)=\int_{|\mathbf u|\leq a}p_{\rm em}(\mathbf u;\mathbf r)\,d^2\mathbf u,\qquad
\int_{\mathbb R^2}p_{\rm em}(\mathbf u;\mathbf r)\,d^2\mathbf u=1.
$$
Here $\mathbf u$ is position in the detection-pinhole plane and $p_{\rm em}$ is the emitted <light intensity> distribution there conditional on collection, with units of inverse <area>. This <point spread function normalization> retains throughput: $D_a$ is a fraction, not a curve rescaled to have peak one. The product expresses excitation followed by detection; it is an intensity/probability product, not multiplication of coherent <wave amplitudes> from different molecules. <Confocal microscopy (chapter manuscript) by Brad Amos and others>[Manuscript p. 16, Fig. 4 discussion; Appendix §5, p. 37, Eq. A17: illumination-times-detection response]{ref}

Assume linear, unsaturated <fluorescence>, independent emitters whose <light intensities> add, a stationary specimen, fixed collection efficiency and <quantum efficiency>, and negligible changes from scattering, absorption or bleaching during a scan. Different excitation and emission <wavelengths> generally give different excitation and detection responses. For a sufficiently small pinhole, $D_a$ is approximately its <area> times $p_{\rm em}$ at the centre; this is the familiar excitation-times-detection <point spread function>. It is not universally the square of one <point spread function>. A spatially invariant response further allows the image to be expressed as a <convolution> with the emitter distribution; depth-dependent <optical aberrations> can invalidate that approximation.

For an explicitly synthetic axial example, put $t=z/z_0$ and choose an on-axis excitation $E(t)=1/(1+t^2)$, inspired by a <Gaussian beam>. Stipulate a circular <Gaussian function> for the detection-plane <light intensity>, with standard deviation $s(t)=s_0\sqrt{1+t^2}$. In dimensionless coordinates $\mathbf v=\mathbf u/s_0$, put $\alpha=a/s_0$. Then
$$
p(\mathbf v;t)=\frac{\exp[-|\mathbf v|^2/(2(1+t^2))]}{2\pi(1+t^2)},\qquad
D_\alpha(t)=1-\exp\!\left[-\frac{\alpha^2}{2(1+t^2)}\right],\qquad
H_\alpha(t)=\frac{D_\alpha(t)}{1+t^2}.
$$
The expression for $D_\alpha$ follows by integrating $p$ over a <disk> using <polar coordinates>. The assumptions specify a toy model with matched axial scales; its parameters are not a calibration for a real objective <optical lens>. It omits <Airy disk> rings, vector <diffraction>, and unequal excitation and emission <wavelengths>.

\Image[confocal-synthetic-axial-pinhole-response.png]
{id=confocal-synthetic-axial-pinhole-response}
{title=Synthetic axial response: narrower pinholes reduce throughput and suppress <defocus>}
{description=Original Gaussian toy model, not measurements. The left panel keeps a common signal scale; the right divides each response by its own peak to compare widths. Here $\alpha=a/s_0$ is a radius in units of detection-plane Gaussian standard deviation, not an Airy-unit diameter. The dashed reference is the same scanned excitation with all collected emission accepted, not conventional uniform widefield illumination. Both panels retain nonzero out-of-focus tails.}

For a vanishingly small pinhole, the peak-normalized synthetic response tends to $(1+t^2)^{-2}$, whose <full width at half maximum> is $2\sqrt{\sqrt2-1}\,z_0\simeq1.2872z_0$. With all collected emission accepted it tends to $(1+t^2)^{-1}$, of width $2z_0$. Meanwhile $H_\alpha(0)=1-e^{-\alpha^2/2}\to0$ as the pinhole closes. The narrower normalized curve therefore accompanies a vanishing detected signal; peak normalization must not conceal that loss.

= Confocal pinhole throughput
{parent=Confocal microscopy}

Opening the pinhole increases accepted in-focus and out-of-focus <photons>; closing it strengthens <confocal optical sectioning> while discarding some useful in-focus <photons> too. In the synthetic model, $\alpha=0.5,1.5,4$ transmits approximately $0.1175,0.6753,0.9997$ of the collected in-focus emission. At $t=2$ the corresponding peak-normalized signals are about $0.0420,0.0597,0.1597$. These are calculated fractions under stated assumptions, not instrument measurements.

An Airy unit conventionally specifies a pinhole diameter equal to the first-zero diameter of the emission <Airy disk> in that plane. For an ideal circular pupil,
$$
d_{\rm AU}\simeq1.22\,\frac{\lambda_{\rm em}|M|}{\mathrm{NA}},
$$
where $M$ is the total specimen-to-pinhole <magnification>, $\lambda_{\rm em}$ the emission <wavelength> in <vacuum>, and $\mathrm{NA}$ the objective <numerical aperture>. A software setting needs its specified <wavelength> and relay <magnification>; it is not a universal micrometre diameter. <Confocal microscopy (chapter manuscript) by Brad Amos and others>[Appendix §6, manuscript pp. 42–43: Airy unit defined between the focal spot's zeros and conversion to real units]{ref}

For ideal independent counting, accepted source and background means $S$ and $B$ give <photon shot noise> with <variance> $S+B$ and <signal-to-noise ratio in photon counting> $S/\sqrt{S+B}$. Closing a pinhole need not improve that ratio: it depends on how both $S$ and $B$ change. Increasing exposure or excitation to replace discarded <photons> can cause bleaching, an irreversible loss of fluorescent emission capacity, or damage. Detection rejection occurs after excitation and therefore does not undo excitation outside the selected depth. Such material can absorb illumination and emit <photons> even when their emission is omitted from the recorded image.

= Fluorescent bead experiment for confocal microscopy
{parent=Confocal microscopy}

A useful motivating experiment moves a bead emitting <fluorescence> through the selected <optical focus> and records the detector signal against position. <Measurement of point-spread function (PSF) for confocal fluorescence microscopy by InCheon Song and others> (2005, §2 and Fig. 1) describes a $543.8\,\mathrm{nm}$ <laser> delivered through a single-mode <optical fiber>, collimation, beam-size adjustment and a dichroic <beam splitter>. A $100\times$, <numerical aperture> $1.3$ oil-immersion objective <optical lens> excites slide-mounted fluorescent beads and collects their emission. Emission passes a filter and collecting <optical lens>, then a pinhole, before an avalanche <photodiode>. A three-dimensional positioning stage moves the specimen. The apparatus also contains a phase plate for deliberately modified responses.<Measurement of point-spread function (PSF) for confocal fluorescence microscopy by InCheon Song and others>[§2 and Fig. 1, pp. 2–3: apparatus and readout]{ref}

The published comparison uses nominal bead diameters $0.17,0.5,1\,\mathrm{\mu m}$: larger beads give broader measured spots. This is a control on the finite emitter, because the image is the <convolution> of bead emission with the confocal <point spread function>. The smallest bead has weaker <signal> relative to <noise>; incorrect bead size, <defocus>, bleaching and <optical aberrations> limit inference from a restored response. The reported bead-size comparison concerns lateral responses; it is not a measured pinhole-size series or a measured sheet-sectioning curve.<Measurement of point-spread function (PSF) for confocal fluorescence microscopy by InCheon Song and others>[§3.2 and Figs. 4–5, pp. 4–5; p. 6, paragraph before §3.3: error sources]{ref}

To test axial pinhole selection with this kind of apparatus, the following is a proposed extension, not an additional result from that paper. Choose an isolated bead small relative to the response, centre it laterally, then step $z$ through <optical focus> while recording background-subtracted signal at several pinhole settings. Hold excitation <power>, dwell interval, detector gain, emission band and sampling fixed. Preserve raw peaks before also plotting peak-normalized traces. A lower peak and faster relative decay with axial displacement would support the paired-selection mechanism; fitting only normalized widths would miss the throughput cost.

Measure blocked-excitation background, include a bead-free region, avoid detector saturation, and repeat scans in reversed order to reveal drift or bleaching. Check pinhole centring separately, since misalignment can mimic signal loss. Record bead diameter, immersion medium, <refractive index>, <numerical aperture>, <wavelengths> and axial step. A finite bead is an approximation to an <optical point source>; a point trace does not certify <confocal optical sectioning> of a whole fluorescent sheet. Depth-dependent <spherical aberration>, scattering and sample motion can broaden or distort the response. Increasing <magnification> or making a display brighter does not correct those effects.

= Point spread function
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\operatorname{PSF}$}

= PSF
{c}
{synonym}

A <point spread function> is an imaging system's response to an <optical point source>. Here it means the distribution of image <light intensity>: an ideal point object becomes a finite spot, often with faint rings, because of <diffraction>. An unresolved <star> and a sufficiently small bead emitting <fluorescence> are practical approximations to an <optical point source>. Their image tells us how nearby object details will overlap, and reveals blurring from <optical aberrations>, <defocus>, and, for an <optical telescope>, <astronomical seeing>.

A useful convention separates shape from throughput. At a fixed image plane, let $p(\mathbf x)\geq0$ be the <point spread function> with $\int_{\mathbb R^2}p(\mathbf x)\,d^2\mathbf x=1$. Its units are inverse <area>. A point delivering detected <radiant flux> $F$ at image position $\mathbf x_0$ produces <light intensity> $Fp(\mathbf x-\mathbf x_0)$; this <point spread function normalization> makes the <integral> over the image equal to $F$. A profile divided by its own peak is convenient for comparing widths, but is a different <point spread function normalization> and does not by itself compare total <radiant flux>.

In <incoherent imaging>, different object points have negligible mutual <spatial optical coherence> over the measurement, so their image <light intensities> add. If the response also obeys <translation invariance> over the chosen <field of view>, the expected continuous image is the <convolution>
$$
I(\mathbf x)=\tau\int_{\mathbb R^2}O(\mathbf u)p(\mathbf x-\mathbf u)\,d^2\mathbf u=\tau(O*p)(\mathbf x).
$$
Here $O$ is the object brightness distribution expressed in image-scaled coordinates, with <magnification> already included, and $\tau$ is constant transmission. For $O(\mathbf u)=F_0\delta(\mathbf u-\mathbf u_0)$, where $\delta$ is the two-dimensional <Dirac delta function>, this gives $I=\tau F_0p(\mathbf x-\mathbf u_0)$. A changing response across the <field of view> instead requires a position-dependent <integral kernel> $p(\mathbf x;\mathbf u)$; one measured <point spread function> is then only a local calibration.

In <coherent imaging>, the <superposition principle> applies to the complex <wave amplitudes>. With <amplitude spread function> $h$, the corresponding ideal model is
$$
E_{\rm image}=h*E_{\rm object},\qquad I_{\rm image}=|E_{\rm image}|^2,\qquad p=\frac{|h|^2}{\int_{\mathbb R^2}|h|^2\,d^2\mathbf x}.
$$
Thus $|h*E|^2$ generally differs from $|h|^2*|E|^2$: coherent contributions produce <wave interference> terms that depend on their relative <wave phases>. Their time average vanishes for mutually incoherent emitters, which explains the intensity <convolution>. Partial <optical coherence> requires the correlations between object points as well.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Review for Optics>[pp. 7–8, §E(a–b): coherent field and incoherent intensity imaging]{ref}

\Image[point-spread-two-source-overlap.png]
{title=Ideal circular-pupil point responses and the overlap of two mutually incoherent sources}

The figure is an original <diffraction> model, not measured data. All panels have the same total <radiant flux>: each member of a pair contributes half the single-source <radiant flux>. Dashed curves show the separate <light intensity> contributions; the solid curve is their sum. The <point spread functions> have tails, so even sources satisfying the <Rayleigh criterion> overlap. As separation decreases, the image changes continuously; there is no abrupt disappearance of all information at one separation.

A motivating measured example is <Measurement of point-spread function (PSF) for confocal fluorescence microscopy by InCheon Song and others> (2005). A $543.8\,\mathrm{nm}$ <laser>, delivered through an <optical fiber>, excited fluorescent beads on a slide through a $100\times$, <numerical aperture> $1.3$ oil-immersion objective. A moving stage scanned the bead; emitted <light> returned through the objective, an emission filter and a pinhole to a <photodiode>. The readout was <fluorescence> <light intensity> against scanned position. Under the same setup, nominal bead <diameters> $0.17$, $0.5$, and $1\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ produced progressively broader measured spots. The size comparison controls for the finite object: the measured response is the <convolution> of bead emission with the effective <point spread function>, rather than a pure point response. Inverting this <convolution> model gave a response compared with the theoretical one. The smaller bead gave a weaker <signal> relative to <noise>; uncertainty in actual bead <diameter> also affected the restored width. These are limits of the calibration. In <confocal microscopy>, excitation and pinhole detection jointly determine the effective <point spread function>; this experiment does not measure the ideal widefield <Airy disk> plotted above.<Measurement of point-spread function (PSF) for confocal fluorescence microscopy by InCheon Song and others>[§2 and Fig. 1, pp. 2–3: apparatus; §3.2 and Figs. 4–5, pp. 4–5: bead-size comparison and limitations]{ref}

For a new calibration, useful additional controls are to measure detector background, avoid saturating the <photodetector>, repeat the scan to check stability, and compare isolated beads at several positions in the <field of view>. Record <wavelength>, <numerical aperture>, pinhole setting, sampling interval and <optical focus>. These are practical recommendations, not extra measurements claimed for the published experiment.

= Point spread function normalization
{parent=Point spread function}
{title2=$\int p\,d^2\mathbf x=1$}

<Point spread function normalization> specifies how the response is scaled. Unit-<integral> scaling separates image shape from total <radiant flux>; unit-peak scaling compares shapes but does not make their total <radiant fluxes> equal.

= Optical point source
{parent=Point spread function}

An <optical point source> has spatial extent negligible relative to the imaging scale being tested. The <Dirac delta function> is its idealization; a finite bead or unresolved <star> only approximates it.

= Incoherent imaging
{parent=Point spread function}

In <incoherent imaging>, contributions from distinct object points add as <light intensities> because their mutual <spatial optical coherence> is negligible. A single <convolution> additionally requires <translation invariance> of the <point spread function>.

= Coherent imaging
{parent=Point spread function}

In <coherent imaging>, complex <wave amplitudes> add with their relative <wave phases> preserved. The measured <light intensity> includes <wave interference> between the contributions.

= Amplitude spread function
{parent=Coherent imaging}
{title2=$h$}

The <amplitude spread function> is the complex field response to an <optical point source>. Its squared magnitude, with a specified <point spread function normalization>, gives the intensity <point spread function>.

= Airy disk
{c}
{parent=Point spread function}
{wiki}

The <Airy disk> is the central bright region, up to the first dark ring, in the scalar <diffraction> pattern of a uniformly illuminated, unobscured circular <aperture>. The whole pattern includes the weaker surrounding rings; its radius is distinct from its <full width at half maximum>.

= Defocus aberration
{parent=Point spread function}
{wiki}

= Defocus
{synonym}

<Defocus> is the mismatch between the receiving plane and the intended <optical focus>. Changing the plane changes the <point spread function> even when the <optical lens> is otherwise ideal.

= Optical transfer function
{parent=Point spread function}
{wiki}
{title2=$H(\boldsymbol\nu)=\widehat p(\boldsymbol\nu)$}

The <optical transfer function> is the <Fourier transform> of the normalized intensity <point spread function> for <incoherent imaging> with <translation invariance>, using <spatial frequency> in cycles per unit length. It has $H(\mathbf0)=1$. For an ideal circular pupil it is the normalized pupil autocorrelation, with cutoff $2\mathrm{NA}/\lambda_0$ in object coordinates: no overlap remains when two pupil copies are shifted apart by more than one pupil <diameter>.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Review for Optics>[p. 8, §E(b): OTF as Fourier transform of intensity PSF and pupil autocorrelation]{ref}

= Diffraction-limited system
{parent=Point spread function}
{wiki}

A <diffraction-limited system> has an optical response close to the ideal <diffraction> response for its specified <optical pupil>, <wavelength> and <optical focus>. Improving the <optical aberrations> further makes little difference to that response. This describes the optics: a poorly sampled or noisy detector can still deliver an image with much worse usable <diffraction-limited resolution>.

The <optical pupil> determines which <wave amplitudes> and <wave phases> contribute to an image point. In a scalar, <paraxial approximation> at the <focal plane> of an <optical lens> in air, write the pupil transmission as $T(u,v)e^{i\phi(u,v)}$, after removing the ideal focusing phase. The <amplitude spread function> is proportional to its scaled <Fourier transform>:
$$
h(x,y)\propto\iint T(u,v)e^{i\phi(u,v)}\exp\left[-\frac{2\pi i}{\lambda f}(ux+vy)\right]\,du\,dv.
$$
Here $(u,v)$ are pupil coordinates, $(x,y)$ are image coordinates, $f$ is <focal length>, and $\lambda$ is the <wavelength> in air. For a uniformly transmitting, unobscured circular <optical pupil> at ideal <optical focus>, the result is an <Airy disk> with surrounding rings. A blocked centre, support structures, or nonuniform transmission changes the ideal <point spread function>; “diffraction limited” does not specify one universal spot shape. The existing <Fourier model of a Lyot coronagraph> illustrates how pupil and focal masks deliberately alter this response.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Review for Optics>[pp. 6–8, §§D(c), E: lens Fourier transform and pupil transfer function]{ref}

<Optical aberrations> add pupil <wave phase> errors. <Defocus> contributes a quadratic pupil <wave phase> in the <paraxial approximation>, redistributing <light intensity> from the best-focused spot. The <Strehl ratio> compares the peak with the ideal response for the same pupil and total <radiant flux>. A broad <optical spectrum> produces a weighted sum of wavelength-dependent <point spread functions>; <chromatic aberration> can additionally change <optical focus> with <wavelength>. Thus the operating conditions belong with any claim that a system is diffraction limited.

= Numerical aperture
{parent=Diffraction-limited system}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{NA}=n\sin\alpha$}

The <numerical aperture> is $n\sin\alpha$, where $n$ is the <refractive index> of the medium between object and objective, and $\alpha$ is the half-<angle> of the admitted cone of <light>. Specify object-side or image-side <numerical aperture>; the microscope convention usually means the object side.<Numerical Aperture by Michael W. Davidson>{ref}

= Diffraction-limited resolution
{parent=Diffraction-limited system}
{title2=$\theta\sim\lambda/D$}

<Diffraction-limited resolution> is a scale for distinguishing details with a specified ideal <diffraction> response and a specified criterion. For two equally bright, mutually incoherent <optical point sources> imaged through a uniformly transmitting, unobscured circular <optical pupil>, the <Rayleigh criterion> places the centre of one <Airy disk> at the first zero of the other. At small <angles> in air this separation is
$$
\theta_{\rm R}=\frac{j_{1,1}}{\pi}\frac{\lambda}{D}\simeq1.22\frac{\lambda}{D},\qquad j_{1,1}\simeq3.831706.
$$
Here $D$ is pupil <diameter>, $\lambda$ is <wavelength> in air, and $j_{1,1}$ is the first positive zero of the order-one <Bessel function of the first kind>. This is the radius to the first zero, not the diameter of the central spot. <Fraunhofer diffraction> gives the radial peak-normalized <light intensity>
$$
P(q)=\left[\frac{2J_1(q)}q\right]^2,\qquad q=\frac{\pi D\theta}{\lambda},\qquad P(0)=1.
$$
The removable value at zero follows from $J_1(q)\sim q/2$. The pupil <Fourier transform> reduces to a radial <integral> by circular symmetry, which produces the <Bessel function of the first kind>; its first zero supplies the factor $1.22$.<Oscillations and Waves by Richard Fitzpatrick/Two-Dimensional Fourier Optics>{ref}

In the usual scalar circular-pupil approximation for a <fluorescence microscope>, the corresponding object-plane <Rayleigh criterion> is
$$
r_{\rm R}=\frac{j_{1,1}}{2\pi}\frac{\lambda_0}{\mathrm{NA}}\simeq0.61\frac{\lambda_0}{\mathrm{NA}},\qquad q=\frac{2\pi\mathrm{NA}\,r}{\lambda_0}.
$$
Here $\lambda_0$ is the vacuum emission <wavelength>, and the object-side <numerical aperture> already includes the medium's <refractive index>. Do not divide $\lambda_0$ by $n$ again. For small cone <angles> in air, $\mathrm{NA}\simeq D/(2f)$ and image-plane radius $f\theta_{\rm R}$ agrees with this form. The assumptions include a single <wavelength>, ideal <optical focus>, and mutually incoherent emitters. High <numerical aperture> can require a vector description with <polarization of an electromagnetic wave> and emitter orientation; <confocal microscopy> has its own excitation-and-detection response. The same $0.61$ formula therefore does not describe every microscope or illumination method.<Resolution by Michael W. Davidson>{ref}

\Image[circular-pupil-point-spread-scale.png]
{title=Doubling numerical aperture halves the ideal circular-pupil point-response scale at fixed wavelength}

In this original model, $\lambda_0=550\,\mathrm{nm}$ and <numerical apertures> $0.25$ and $0.50$ give first-zero radii $1.342\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ and $0.671\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. Each <point spread function> is divided by its own peak to compare shape. The pupil axes are transverse <wave vector> components multiplied by $\lambda_0/(2\pi)$, so pupil radius equals <numerical aperture>. A wider pupil accepts a wider range of transverse <wave vectors>, producing a narrower image. Increasing <magnification> alone enlarges the same spot and does not reduce its object-plane radius.

The <Rayleigh criterion> is a useful visual convention, not an absolute information barrier. For equal incoherent sources with total detected <radiant flux> $F$ and separation vector $\mathbf s$, the ideal image is
$$
I(\mathbf x)=\frac F2\left[p(\mathbf x-\mathbf s/2)+p(\mathbf x+\mathbf s/2)\right].
$$
Below the <Rayleigh criterion> this still differs from $Fp(\mathbf x)$; for small $\mathbf s$, symmetry cancels the linear change and the first change is quadratic in separation. Estimating two unknown source positions is then increasingly sensitive to <noise>, background, relative brightness and uncertainty in the <point spread function>. Localizing one isolated source and recovering an arbitrary extended object are different inference tasks. Measurements that separate optical modes can also extract separation information differently from direct image <light intensity> measurement.<Quantum Theory of Superresolution for Two Incoherent Optical Point Sources by Mankei Tsang and others>{ref}

There remains a real limit for arbitrary images in this model: the <optical transfer function> is zero above the transmitted <spatial frequency> band. Its cutoff for scalar <incoherent imaging> is $\nu_c=2\mathrm{NA}/\lambda_0$ in object-plane cycles per unit length. The <Nyquist–Shannon sampling theorem> therefore suggests object-referred <detector pixel> pitch $p_{\rm pix}\leq\lambda_0/(4\mathrm{NA})$, with finer sampling providing margin. Finite <detector pixels> also integrate over their <area>, suppressing contrast; coarse sampling can cause <aliasing>. Finer sampling cannot alone recover <spatial frequencies> removed by the optics, and <Fourier deconvolution> amplifies <noise> where the <optical transfer function> is small. <Photon shot noise>, <read noise>, <optical aberrations> and <defocus> can all worsen practical discrimination. A useful resolution claim therefore reports the object model, criterion, <wavelength>, <numerical aperture>, sampling and <signal-to-noise ratio in photon counting>.

= Rayleigh criterion
{c}
{parent=Diffraction-limited resolution}
{title2=$\theta_{\rm R}=1.22\lambda/D$}

The <Rayleigh criterion> calls two equal, mutually incoherent <optical point sources> just resolved when one circular-pupil <Airy disk>'s centre coincides with the other's first zero. It is a specified discrimination convention, distinct from <full width at half maximum> and from precision in estimating one source's position.

= Spatial resolution element
{parent=Diffraction-limited resolution}
{title2=$\Delta\vartheta\sim\lambda/D$}

A <spatial resolution element> is one independently resolved angular or spatial scale under a specified resolution criterion. For an ideal telescope of <diameter> $D$, its angular scale is of order $\lambda/D$ and its angular area of order $(\lambda/D)^2$. A <photodetector> <detector pixel> or <spaxel> can be smaller than a resolution element without adding independent spatial information.

= Strehl ratio
{c}
{parent=Point spread function}
{wiki}
{title2=$S=I_{\rm peak}/I_{\rm ideal,peak}$}

The Strehl ratio compares the peak of an observed <point spread function> with the ideal <diffraction-limited system> peak for the same pupil and total flux. It measures how strongly <wavefront errors> redistribute light away from the central peak.

= Maréchal approximation
{c}
{parent=Strehl ratio}
{title2=$S\simeq e^{-\sigma_\phi^2}$}

For small residual phase errors, $S\simeq e^{-\sigma_\phi^2}$, where $\sigma_\phi=2\pi h_{\rm rms}/\lambda$. The variance is taken after removing irrelevant constant phase. This approximation to the <Strehl ratio> is most useful near the <diffraction limit of a telescope>.

= Speckle pattern
{parent=Physical optics}
{wiki}

A speckle pattern is a granular intensity pattern from coherent <wave interference>. In stellar imaging, residual <wavefront errors> produce speckles around the central <point spread function>.

= Speckle contrast of a sinusoidal wavefront error
{parent=Speckle pattern}
{title2=$C=(\pi h_0/\lambda)^2$}

For a small sinusoidal <optical path length> error $h_0\cos(2\pi x/\Lambda)$, expand its phase factor to first order. The two exponential harmonics each have field amplitude $i\pi h_0/\lambda$ relative to the unperturbed field, so each speckle has contrast $C=(\pi h_0/\lambda)^2$. Its angular displacement is $\lambda/\Lambda$. Here $h_0$ is optical path error, not physical mirror displacement; a reflecting mirror introduces twice its surface displacement in the near-normal limit.

= Geometrical optics
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

= Geometric optics
{synonym}

Geometrical optics describes propagation by rays and <Snell's law>, neglecting <diffraction> on scales much larger than the <wavelength>.

A <light ray> is a path in an approximation, not a physical thread of zero width. In a transparent, homogeneous, isotropic medium it is straight and perpendicular to a <wavefront>; boundaries redirect it by the <law of reflection> or <Snell's law>. The approximation is useful when the <wavelength> is small compared with relevant apertures, surface curvature scales, and distances over which the <refractive index> changes. For an illustrative <wavelength> of $500\,\mathrm{nm}$ and a $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ aperture, their ratio is $5\times10^{-5}$. That small ratio supports <ray tracing> for image location, but does not eliminate <diffraction> when calculating the fine structure of a focused spot. At a <wave caustic>, <geometrical optics> can fail despite large optical elements. <MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>{ref}.

The mechanism of <refraction> is a change in <phase velocity>. For transparent isotropic media at the chosen <frequency>, the <refractive index> is
$$
n=\frac{c}{v_{\mathrm{phase}}},\qquad n_1\sin\theta_1=n_2\sin\theta_2,
$$
where $c$ is the vacuum <speed of light> and the <angles> are measured from the interface <normal vector>. At a stationary planar interface the <frequency> and tangential <wavevector> match across the boundary. Since the <wavenumber> is $k=n\omega/c$, that matching gives <Snell's law>. The transmitted <wavelength> changes while the <frequency> stays fixed. Here $n$ uses <phase velocity>, which need not equal <group velocity>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/1-3-refraction[OpenStax: Refraction]{ref}. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-974-fundamentals-of-photonics-quantum-electronics-spring-2006/ce6075363fa304e0a61078f7048e96f9_maxwell_eq_iso.pdf[MIT: Classical electromagnetism and optics, phase and group velocities]{ref}.

Choose model air with $n_1=1$ and model glass with $n_2=1.5$, without claiming these are exact values for every sample or <wavelength>. At $\theta_1=45^\circ$, <Snell's law> gives
$$
\theta_2=\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\sin45^\circ}{1.5}\right)\approx28.1255^\circ.
$$
The transmitted <light ray> bends toward the <normal vector>; its <phase velocity> is $c/1.5$, and its <wavelength> is two thirds of the air value. By <reversibility of an optical ray>, incidence from glass at $28.1255^\circ$ returns the outgoing air <angle> $45^\circ$.

Incidence from glass at $45^\circ$ is a different experiment. It would require $\sin\theta_2=1.5\sin45^\circ\approx1.06066$, so there is no real transmitted <angle>. The <critical angle for total internal reflection> is
$$
\theta_c=\arcsin(1/1.5)\approx41.8103^\circ.
$$
Above this <angle>, an ideal lossless glass-air boundary produces <total internal reflection>. The <physical optics> description still permits an <evanescent wave> in the air, although no propagating transmitted <light ray> crosses the boundary. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/1-4-total-internal-reflection[OpenStax: Total internal reflection]{ref}. https://web.mit.edu/2.710/Fall06/2.710-wk7-b-ho.pdf[MIT optics: Total internal reflection and evanescent waves, slide 40]{ref}.

\Image[optical-refraction-rays.png]
{title=Refraction into glass and total internal reflection on incidence from glass; all angles are measured from the normal}

The usefulness of <geometrical optics> is that complicated instruments can be assembled from these local rules. Applying <Snell's law> at successive curved surfaces changes a spreading bundle into a converging one. The <Fermat principle> unifies the construction through stationary <optical path length>; stationarity does not require a global minimum. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_26.html[Feynman Lectures: The principle of least time, section 26-5]{ref}. Fang's MIT notes explicitly connect this principle to <specular reflection>, <refraction>, and <thin lens> imaging, providing a route from individual paths to complete instruments. <MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>{ref}.

= Wave caustic
{parent=Geometrical optics}

= Caustic
{synonym}

A <wave caustic> is a locus where the projection of a family of rays or wavefronts becomes singular. Several ray paths may coalesce there, and the geometrical approximation can predict divergent intensity. Finite-wavelength <diffraction> replaces that ideal divergence by a resolved wave pattern. The construction applies to acoustic as well as optical waves.

= Light ray
{parent=Geometrical optics}

A path of light propagation in the <geometrical optics> approximation. In a smoothly varying propagation speed, <Fermat principle> makes its travel time stationary.

= Circular light ray in a linear speed profile
{parent=Light ray}
{title2=$(x-x_0)^2+(y+c_0)^2=R^2$}

For speed $c(y)=y+c_0>0$, <Fermat principle> and the <Beltrami identity> yield circular <light rays> whose centres lie on $y=-c_0$. The ray between $(\pm a,0)$ in the upper half-plane is $y=\sqrt{a^2+c_0^2-x^2}-c_0$.

= Eikonal equation for a variable-speed wave
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{title2=$\omega^2=c^2|\mathbf k|^2$}

For the <wave equation> $\varphi_{tt}-\nabla\cdot(c^2\nabla\varphi)=0$ with slow variables and phase $e^{i\theta/\varepsilon}$, define $\omega=-\theta_T$ and $\mathbf k=\nabla\theta$. The leading balance is $\omega^2=c^2|\mathbf k|^2$. Its positive-frequency <dispersion relation> is $\Omega=c|\mathbf k|$, and the <group velocity> is $c\mathbf k/|\mathbf k|$.

= Wave-action transport for a variable-speed wave
{parent=Eikonal equation for a variable-speed wave}
{title2=$(\omega A^2)_T+\nabla\cdot(c^2\mathbf kA^2)=0$}

The first-order imaginary balance of the <wave equation> in <geometrical optics> gives $\partial_T(\omega A^2)+\nabla\cdot(c^2\mathbf k A^2)=0$ for a real leading <amplitude>. The action density $\omega A^2$ travels with the <group velocity> $c^2\mathbf k/\omega$. For a complex <amplitude> replace $A^2$ by $|A|^2$.

= Stationary wave amplitude in a stratified wavespeed
{parent=Wave-action transport for a variable-speed wave}
{title2=$A=\mu/(c\sqrt\ell)$}

For an individual monochromatic component in $c=c(Y)$, conserved $\omega,k$ give $\ell=\sqrt{\omega^2/c^2-k^2}$. With amplitude independent of $X,T$, <wave-action transport for a variable-speed wave> conserves $c^2\ell A^2$ on a positive-$\ell$ branch, hence $A=\mu/(c\sqrt\ell)$. The divergence at $\ell=0$ signals failure of the outer <WKB approximation>.

= Phase compatibility in geometrical optics
{parent=Eikonal equation for a variable-speed wave}
{title2=$k_Y=\ell_X,\quad\mathbf k_T=-\nabla\omega$}

A smooth phase gives $\partial_{X_i}k_j=\partial_{X_j}k_i$ and $\mathbf k_T=-\nabla\omega$. These mixed-<derivative> identities allow the spatially differentiated <dispersion relation> to be rewritten as <Hamiltonian ray equations for a local dispersion relation>. Away from phase singularities they express the curl-free character of a local wavevector.

= Lagrange optical invariant
{c}
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{title2=$H=n(y_1u_2-y_2u_1)$}

For paraxial rays, the <Lagrange optical invariant> is $H=n(y_1u_2-y_2u_1)$. Lossless paraxial ray transfer preserves this quantity: in coordinates $(y,nu)$, free propagation and a <thin lens> act by <matrices> with <determinant> one, which preserve the oriented area of two ray vectors. Consequently a full angular <optical slit> width $\theta$ at an <optical pupil> <diameter> $D$ satisfies $D\theta=A_{\rm cam}p/f_{\rm cam}$ for its image width $p$ and <optical camera> <optical pupil> width $A_{\rm cam}$ in the same meridional plane.

= Etendue
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{title2=$G=n^2A\Omega$}

<Etendue> measures how much position-and-direction space a bundle of <light rays> occupies. A small spot accepting a wide range of directions can have the same <etendue> as a large spot accepting a narrow range. This makes <etendue> useful for deciding whether an <optical instrument> can deliver an extended source's entire admitted <radiant flux> to a small receiver. For forward crossings of a surface, the <geometrical optics> definition is
$$
G=\int_{\mathcal B}n^2\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega,
\qquad 0\leq\theta<\frac{\pi}{2},
$$
where $\mathcal B$ is the occupied set of surface positions and directions, $n$ is the local <refractive index>, $dA$ is actual surface <area>, $d\Omega$ is <solid angle>, and $\theta$ is the <angle> to the forward <unit normal>. The <cosine> supplies the <projected area>. Opposite crossing directions must be counted separately with positive <projected area>, rather than cancelling them in a signed <integral>. <Étendue (Photon Snacks) by R. John Koshel>[Étendue, p. 1, equation (1.1): refractive-index-weighted projected-area integral]{ref}

The shorthand $G\simeq n^2A\Omega$ assumes constant <refractive index>, a narrow nearly normal bundle, and the same admitted directions across the whole <area> $A$. At larger <angles>, use the <cosine>-weighted <integral>; a position-dependent angular acceptance cannot generally be replaced by the product of two independent extents.

= Conservation of etendue
{parent=Etendue}
{title2=$G_{\rm out}=G_{\rm in}$}

Lossless reversible propagation preserves the <etendue> of the same bundle of <light rays>. Here the model uses stationary isotropic media, <specular reflection> and <refraction>, with every selected <light ray> reaching the output and with no <optical gain> or <scattering of light>. An <aperture> that blocks some incident <light rays> changes which bundle is counted; the full incident and transmitted <etendue> need not be equal. <Étendue (Photon Snacks) by R. John Koshel>[Étendue, pp. 1–2, lossless-system and entrance-pupil restrictions; p. 3, Figure 1, preserved generalized phase-space element]{ref}

The underlying <phase space> has four coordinates. In a planar section with constant <refractive index>, let $(x,y)$ locate a <light ray> and set
$$
p_x=n\sin\theta\cos\phi,\qquad p_y=n\sin\theta\sin\phi.
$$
The <Jacobian determinant> from <spherical coordinates> gives
$$
dp_x\,dp_y=n^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi
=n^2\cos\theta\,d\Omega,
\qquad dG=dx\,dy\,dp_x\,dp_y.
$$
Thus <etendue> is a four-dimensional volume, preserved by the optical counterpart of <Liouville theorem in Hamiltonian mechanics>. Individual position and direction extents may change, and correlations between them matter. The <Lagrange optical invariant> is a related <paraxial approximation> invariant for pairs of <light rays>, rather than the full four-dimensional <measure>.

= Basic radiance
{parent=Conservation of etendue}
{title2=$L/n^2$}

= Reduced radiance
{synonym}

<Basic radiance> is <radiance> divided by the square of the <refractive index>. For <radiance> $L$ at a fixed <wavelength>,
$$
d\Phi=\frac{L}{n^2}\,dG.
$$
Lossless propagation conserves <reduced radiance> along each <light ray>; $L$ itself is conserved only when the endpoint <refractive indices> agree. A larger <irradiance> at a smaller image can result from admitting more directions without increasing <reduced radiance>. Uniform <wave absorption> can lower <radiant flux> while leaving the occupied geometric <etendue> unchanged; an opaque <aperture> removes part of that occupied set. <Radiative Transfer lecture notes by John E. Greivenkamp>[Section 22, slides 22-18 and 22-20: lossless radiance conservation and invariant basic radiance L/n²]{ref}

= Etendue of a circular aperture and cone
{parent=Etendue}
{title2=$G=n^2A\pi\sin^2\theta_{\max}$}

Consider a planar circular <aperture> of <diameter> $D$ in constant <refractive index> $n$. Suppose every position in its <area> $A=\pi D^2/4$ is filled with directions inside the same rotationally symmetric cone about the <unit normal>, with half-<angle> $\theta_{\max}$. Using <spherical coordinates>, the exact <etendue> is
$$
G=n^2A\int_0^{2\pi}\!\int_0^{\theta_{\max}}
\cos\theta\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\phi
=n^2A\pi\sin^2\theta_{\max}=\pi A\,\mathrm{NA}^2,
$$
where $\mathrm{NA}=n\sin\theta_{\max}$ is the appropriate side's <numerical aperture>. The ordinary cone <solid angle> is $2\pi(1-\cos\theta_{\max})$; its <cosine>-weighted value is $\pi\sin^2\theta_{\max}$. A full forward <hemisphere> therefore gives $G=n^2A\pi$, rather than $2\pi n^2A$. <Radiative Transfer lecture notes by John E. Greivenkamp>[Section 22, slides 22-3–22-4, solid-angle element and exact cone solid angle; slide 22-7, projected-area hemisphere factor]{ref}

For an original model example, choose $n=1$, $D=10\,\mathrm{mm}$, and $\theta_{\max}=30^\circ$. Then
$$
A=25\pi\,\mathrm{mm^2},\qquad
G=\frac{25\pi^2}{4}\,\mathrm{mm^2\,sr}
\approx61.685\,\mathrm{mm^2\,sr}.
$$
If the <radiance> is uniform across this filled bundle and equals $L=100\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}\,sr^{-1}}$, its <radiant flux> is $\Phi=LG/n^2\approx6.1685\,\mathrm{mW}$. Neither the geometry nor the chosen <radiance> is an experimental measurement.

= Area-angle tradeoff
{parent=Etendue}
{title2=$n_1^2A_1\sin^2\theta_1=n_2^2A_2\sin^2\theta_2$}

When both endpoint bundles have the filled circular-cone geometry of <etendue of a circular aperture and cone>, <conservation of etendue> gives
$$
n_1^2A_1\sin^2\theta_1=n_2^2A_2\sin^2\theta_2.
$$
For the same <refractive index>, quadrupling the <area> halves the <sine> of the cone half-<angle>. In the $10\,\mathrm{mm}$, $30^\circ$ example, an ideal output of <diameter> $20\,\mathrm{mm}$ must have $\sin\theta_2=1/4$, so $\theta_2\approx14.4775^\circ$. The exact result differs from simply halving $30^\circ$. Conversely, reducing the <diameter> to $5\,\mathrm{mm}$ requires a complete forward <hemisphere>, $\theta_2=90^\circ$, as a limiting case.

\Image[etendue-area-angle-exchange.png]
{title=Equal <etendue>: four times the spatial <area> exchanges for one quarter of the directional <area>}
{description=The circles are cross-sections of the four-dimensional <phase space>: spatial disks in $(x,y)$ and direction disks in $(p_x,p_y)$. Scales agree within each row. The displayed transformation preserves their product volume; it is a schematic of endpoint bundles, not a claim that a particular <optical lens> exactly realizes the transformation at every <angle>.}

Equal bounding <area> and <solid angle> do not alone prove equal <etendue>: an actual output can have unoccupied directions or position-direction correlations. The filled-cone equation is an exact model of the specified bundles and a capacity constraint on an <optical instrument>, not a guarantee of an aberration-free implementation.

= Aperture truncation of etendue
{parent=Etendue}
{title2=$G_{\rm transmitted}/G_{\rm incident}=(d/D)^2$}

Insert a centred opaque circular stop of <diameter> $d=6\,\mathrm{mm}$ directly in the plane of the $D=10\,\mathrm{mm}$ bundle. It transmits the same $30^\circ$ directions at every surviving position, but discards the surrounding annulus. Therefore
$$
\frac{G_{\rm transmitted}}{G_{\rm incident}}=\frac{d^2}{D^2}=0.36,
\qquad
G_{\rm transmitted}=\frac{9\pi^2}{4}\,\mathrm{mm^2\,sr}
\approx22.207\,\mathrm{mm^2\,sr}.
$$
With the uniform <radiance> of <etendue of a circular aperture and cone>, the transmitted <radiant flux> is $2.2207\,\mathrm{mW}$ and the blocked fraction is $64\%$. Surviving <light rays> retain their <radiance>; the stop has not concentrated them. Lossless propagation after the stop preserves the <etendue> of this surviving subset, not that of the original incident bundle. A stop placed elsewhere can select positions and directions together, so its physical <area> ratio alone need not give the transmitted <radiant flux> fraction.

= Optical concentrator
{parent=Etendue}
{title2=$C=A_{\rm in}/A_{\rm out}$}

An <optical concentrator> redirects <light> from an entrance <area> $A_{\rm in}$ to a smaller receiver <area> $A_{\rm out}$. Its geometric concentration ratio is $C=A_{\rm in}/A_{\rm out}$. Specify the admitted angular distribution and losses before interpreting $C$ as an <irradiance> gain: if a fraction $\eta$ of incident <radiant flux> reaches the receiver, the ratio of mean receiver to mean entrance <irradiance> is $\eta C$.

= Etendue concentration limit
{parent=Optical concentrator}
{title2=$C\leq n_{\rm out}^2/(n_{\rm in}^2\sin^2\theta_{\rm in})$}

Suppose an extended source fills every position of the entrance <aperture> with a cone of half-<angle> $\theta_{\rm in}$, and all these <light rays> must reach a one-sided planar receiver. If the receiver accepts at most a cone of half-<angle> $\theta_{\rm out}$, its available <etendue> bounds the admitted <etendue>:
$$
n_{\rm in}^2A_{\rm in}\pi\sin^2\theta_{\rm in}
\leq n_{\rm out}^2A_{\rm out}\pi\sin^2\theta_{\rm out}.
$$
Consequently
$$
C\leq\frac{n_{\rm out}^2\sin^2\theta_{\rm out}}
{n_{\rm in}^2\sin^2\theta_{\rm in}}
\leq\frac{n_{\rm out}^2}{n_{\rm in}^2\sin^2\theta_{\rm in}}.
$$
This original derivation combines <conservation of etendue> with the exact cone <integral>; the second inequality allows the complete output <hemisphere>. It bounds capacity and does not prove that a chosen shape attains the bound.

\Image[etendue-aperture-truncation-concentration.png]
{title=<Aperture> clipping discards <etendue>; full-flux concentration requires a larger output angular range}
{description=Left: a $6\,\mathrm{mm}$ stop transmits $36\%$ of the spatial disk with unchanged directional acceptance. Right: endpoint geometry for the ideal air-to-air $30^\circ$ input-cone bound, $C\leq4$. Interior sides are only a symbolic connection between ports, not a designed reflector or a traced ray path.}

For the $30^\circ$ input cone with $n_{\rm in}=n_{\rm out}=1$, $C\leq4$: a $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ entrance cannot deliver the entire filled bundle to a circular receiver smaller than $5\,\mathrm{mm}$ in <diameter>. If the output half-<angle> is restricted to $60^\circ$, $C\leq3$ and the receiver <diameter> must be at least $10/\sqrt3\approx5.7735\,\mathrm{mm}$. For a receiver medium with model <refractive index> $n_{\rm out}=1.5$, the full-<hemisphere> capacity bound instead gives $C\leq9$; that larger value also depends on transmitting the bundle into that medium.

Attempting $C=10$ in the air-to-air example forces rejection of some input. For the uniform <radiance> already specified, even filling the entire output <hemisphere> gives
$$
\eta=\frac{\Phi_{\rm out}}{\Phi_{\rm in}}
\leq\frac{G_{\rm out,capacity}}{G_{\rm in}}=\frac4{10}=0.4.
$$
The maximum mean <irradiance> gain remains $\eta C\leq4$. With nonuniform <radiance>, rejecting a given fraction of geometric <etendue> need not reject the same fraction of <radiant flux>.

These bounds use a filled extended-source bundle in passive reversible <geometrical optics>, comparing the same <wavelength> and the same radiation channels. An ideal <optical point source> does not fill that four-dimensional set. <Diffraction>, <optical coherence>, or coupling a <Gaussian beam> into an <optical fiber> require a <physical optics> calculation; the cone bound is not a universal guarantee for a single wave mode. Likewise, <optical gain> or <fluorescence> changes the propagation assumptions.

= Etendue bench measurement
{parent=Etendue}

A proposed <optical bench> test uses a steady illuminated diffuse patch, a relay made from two <optical lens> elements, interchangeable centred circular stops, and a calibrated <optical power meter> large enough to collect the whole output bundle. Start with small cone <angles> so an ordinary relay approximates the <area-angle tradeoff>. Measure the patch's image <diameter> and the accepted angular extent at both conjugate planes; the <transverse magnification> gives the spatial scale, but does not by itself measure <etendue>.

Map the local <radiance> with a baffled <photodiode> on translation and rotation stages, using two small aligned holes to select a narrow known <solid angle>. Keep the sampled position fixed while scanning direction, calibrate the sampling footprint and angular response, and repeat at several positions. Numerically integrate $n^2\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega$ over the occupied samples, and integrate $(L/n^2)dG$ to predict the collected <radiant flux>. This checks the filled-cone assumption instead of inferring it from two outer dimensions.

Compare the relay without a stop and with a stop of $0.6$ times the illuminated <diameter> in the same plane. The uniform-bundle prediction is a <radiant flux> ratio of $0.36$, with unchanged local <radiance> in the surviving samples. Cover the input for a dark reading, monitor source drift, repeat the open-stop reading, check detector linearity with calibrated <optical attenuators>, and enlarge the collecting detector or scan it to check for missed <light rays>. Lens losses, angular response, illumination gradients, alignment errors and <diffraction> can spoil the ideal prediction; their effects must be measured separately. This is a procedure to try, with predicted readouts, not purported experimental data.

= Optical axis
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{wiki}

An optical axis is the reference line through the centres of rotational symmetry of an optical system. A folded system can have successive axis segments related by reflection.

= Law of reflection
{parent=Geometrical optics}

The incident ray, reflected ray and surface normal lie in one plane; incidence and reflection angles measured from the normal are equal. For a unit incident direction $\mathbf d$ and unit normal $\mathbf n$, reflection gives $\mathbf d-2(\mathbf d\cdot\mathbf n)\mathbf n$.

= Reversibility of an optical ray
{parent=Geometrical optics}

In a stationary reciprocal optical medium, reversing the direction of a ray produces a ray along the same path. <Snell's law> is unchanged when its two media and ray directions are interchanged.

= Lens
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{wiki}

= Optical lens
{synonym}

An <optical lens> is a transparent element, or assembly of elements, that redirects a bundle of <light rays> to form images or change how the bundle spreads. In an ordinary glass <optical lens>, <refraction> at the surfaces provides the redirection: their changing <normal vectors> make <Snell's law> bend different <light rays> by different amounts. A converging <optical lens> brings incoming <light rays> parallel to its <optical axis> toward an <optical focus>; a diverging <optical lens> makes them spread as if from an <optical focus> on the incoming side. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-4-thin-lenses[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, §2.4: Thin lenses]{ref}.

For a usual glass <thin lens> in air, a form thicker at the centre than at the edges normally converges, with positive <focal length>; one thinner at the centre normally diverges. Shape alone is insufficient: the <refractive index> relative to the surroundings also matters, as the <lensmaker's equation> shows. An <aperture> selects which <light rays> pass; an <optical lens> also changes their directions. The distinction explains how an <optical camera> can gather <light> from one object point over many incoming directions and deliver it to one image location.

The beautiful simplification is that an entire bundle can be characterized by a <focal length>, and two constructed <light rays> can locate an image. The <thin lens> examples below turn that geometry into a prediction of image position, orientation and <transverse magnification>. A real <optical lens> retains <optical aberrations> and <diffraction>, so the construction is a useful leading model rather than an exact solution of <Maxwell's equations>.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[§H]{ref}

Early mathematical treatments include <Johannes Kepler>'s 1611 Dioptrice, whose title explicitly concerns vision through newly invented instruments using transparent glass, and René Descartes's 1637 La Dioptrique, whose seventh discourse discusses improving vision with shaped glass. These are landmarks in explaining and designing <optical instruments>, rather than dates for the invention of the <optical lens>. https://www.e-rara.ch/zut/content/titleinfo/120202[Kepler, Dioptrice, 1611, original edition digitized by ETH-Bibliothek]{ref}; https://classiques.uqam.ca/classiques/Descartes/dioptrique/dioptrique.html[Descartes, La Dioptrique, 1637, seventh discourse, UQAM text edition]{ref}.

= Focus
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Lens (optics)}
{wiki}
{title2=$F$}

= Optical focus
{synonym}

An <optical focus> is a point where a specified bundle of <light rays> converges, or where its backward extensions meet. For a converging <thin lens> receiving <light rays> parallel to the <optical axis>, the rear <optical focus> is $(f,0)$ when the <thin lens> is at $x=0$; its front <optical focus> is $(-f,0)$. An object's image is generally elsewhere: the <optical focus> describes the parallel incoming bundle, while the <thin lens> equation locates the image for a finite object distance.

= Focal length
{parent=Lens (optics)}
{wiki}
{title2=$f$}

The <focal length> $f$ specifies the signed distance from an ideal <thin lens> to its <optical focus> for incident <light rays> parallel to the <optical axis>. A converging <thin lens> has $f>0$, with a rear <optical focus> beyond the <thin lens>; a diverging <thin lens> has $f<0$, with a virtual <optical focus> on the incoming side. The <focal length> is a property of the <optical lens> in its specified surroundings, not the image distance for every object. For example, a converging <thin lens> with $f=1\,\mathrm m$ images an object $3\,\mathrm m$ away at $1.5\,\mathrm m$, as computed below. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_27.html[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics I, §§27-3–27-4]{ref}.

= Optical power
{parent=Focal length}
{wiki}
{title2=$\Phi=1/f$}

The <optical power> is reciprocal signed <focal length>, $\Phi=1/f$. With $f$ expressed in metres, <optical power> is expressed in <dioptres>: $f=1\,\mathrm m$ gives $\Phi=+1\,\mathrm D$, while $f=-0.5\,\mathrm m$ gives $\Phi=-2\,\mathrm D$. The sign distinguishes converging and diverging <optical lens> elements; the magnitude measures how strongly they change the direction of a <light ray> at a given height. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/25-6-image-formation-by-lenses[Urone and Hinrichs, OpenStax College Physics 2e, §25.6: Optical power and diopters]{ref}.

For two ideal <thin lens> elements in contact in air, <optical powers> add: $\Phi_{\rm total}=\Phi_1+\Phi_2$. Thus $+2\,\mathrm D$ and $-1\,\mathrm D$ combine to give $+1\,\mathrm D$, or $f_{\rm total}=1\,\mathrm m$. Separation changes the rule: for two ideal <thin lens> elements separated by $d$ in air, their equivalent <optical power> is $\Phi_1+\Phi_2-d\Phi_1\Phi_2$. The contact rule is the $d=0$ case.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[§J: Composite lenses]{ref}

= Dioptre
{parent=Optical power}
{wiki}
{title2=$1\,\mathrm D=1\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}$}

= Diopter
{synonym}

A <dioptre>, also spelled <diopter>, is the unit of <optical power> equal to one reciprocal metre. In $\Phi=1/f$, convert <focal length> to metres before taking the reciprocal: $f=25\,\mathrm{cm}=0.25\,\mathrm m$ gives $\Phi=4\,\mathrm D$, not $0.04\,\mathrm D$. A <dioptre> measures focusing strength, distinct from the <transverse magnification> of an image.

= Thick lens
{parent=Lens (optics)}

A <thick lens> retains the separation of its refracting surfaces when predicting <image formation>. In the <paraxial approximation> with air on both sides, object and image distances measured from the respective <principal planes> satisfy the familiar imaging equation. Measuring instead from one mechanical centre generally introduces an error.<Refracting systems (details), University of Tennessee physics teaching resource>[University of Tennessee physics teaching resource, Refracting systems (details), final paragraph: object and image distances must refer to the principal planes]{ref}

= Principal plane
{parent=Thick lens}
{title2=$H,H'$}

The two <principal planes> are reference planes that represent a centred imaging system in the <paraxial approximation>. In air on both sides, measure object distance from the front <principal plane>, image distance from the rear <principal plane>, and <focal length> from each <principal plane> to its corresponding <optical focus>. They need not coincide with a surface or mechanical centre. Their signed separation matters in the <Bessel lens displacement method>.<Refracting systems (details), University of Tennessee physics teaching resource>[Refracting systems (details), definition of principal planes, unit lateral magnification, and final imaging equation]{ref}

= Thin lens
{parent=Lens (optics)}
{wiki}
{title2=$1/u+1/v=1/f$}

A <thin lens> models an <optical lens> by collapsing its refracting surfaces into one plane, neglecting thickness relative to the relevant <radius of curvature> values and object and image distances. Here use an ideal converging <thin lens> in air and the <paraxial approximation>: <light rays> make small <angles> with the <optical axis>, so <Snell's law> can be linearized using the <small-angle approximation>. Equal surrounding <refractive indices> on both sides give the familiar symmetric equation.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[§§E–G]{ref}

Put the <thin lens> at $x=0$, with incident <light> travelling to the right. An object at $x=-u$ has $u>0$. The image coordinate is $x=v$: $v>0$ for a <real image> and $v<0$ for a <virtual image>. The converging <thin lens> has <focal length> $f>0$, and heights $h_o,h_i$ are signed relative to the <optical axis>. The <thin lens> equation and <transverse magnification> are
$$
\frac1f=\frac1u+\frac1v,\qquad v=\frac{fu}{u-f},\qquad m=\frac{h_i}{h_o}=-\frac vu.
$$
The expression for $v$ requires $u\ne f$. Negative <transverse magnification> means inverted; positive <transverse magnification> means upright. Remember that the positive distance $u$ differs in sign from the object's coordinate $-u$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-4-thin-lenses[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, §2.4: Sign conventions]{ref}.

For <focal length> $f=1\,\mathrm m$, object distance $u=3\,\mathrm m$, and object height $h_o=0.1\,\mathrm m$, calculation gives
$$
\frac1v=\left(1-\frac13\right)\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}=\frac23\,\mathrm{m^{-1}},\qquad v=1.5\,\mathrm m,\qquad m=-0.5,\qquad h_i=-0.05\,\mathrm m.
$$
The <real image> is inverted, half as tall, and located $1.5\,\mathrm m$ to the right of the <thin lens>. A screen at that position receives the image because the outgoing <light rays> actually meet there.

\Image[thin-lens-real-image.png]
{title=A converging <thin lens> forms a <real image>: $f=1\,\mathrm m$, $u=3\,\mathrm m$, $v=1.5\,\mathrm m$, $m=-0.5$}
{description=The object height is exactly $0.1\,\mathrm m$ and the image height is $-0.05\,\mathrm m$ in the ideal <thin lens> model. Both outgoing <light rays> travel to the right and intersect at the image tip. The vertical scale is enlarged for visibility; the drawing is a schematic of <geometrical optics>.}

For the same <focal length> $f=1\,\mathrm m$ and object height $h_o=0.1\,\mathrm m$, move the object inside the front <optical focus>, to $u=0.5\,\mathrm m$. Then
$$
\frac1v=(1-2)\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}=-1\,\mathrm{m^{-1}},\qquad v=-1\,\mathrm m,\qquad m=2,\qquad h_i=0.2\,\mathrm m.
$$
The <virtual image> is upright, twice as tall, and apparently $1\,\mathrm m$ to the left of the <thin lens>. Outgoing <light rays> still travel to the right; only their backward extensions intersect at the <virtual image>. A screen there cannot collect that image from the outgoing bundle. This geometry explains why a converging <optical lens> can act as a magnifier when held close to an object. https://openstax.org/books/college-physics-2e/pages/25-6-image-formation-by-lenses[Urone and Hinrichs, OpenStax College Physics 2e, §25.6: Virtual images inside the focus]{ref}.

\Image[thin-lens-virtual-image.png]
{title=A converging <thin lens> forms a <virtual image>: $f=1\,\mathrm m$, $u=0.5\,\mathrm m$, $v=-1\,\mathrm m$, $m=2$}
{description=The object height is exactly $0.1\,\mathrm m$ and the image height is $0.2\,\mathrm m$ in the ideal <thin lens> model. Solid outgoing <light rays> have arrows to the right. Dashed backward extensions meet at the image tip; they are not transmitted <light rays>. The vertical scale is enlarged for visibility.}

Both diagrams use two rules for <ray tracing>. A <light ray> from the object's tip parallel to the <optical axis> reaches the <thin lens> at $(0,h_o)$ and exits along the <straight line> through the rear <optical focus> $(f,0)$. Another <light ray> through the centre $(0,0)$ continues undeviated in the ideal <thin lens> model. Their intersection, or the intersection of their backward extensions, locates the image tip. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/2-4-thin-lenses[Ling, Sanny and Moebs, OpenStax University Physics Volume 3, §2.4: Ray tracing]{ref}.

A short geometric proof outline makes the signs transparent. The two outgoing <straight lines> are
$$
y_{\rm parallel}(x)=h_o\left(1-\frac xf\right),\qquad y_{\rm centre}(x)=-\frac{h_o}{u}x.
$$
For nonzero $h_o$, equating their heights at $x=v$ gives $1-v/f=-v/u$, hence the <thin lens> equation; the centre <light ray> gives $h_i/h_o=-v/u$, hence the <transverse magnification>. This derives the image equations from the ideal <ray tracing> rules, whose physical justification comes from <Snell's law> in the <paraxial approximation>.

At $u=f$, the two outgoing <straight lines> are parallel: there is no finite image position. For $u>f$ the <real image> has $m<0$; for $0<u<f$ the <virtual image> has $m>0$. These cases let an <optical camera> designer choose a receiving position and let a user understand a magnifier. An actual <optical lens> has finite <apertures>, which cause <diffraction> and impose <diffraction-limited resolution>; <optical aberrations> can further blur the image. The diagrams are ideal <geometrical optics>, with exact coordinates within that model, rather than exact predictions of <Maxwell's equations>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_27.html[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics I, §§27-6–27-7: Aberrations and resolving power]{ref}.

= Bessel lens displacement method
{c}
{parent=Thin lens}
{title2=$f=(L^2-d^2)/(4L)$}

The <Bessel lens displacement method> measures a converging <thin lens>'s <focal length> by moving only the <optical lens> between a fixed object and screen. If their separation is $L>4f$, two settings give sharp <real images>: one enlarged, the other reduced. Read their separation $d$ on an <optical bench>, then calculate
$$
d=\sqrt{L^2-4Lf},\qquad f=\frac{L^2-d^2}{4L}.
$$
The two settings exchange object and image distances, so their signed <transverse magnifications> are reciprocals. This makes the experiment a visible test of <conjugate optical planes> as well as a <focal length> measurement.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[p. 13, Practice problem 2 and adjacent magnification relation]{ref}

For an original stipulated example, put the object at $x=0$, the screen at $x=L=45\,\mathrm{cm}$, and take an ideal <thin lens> with $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$. Its two settings are $x_-=15\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $x_+=30\,\mathrm{cm}$, with $d=15\,\mathrm{cm}$. The <transverse magnifications> are $m_-=-2$ and $m_+=-1/2$: both <real images> are inverted. An object $0.50\,\mathrm{cm}$ tall would give signed image heights $-1.00\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $-0.25\,\mathrm{cm}$. Conversely, stipulated readings $L=45\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $d=15\,\mathrm{cm}$ give $f=(45^2-15^2)/(4\times45)=10\,\mathrm{cm}$. These are model calculations, not measured data or a manufacturer's calibration.

\Image[bessel-lens-conjugate-position-ray-geometry.png]
{title=Two <thin lens> settings image the same fixed object onto the same fixed screen}
{description=Original <ray tracing> for the stipulated $L=45\,\mathrm{cm}$, $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$ example. Each panel contains one physical <thin lens>; its alternative setting is dashed. The object and screen stay fixed, while object and image distances exchange. Both panels use the same scales, with heights enlarged for visibility.}

A fixed offset between a carrier's reference mark and an ideal <thin lens> plane cancels when subtracting the two carrier readings. That useful cancellation does not make a <thick lens> equivalent to one unknown optical centre: its two <principal planes> can be separated. The <Bessel lens displacement experiment> below makes this limitation explicit.

= Bessel two focus positions
{c}
{parent=Bessel lens displacement method}
{title2=$x_\pm=(L\pm\sqrt{L^2-4Lf})/2$}

Place a converging <thin lens> at coordinate $x$ between the object at $0$ and the screen at $L$. In air and the <paraxial approximation>, the required object and image distances are $u=x$ and $v=L-x$. The <thin lens> equation gives a <quadratic equation>:
$$
\frac1f=\frac1x+\frac1{L-x}=\frac{L}{x(L-x)},\qquad x^2-Lx+Lf=0.
$$
Its <discriminant> is $L^2-4Lf$. For $L>4f>0$, the two roots and their separation are
$$
x_\pm=\frac{L\pm d}{2},\qquad d=x_+-x_-=\sqrt{L^2-4Lf},\qquad x_-+x_+=L.
$$
Both roots lie inside $(0,L)$, and both object and image distances exceed $f$. The two roots therefore describe finite <real images> on the screen. Exchanging the roots exchanges $u,v$; from <transverse magnification> $m=-v/u$,
$$
m_-=-\frac{L+d}{L-d},\qquad m_+=-\frac{L-d}{L+d},\qquad m_-m_+=1.
$$
The image-height ratio between the enlarged and reduced settings is $|m_-|/|m_+|=|m_-|^2$, rather than $|m_-|$.

At $L=4f$, the roots coincide: $d=0$, $x=L/2=2f$, $u=v=2f$ and $m=-1$. There is one setting, not two separately measurable settings. For $0<L<4f$ the <discriminant> is negative, so no position between the fixed object and screen gives a sharp <real image> there. This does not forbid a <real image> elsewhere if the screen is moved. Another short proof observes that $x(L-x)$ has maximum $L^2/4$ at $x=L/2$; the focusing requirement $x(L-x)=Lf$ is possible exactly when $L\ge4f$.

= Bessel lens displacement experiment
{c}
{parent=Bessel lens displacement method}

KIT's teaching apparatus uses a glass spoke-wheel or test negative as the object, a halogen lamp with an illuminating <optical lens>, an imaging <optical lens>, a screen and a triangular rail with a position scale. The illuminating <optical lens> concentrates the lamp filament's image on the imaging <optical lens>'s centre; translucent paper checks that illumination. This reduces the contribution of peripheral <light rays> and their <spherical aberration>. The instructions require fixed object-screen separation exceeding $4f$, at least ten displacement measurements, and separate red- and blue-filter measurements to test <chromatic aberration>.<Brennweite dünner Linsen by Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Faculty of Physics teaching laboratory>[Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Brennweite dünner Linsen, Durchführung and Aufgaben 1a and 2: target, illumination check, rail readout, repeated Bessel measurements and colour controls]{ref}

The following measurement protocol elaborates that apparatus rather than reporting additional KIT results. Align the target centre, <optical lens> and screen normal along one <optical axis>; measure $L$ between the target pattern plane and receiving screen face. Lock the target and screen. Slide the imaging <optical lens> to obtain the enlarged <real image>, then the reduced <real image>, reading the same carrier reference mark at each setting. Record $r_-,r_+$ and $d=r_+-r_-$ for each pair. Record corresponding target and image intervals to test the reciprocal <transverse magnifications> independently; merely observing different image sizes is a weaker test.

Use the same central target detail to judge best <optical focus>. Bracket each setting by moving through both visibly blurred sides, repeat from both travel directions, and record the accepted position range as well as repeat scatter. Rail divisions set a readout limit but do not alone specify focusing uncertainty. Check scale calibration, carrier play and drift; a common additive carrier offset cancels in $d$, whereas scale-factor error remains. Keep illumination, <aperture>, target, screen and <optical lens> orientation fixed within a series. Change the <aperture> and colour filter in separate control series: <spherical aberration> and <chromatic aberration> can shift the apparent best <optical focus>, while a very small <aperture> introduces stronger <diffraction> and makes focus discrimination harder. Record these settings alongside the inferred <focal length>, rather than interpreting all disagreement as random <measurement error>.

For a rigid <thick lens> in air, let $h=x_{H'}-x_H$ be the signed separation of its rear and front <principal planes> along the direction of propagation. Then the positive object and image distances measured from the <principal planes> obey $u+v=L-h$. Translating the whole assembly without reversing it moves both <principal planes> equally, so the measured carrier displacement still equals the difference of the two solutions for $u$. The same algebra therefore gives
$$
d^2=(L-h)^2-4f(L-h),\qquad f=\frac{(L-h)^2-d^2}{4(L-h)},\qquad L-h>4f.
$$
Here $f$ is the effective <focal length> measured from the <principal planes>. This deduction uses the principal-plane imaging equation, not the uncorrected <thin lens> formula.<Refracting systems (details), University of Tennessee physics teaching resource>[Refracting systems (details), principal-plane definition and final paragraph on object/image distances]{ref} Mechanical centre thickness is not generally $h$. Record thickness and <optical lens> orientation, but use a justified bound or model for $h$ if it matters at the claimed precision. The two readings $L,d$ alone cannot determine both $f$ and unknown $h$; cancelling a carrier offset does not cancel this limitation.

= Bessel focal length uncertainty
{c}
{parent=Bessel lens displacement method}
{title2=$\sigma_f^2\simeq A^2\sigma_L^2+B^2\sigma_d^2+2AB\operatorname{Cov}(L,d)$}

For small <measurement errors>, write $f=L/4-d^2/(4L)$. Its <partial derivatives> and first-order <propagation of uncertainty> are
$$
A=\frac{\partial f}{\partial L}=\frac14\left(1+\frac{d^2}{L^2}\right),\qquad B=\frac{\partial f}{\partial d}=-\frac{d}{2L},
$$
$$
\sigma_f^2\simeq A^2\sigma_L^2+B^2\sigma_d^2+2AB\operatorname{Cov}(L,d).
$$
Here the $\sigma$ values are <standard deviations> used to describe uncertainty, and $\operatorname{Cov}(L,d)$ is the <covariance>. Independent carrier-reading errors give $\sigma_d^2=\sigma_{r_-}^2+\sigma_{r_+}^2$; correlated readings require the term $-2\operatorname{Cov}(r_-,r_+)$. Averaging repeated independent pairs reduces the random component of the uncertainty of the mean, but does not remove calibration or model errors.

As a stipulated uncertainty calculation, let $L=45\,\mathrm{cm}$, $d=15\,\mathrm{cm}$, $\sigma_L=0.10\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $\sigma_{r_-}=\sigma_{r_+}=0.05\,\mathrm{cm}$, with all three input errors independent. Then $\sigma_d=0.0707\,\mathrm{cm}$, $A=5/18$, $B=-1/6$ and $\sigma_f\simeq0.0302\,\mathrm{cm}$. The result would be $f=10.000\,\mathrm{cm}$ with this standard uncertainty in the stipulated <thin lens> model; these numbers are not a measured uncertainty budget. A small neglected principal-plane separation gives an additional first-order bias $f_{\rm thin}-f\simeq Ah$, distinct from that random budget.

\Image[bessel-lens-position-focal-length-sensitivity.png]
