{title=<Bessel lens displacement method>: merging settings and <focal length> sensitivity}
{description=Original ideal-model curves. Left: settings versus object-screen separation for fixed $f=10\,\mathrm{cm}$; below $L=40\,\mathrm{cm}$ there is no real setting on the fixed screen. Right: inferred <focal length> versus measured displacement at fixed $L=45\,\mathrm{cm}$, with its tangent at the stipulated $d=15\,\mathrm{cm}$. The curves are predictions, not experimental points.}

The two kinds of sensitivity must be distinguished. At fixed <focal length>,
$$
\frac{\partial d}{\partial L}=\frac{L-2f}{d},\qquad \frac{\partial x_\pm}{\partial L}=\frac12\left(1\pm\frac{L-2f}{d}\right).
$$
These <derivatives> diverge as $L$ approaches $4f$ from above: a small change in object-screen spacing strongly changes the separation of the two settings. Yet the inferred <focal length>'s displacement sensitivity $|B|=d/(2L)$ tends to zero. That does not make coalescing settings easy to measure. For fixed $L$, define the axial mismatch between the predicted <real image> and the screen as $g(x)=fx/(x-f)-(L-x)$, for $x>f$. At a focused setting,
$$
g'(x)=1-\frac{f^2}{(x-f)^2}=1-m^2.
$$
Near $u=v=2f$, moving the <optical lens> changes this mismatch only weakly, making visual discrimination of the two settings difficult. At the example's settings $g'(15\,\mathrm{cm})=-3$ and $g'(30\,\mathrm{cm})=3/4$, so equal carrier motion produces different axial <defocus> responses. This is an axial mismatch calculation, not a full prediction of perceived image sharpness.

Choose $L$ sufficiently above $4f$ to separate the two accepted focusing ranges, while retaining both <real images> on the available screen and rail. There is no universal best $L$: focusing uncertainty itself depends on <aperture>, <transverse magnification>, <optical aberrations> and the target. Close to $d=0$, the nonnegative measured separation and the quadratic term in $d$ also make first-order <propagation of uncertainty> unreliable if its error is comparable to its value. Repeatability, controls and the <principal plane> correction determine whether the elegant two-distance formula is an adequate measurement model.

= Lensmaker's equation
{parent=Thin lens}

The <lensmaker's equation> connects <focal length> to the <refractive index> and shape of a <thin lens>. For a <thin lens> in air with surfaces shaped as parts of <spheres>, <refractive index> $n$, and incident <light> travelling to the right,
$$
\frac1f=(n-1)\left(\frac1{R_a}-\frac1{R_b}\right).
$$
Each signed <radius of curvature> is positive when its centre of <curvature> lies to the right of that surface, and negative when it lies to the left. A symmetric biconvex <thin lens> with $n=1.5$, $R_a=1\,\mathrm m$, and $R_b=-1\,\mathrm m$ therefore has $1/f=0.5(1+1)\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}=1\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}$, giving $f=1\,\mathrm m$ and <optical power> $+1\,\mathrm D$. This is an ideal shape example consistent with the <thin lens> diagrams, not a specification of their finite thickness.

In a uniform surrounding medium of <refractive index> $n_s$ on both sides, replace $n-1$ by $n/n_s-1$. Thus the same shape reverses its focusing sign if its <refractive index> is lower than its surroundings: for $n=1.5$ and $n_s=2$, the same radii give $1/f=-0.5\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}$, or $f=-2\,\mathrm m$. Positive <focal length> therefore cannot be inferred from convex shape in every environment. A proof outline applies <Snell's law> with the <small-angle approximation> at each surface, adds their direction changes, and neglects the distance between them. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_27.html[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, The Feynman Lectures on Physics I, §27-3, equations 27.10–27.12]{ref}.

= Optical aberration
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{wiki}

An <optical aberration> is a departure from ideal <image formation>: <light rays> from one object point may miss their intended common image point, or image positions may depart from the intended mapping. <Spherical aberration>, <coma>, and <astigmatism> describe different ways of spreading a point's <light rays>; <optical distortion> can change image shape without spreading each point. In <physical optics>, these departures can be described by <wavefront error> relative to a reference <wavefront>. <Diffraction> also gives a finite <point spread function> in a perfect <optical instrument>, so an extended image alone does not establish an <optical aberration>.

= Seidel aberration
{c}
{parent=Optical aberration}

The five Seidel aberrations of a rotationally symmetric optical system are <spherical aberration>, <coma>, <astigmatism>, <field curvature>, and <optical distortion>.

= Spherical aberration
{parent=Seidel aberration}
{wiki}

Spherical aberration makes rays at different pupil radii focus at different axial positions. Its primary pupil dependence is quartic; the balanced <Zernike spherical mode> also contains quadratic and constant terms to remove defocus and piston.

= Coma
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Seidel aberration}
{wiki}

= Coma
{synonym}

<Coma> makes an off-axis <optical point source> appear asymmetric, often with a compact head and a flaring tail like a comet. Different parts of the <optical pupil> send its <light rays> to different transverse image positions. A sharp central <star> image therefore does not guarantee sharp images across an <optical telescope>'s <field of view>. In an idealized, aligned system with <rotational symmetry>, <coma> vanishes on the <optical axis>; misalignment can introduce <coma> even at the nominal field centre.<Amateur Telescope Optics by Vladimir Sacek/Coma aberration>[Amateur Telescope Optics, §4.2, opening paragraphs and Figure 39: field coma versus misalignment coma]{ref}

<Coma> differs from <spherical aberration>, whose dependence on <optical pupil> radius can blur an on-axis point without selecting a transverse direction. <Astigmatism> gives different <optical foci> in two perpendicular sections of an off-axis bundle; a through-focus sequence can change its elongated image's orientation. <Diffraction> persists when these <optical aberrations> vanish. A real asymmetric <point spread function> may mix several effects, so its appearance alone is not a unique diagnosis of <coma>.

The classical primary description belongs to the nineteenth-century <Seidel aberration> classification of five primary monochromatic image errors.<Amateur Telescope Optics by Vladimir Sacek/Seidel aberrations, wavefront aberration function>[Amateur Telescope Optics, §3.5.1, opening paragraph: historical classification and equation 5]{ref} It remains useful in modern design: OpticStudio's <Seidel Coefficients by ANSYS, Inc.> documentation lists <coma> as $W_{131}$ and tabulates each surface's contribution as well as the system sum (Discussion). Cancellation of contributions can reduce <coma> without making every surface individually free of it. The same documentation limits this calculation to specified systems with axially symmetric surfaces; higher-order terms and broken <rotational symmetry> require more general <ray tracing>.

= Seidel coma pupil mapping
{c}
{parent=Coma (optics)}
{title2=$w=h\rho^3\cos\theta$}

Consider an original, explicitly normalized model of primary <coma>, rather than a prescription for a particular <optical lens>. Let $(u,v)$ be <Cartesian coordinates> in a circular <optical pupil> divided by its radius $a$, with $u^2+v^2\leq1$. Let $h=\alpha/\alpha_0$ be a signed small field <angle> divided by an arbitrary positive reference <angle>. With <polar coordinates> $u=\rho\cos\theta$, $v=\rho\sin\theta$, prescribe the <wavefront error>
$$
W(u,v)=W_0w(u,v),\qquad
w(u,v)=h\rho^3\cos\theta=h\,u(u^2+v^2).
$$
Here $W_0$ is an arbitrary positive <optical path difference> scale, not a measured error. The cubic <optical pupil> dependence and linear field dependence are the classical Seidel form.<Amateur Telescope Optics by Vladimir Sacek/Coma aberration>[Amateur Telescope Optics, §4.2, classical coma formula after equation 15 and equation 13: pupil-cubic and field-linear dependence]{ref}

In the <paraxial approximation>, <light ray> direction errors follow the transverse <gradient> of <wavefront error>. For a reference sphere a distance $L$ from the exit <optical pupil> in a uniform image medium of <refractive index> $n$, the corresponding image-error scale is $s=LW_0/(na)$. Choose image-error coordinate signs so the normalized displacement is $\nabla w$; reversing the convention reverses the plotted orientation. Image coordinates below are errors relative to the ideal image of this particular object point, rather than positions of different objects:
$$
X=\frac{\partial w}{\partial u}=h(3u^2+v^2),\qquad
Y=\frac{\partial w}{\partial v}=2huv.
$$
The <partial derivatives> explain the asymmetry: opposite <optical pupil> points $(u,v)$ and $(-u,-v)$ land together, rather than on opposite sides of the ideal image. For $h>0$, every $X$ is nonnegative, while $Y$ can have either sign. The figures stipulate this single <wavefront error>, omit other <optical aberrations> and <diffraction>, and assign no instrument dimensions.

For a ring of fixed <optical pupil> radius $\rho$, the double-angle formulas for <trigonometric functions> give
$$
X=h\rho^2(2+\cos2\theta),\qquad
Y=h\rho^2\sin2\theta,\qquad
(X-2h\rho^2)^2+Y^2=h^2\rho^4.
$$
Thus each ring maps into a <circle> of radius $|h|\rho^2$ whose centre is displaced by $2h\rho^2$. Doubling $\rho$ quadruples both sizes; for $h=1$, the edge ring has centre $(2,0)$ and radius $1$, while the half-radius ring has centre $(1/2,0)$ and radius $1/4$. Superposing these displaced <circles> produces the comet-like envelope.

\Image[seidel-coma-pupil-ring-mapping.png]
{id=seidel-coma-pupil-ring-mapping}
{title=Primary <coma>: <optical pupil> rings map into displaced image <circles>}
{description=Original normalized <Seidel coma pupil mapping> at $h=1$. Colours identify equal-radius <optical pupil> rings and their image <circles>; crosses mark circle centres. The opposite points A and B have the same image position. These are <light ray> intersections, with no instrument dimensions or <diffraction> calculation.}

For uniform <irradiance> over the circular <optical pupil>, equal <area> samples give an equal-weight geometrical image. Its <centroid> is $(h,0)$ because $\langle u^2\rangle=\langle v^2\rangle=1/4$ and $\langle uv\rangle=0$. Moving the coordinate origin to that <centroid> translates the image; it does not remove its asymmetric shape. Adding a linear term to $w$ likewise translates all <light rays> equally. These origin choices must be distinguished from changing the <optical aberration> itself.

\Image[seidel-coma-off-axis-point-image.png]
{id=seidel-coma-off-axis-point-image}
{title=The normalized geometrical point image grows and reverses with field <angle>}
{description=Original equal-<area> sampling of the same circular <optical pupil>, mapped at $h=0,1/2,1,-1$ with one image scale. The orange cross is the geometrical <centroid>. Each blue point represents one equal-weight <light ray>; point density is not a calibrated <light intensity> display or a measured <point spread function>. At $h=0$ all model <light rays> coincide, while a real finite <aperture> still gives <diffraction>.}

The model's full transverse extent is $3|h|$ along $X$ and $2|h|$ along $Y$. Halving the field <angle> halves the blur, and reversing it reverses the pattern about the ideal image. This isolates why <coma> matters for an <optical camera>'s usable <field of view>: collecting more <light> does not ensure that off-axis points remain compact. Near <diffraction-limited resolution>, a <physical optics> calculation of the <point spread function> is needed instead of interpreting the geometrical tip as an infinitely sharp image.<Amateur Telescope Optics by Vladimir Sacek/Coma aberration>[Amateur Telescope Optics, §4.2, Figure 41: geometrical spots versus diffraction images]{ref}

= Off-axis pinhole test for coma
{parent=Coma (optics)}

The institution-issued <AY 105 Lab Experiment 3: Optical aberrations by California Institute of Technology> (Spring 2011 offering, Part A, pp. 2–3, 7–8) specifies a concrete <optical bench>: a lamp, opal diffuser and condensing <optical lens> illuminate a $25$ or $30\,\mu\mathrm m$ <optical pinhole>; a photographic telephoto <optical lens> acts as a <collimator>, producing an approximately $75\,\mathrm{mm}$ beam. A test <optical lens> sits on a rotation stage, followed by an adjustable <optical microscope>. The procedure establishes the on-axis image, then rotates the test <optical lens> a few degrees and examines the off-axis image through <optical focus>. It compares this readout with through-focus <ray tracing> at the corresponding field <angles> and offers a <Ronchi ruling> to calibrate the measuring eyepiece.

Part B (pp. 9–10) substitutes a spherical mirror of $1\,\mathrm m$ radius of <curvature>, with the <optical microscope> beside the incoming beam so the image is necessarily off-axis. It calls for measuring the field <angle>, calibrating the eyepiece and recording the image's length and width at minimum size. These are specified apparatus dimensions and instructions, not supplied measurement results or dimensions of the normalized figures.

A controlled extension would record images at zero and equal positive and negative field <angles>, repeat zero afterwards, and scan through <optical focus> at every setting. Keep the illuminated <optical pupil> and illumination fixed, recenter the <optical microscope> without clipping the bundle, and use its calibrated scale to separate a mere image displacement from a change in blur shape. The pure <Seidel coma pupil mapping> predicts reversal and linear growth; a stationary asymmetric central image instead calls for checking alignment. A focus-dependent change between perpendicular elongations suggests an <astigmatism> contribution. These comparisons test the model rather than assuming every off-axis flare is pure <coma>.

The <optical pinhole> has finite angular size, approximately its <diameter> divided by the <collimator>'s <focal length>; the <collimator> and observing <optical microscope> also contribute their own <optical aberrations>. A broadband lamp can mix <chromatic aberration> with the geometric blur, and <diffraction> limits the smallest visible structure. A narrow spectral band, repeated field settings and a reference image help separate these effects. Consequently a measured length and width are useful checks on off-axis <image formation>, but they cannot by themselves determine a unique <coma> coefficient or recover the full <point spread function>.

= Field curvature
{synonym}

= Distortion
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Seidel aberration}
{wiki}

= Optical distortion
{synonym}

= Chromatic aberration
{parent=Optical aberration}
{wiki}

An <optical camera> can focus a white object sharply in one colour while leaving coloured fringes around its edges in another. <Chromatic aberration> is the dependence of image position or image scale on <wavelength>, caused by <optical dispersion> in the <optical lens> materials. <Axial chromatic aberration> separates the <optical foci> along the <optical axis>; <lateral chromatic aberration> changes the <transverse magnification> with <wavelength>. Moving the receiving plane can favour one colour without bringing all colours into focus. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/302l/lectures/node142.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, University of Texas at Austin, Chromatic aberration]{ref}; https://imaging.nikon.com/sport-optics/guide/fieldscopes/choosing/choosing_03/[Nikon, Comparison of ED glass and ordinary lens]{ref}.

For ordinary <optical glass> in the visible range, the blue <refractive index> exceeds the red <refractive index>. For a fixed positive <thin lens> in air, the <lensmaker's equation> then gives
$$
\Phi(\lambda)=\frac{1}{f(\lambda)}=[n(\lambda)-1]\left(\frac{1}{R_a}-\frac{1}{R_b}\right),\qquad n_{\rm blue}>n_{\rm red}\ \Longrightarrow\ \Phi_{\rm blue}>\Phi_{\rm red}>0,\quad f_{\rm blue}<f_{\rm red}.
$$
Here $\lambda$ is <wavelength>, $n$ is <refractive index>, $\Phi$ is <optical power>, $f$ is <focal length>, and $R_a,R_b$ are the signed surface <radius of curvature> values in the <lensmaker's equation>. The blue <light rays> therefore bend more strongly and meet sooner. This ordering is a statement about ordinary visible <optical dispersion>, not a universal rule for every material and every spectral range. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/302l/lectures/node142.html[Fitzpatrick, Chromatic aberration, colour-dependent focusing]{ref}.

For a worked example, take a single positive <thin lens> with reference <optical power> $\Phi_d=5\,\mathrm D$ and <Abbe number> $V_d=60$, hence reference <focal length> $f_d=0.2\,\mathrm m$. Its F–C <optical power> difference is $\Delta\Phi=\Phi_F-\Phi_C=\Phi_d/V_d=5/60\,\mathrm D\approx0.08333\,\mathrm D$, by the <achromatic doublet power balance> relation below. This difference alone does not locate the reference <optical power> within the F–C interval. To draw a transparent example, introduce a synthetic colour coordinate $q$, a <dimensionless quantity>, with red $q=-1/2$, reference $q=0$, and blue $q=+1/2$, and choose the toy <optical dispersion> model
$$
\Phi(q)=\Phi_d\left(1+\frac{q}{V_d}\right),\qquad f(q)=\frac{1}{\Phi(q)}.
$$
The diagram's red and blue endpoints represent the C and F endpoints of this toy model. Its reference point is placed symmetrically in <optical power> by construction; $q$ is not a measured <wavelength> scale, and the model is not measured <optical glass> data. The endpoint <focal lengths> are
$$
f_{\rm red}=\frac{1}{5(1-1/120)}\,\mathrm m=\frac{24}{119}\,\mathrm m\approx0.201681\,\mathrm m,\qquad f_{\rm blue}=\frac{1}{5(1+1/120)}\,\mathrm m=\frac{24}{121}\,\mathrm m\approx0.198347\,\mathrm m.
$$
Thus the <axial chromatic aberration> in this example separates the endpoint <optical foci> by about $3.334\,\mathrm{mm}$. A single receiving plane cannot coincide with both <optical foci>.

\Image[chromatic-aberration-focal-split.png]
{title=<Chromatic aberration>: the blue <optical focus> precedes the red <optical focus> in a toy single <thin lens>}
{description=Parallel incoming <light rays> meet an ideal positive <thin lens> at $x=0$. The right panel enlarges the neighbourhood of the three <optical foci>; the reference <focal length> is $200\,\mathrm{mm}$. The synthetic model uses $\Phi_d=5\,\mathrm D$, $V_d=60$, and $q=-1/2,0,+1/2$. This is toy <optical dispersion>, not measured <optical glass>; the vertical scales are enlarged.}

The useful design idea is to cancel changes of <optical power> while retaining a positive total <optical power>. An <achromatic lens> does this with materials having different <Abbe numbers>. It greatly reduces <chromatic aberration>, but correcting two chosen <wavelengths> still leaves the possibility of a <secondary spectrum>.

= Axial chromatic aberration
{parent=Chromatic aberration}
{title2=$f(\lambda)$}

= Longitudinal chromatic aberration
{synonym}

<Axial chromatic aberration>, also called <longitudinal chromatic aberration>, is colour-dependent focus position along the <optical axis>. For incident <light rays> parallel to the <optical axis>, it appears as a changing <focal length>. The <chromatic aberration> diagram isolates this effect using an ideal <thin lens> at each colour.

= Lateral chromatic aberration
{parent=Chromatic aberration}
{title2=$m(\lambda)$}

= Transverse chromatic aberration
{synonym}

<Lateral chromatic aberration>, also called <transverse chromatic aberration>, is colour-dependent image scale: an object away from the <optical axis> can have different image heights at different <wavelengths>. Its changing <transverse magnification> produces displaced coloured edges. The on-axis parallel bundle in the <chromatic aberration> diagram illustrates <axial chromatic aberration>, and does not measure <lateral chromatic aberration>. https://imaging.nikon.com/sport-optics/guide/fieldscopes/choosing/choosing_03/[Nikon, wavelength-dependent focal point and magnification]{ref}.

= Achromatic lens
{parent=Chromatic aberration}
{wiki}

An <achromatic lens> combines <optical lens> elements with different <optical dispersion> so that two specified <wavelengths> have the same <focal length> in the <paraxial approximation>. This brings their parallel incoming <light rays> to a common <optical focus>, helping an <optical camera> form a sharper image under mixed-colour illumination. A common implementation is an <achromatic doublet>: a positive <crown glass> element paired with a negative <flint glass> element. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/302l/lectures/node142.html[Fitzpatrick, Chromatic aberration, compound lenses]{ref}.

The <achromatic doublet> does not require either material to have zero <optical dispersion>. Instead, the positive element gains positive <optical power> toward blue, while the negative element becomes more negative. Suitable <Abbe numbers> and signed <optical powers> let these changes cancel even though their reference <optical powers> do not cancel. For example, the <achromatic doublet power balance> calculation below combines $+10\,\mathrm D$ with $-5\,\mathrm D$ to obtain $+5\,\mathrm D$, or <focal length> $0.2\,\mathrm m$, with zero primary F–C <optical power> difference. The smaller <Abbe number> of the negative element makes its weaker reference <optical power> sufficient to oppose the positive element's colour change.

An <achromatic lens> is corrected at two chosen <wavelengths>, not necessarily at every <wavelength> between them. The remaining <focal length> variation is the <secondary spectrum>. Correcting <axial chromatic aberration> also does not automatically remove <lateral chromatic aberration> throughout an imaging system, <spherical aberration>, or <diffraction>. Actual designs therefore need <ray tracing> with the intended <wavelengths>, element thicknesses, separations and <apertures>. https://imaging.nikon.com/sport-optics/guide/fieldscopes/choosing/choosing_03/[Nikon, ED glass and secondary spectrum]{ref}; <AY 105 Lab Experiment 3: Optical aberrations by California Institute of Technology>[Caltech AY 105, Optical aberrations, 2011, Part A: doublet ray tracing and through-focus comparison]{ref}.

= Achromatic doublet
{parent=Achromatic lens}

An <achromatic doublet> is an <achromatic lens> with two <optical lens> elements. In the contact <thin lens> model, their <optical powers> add and their primary colour changes cancel according to <achromatic doublet power balance>. The conventional positive <achromatic doublet> uses a converging <crown glass> element and a weaker diverging <flint glass> element; these names describe a common choice, not a universal ordering of every modern <optical glass> composition.

= Achromatic doublet power balance
{parent=Achromatic lens}

<Achromatic doublet power balance> chooses two signed <optical powers> to meet two goals: obtain the required <focal length> and cancel the primary F–C colour difference. Model two <thin lens> elements in contact in air, using the <paraxial approximation>. Let $i=c,f$ label the <crown glass> and <flint glass> elements, and let $\Phi_{i,d}$ denote each element's <optical power> at the reference d line. The <Abbe number> for each material is
$$
V_i=\frac{n_{i,d}-1}{n_{i,F}-n_{i,C}}.
$$
The subscripts label standard <spectral lines>: blue F near $486.1\,\mathrm{nm}$, red C near $656.3\,\mathrm{nm}$, and reference d near $587.6\,\mathrm{nm}$. These are three specified <wavelengths>, not colour bands. A larger <Abbe number> means less relative <optical dispersion> between F and C. https://media.schott.com/api/public/content/b37dbd8fa7e64662b2d0ae523ae56238?download=true&v=97f67105[SCHOTT, Optical Glass 2025, §1.1 and §10: Wavelength table]{ref}.

For a fixed <thin lens> shape in air, write the <lensmaker's equation> as $\Phi_i(\lambda)=K_i[n_i(\lambda)-1]$, with signed shape factor $K_i=1/R_{i,a}-1/R_{i,b}$. Subtracting its C value from its F value gives the primary <optical power> difference
$$
\Delta\Phi_i=\Phi_{i,F}-\Phi_{i,C}=K_i(n_{i,F}-n_{i,C})=\frac{\Phi_{i,d}}{V_i}.
$$
The negative element has negative $\Delta\Phi_i$: its blue <optical power> is more negative, not less negative. The contact rule for <optical power> therefore gives
$$
\Phi_d=\Phi_{c,d}+\Phi_{f,d}=\frac{1}{f_d},\qquad \Delta\Phi_{\rm total}=\frac{\Phi_{c,d}}{V_c}+\frac{\Phi_{f,d}}{V_f}=0.
$$
This zero is equality of the total F and C <optical powers>, hence equality of their <focal lengths> when the total <optical power> is nonzero. The relation follows directly from the <lensmaker's equation> and the <Abbe number>; it is exact within the fixed-shape contact <thin lens> model. https://phys.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Optics/Geometric_Optics_(Tatum)/02:_Lens_and_Mirror_Calculations/2.10:_Designing_an_Achromatic_Doublet[Jeremy Tatum, University of Victoria, Geometric Optics §2.10, equations 2.10.8–2.10.11, authored text hosted by LibreTexts]{ref}. Tatum uses a yellow D-line reference; here the <Abbe number> convention uses the d line consistently.

For a toy design, choose $V_c=60$, $V_f=30$, $\Phi_{c,d}=10\,\mathrm D$ and $\Phi_{f,d}=-5\,\mathrm D$. Then
$$
\Phi_d=(10-5)\,\mathrm D=5\,\mathrm D,\qquad f_d=\frac{1}{5\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}}=0.2\,\mathrm m,\qquad \Delta\Phi_{\rm total}=\left(\frac{10}{60}-\frac{5}{30}\right)\mathrm D=0.
$$
The positive element contributes $+1/6\,\mathrm D$ to the F–C difference and the negative element contributes $-1/6\,\mathrm D$. This retains the same reference <focal length> as the single $5\,\mathrm D$, $V_d=60$ <thin lens> in <chromatic aberration>, while cancelling its primary colour split. The values are illustrative design parameters, not a catalogue <optical glass> pair.

Use the same synthetic colour coordinate $q=-1/2,0,+1/2$ and toy <optical dispersion> as in <chromatic aberration>. The two element <optical powers> and their sum become
$$
\Phi_c(q)=10\left(1+\frac{q}{60}\right)\mathrm D,\qquad \Phi_f(q)=-5\left(1+\frac{q}{30}\right)\mathrm D,\qquad \Phi_{\rm total}(q)=\left(5+q\left[\frac{10}{60}-\frac{5}{30}\right]\right)\mathrm D=5\,\mathrm D.
$$
At the red endpoint, the element <optical powers> are $9.916667\,\mathrm D$ and $-4.916667\,\mathrm D$; at the blue endpoint, they are $10.083333\,\mathrm D$ and $-5.083333\,\mathrm D$. Both sums are $5\,\mathrm D$. In this first-order toy model alone, the sum is constant for every $q$ and the <focal length> remains $0.2\,\mathrm m$ throughout. Real material <optical dispersion> is not captured by these two straight slopes, so the flat line is not a claim that a real <achromatic doublet> focuses all colours perfectly.

\Image[achromatic-doublet-power-cancellation.png]
{title=<Achromatic doublet power balance>: opposite colour changes preserve $5\,\mathrm D$ in the first-order toy model}
{description=The left panel shows each signed <optical power> and their sum against synthetic colour coordinate $q$. The right panel magnifies their changes from the reference <optical powers>: equal and opposite slopes cancel. The <crown glass> and <flint glass> labels denote toy elements with $V_c=60$ and $V_f=30$, not measured <optical glass>. Only this linear toy <optical dispersion> makes the total exactly flat throughout the interval; a real <achromatic lens> can retain a <secondary spectrum>.}

For specified nonzero reference <optical power> $\Phi_d$ and distinct <Abbe numbers>, solving the two balance equations gives
$$
\Phi_{c,d}=\frac{\Phi_d V_c}{V_c-V_f},\qquad \Phi_{f,d}=-\frac{\Phi_d V_f}{V_c-V_f}.
$$
Thus $\Phi_d>0$ and $V_c>V_f>0$ require positive <crown glass> <optical power> and negative <flint glass> <optical power>. If the two finite <Abbe numbers> were equal, cancellation would force zero total <optical power>. If they are close, the required element <optical powers> become large compared with the total: cancelling primary colour can demand much stronger individual elements.

The contact assumption matters. For two <thin lens> elements separated by distance $s$ in air, the equivalent <optical power> is $\Phi_c+\Phi_f-s\Phi_c\Phi_f$, evaluated at each <wavelength>; its colour change need not satisfy the contact balance. Finite thickness, departures from the <paraxial approximation>, and the <secondary spectrum> also limit the simple prediction.<MIT 2.71 and 2.710 Optics, Spring 2014 by Nicholas X. Fang/Lecture Notes on Geometrical Optics>[§J: Composite lenses]{ref}

= Secondary spectrum
{parent=Achromatic lens}

The <secondary spectrum> is residual <chromatic aberration> after an <achromatic lens> brings two selected <wavelengths> to the same <optical focus>. For example, equal red and blue <focal lengths> can coexist with a different green <focal length>. The <Abbe number> fixes one F–C relative difference, not the complete shape of <optical dispersion> between or beyond those <wavelengths>. The perfectly flat sum in the <achromatic doublet power balance> diagram deliberately omits this residual effect. https://imaging.nikon.com/sport-optics/guide/fieldscopes/choosing/choosing_03/[Nikon, ED glass and secondary spectrum]{ref}.

= Dispersion
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{wiki}

= Optical dispersion
{synonym}

Optical dispersion is the dependence of <refractive index> or <phase velocity> on <frequency>. It gives an <optical prism> wavelength-dependent deviation and causes <chromatic aberration>.

= Abbe number
{c}
{parent=Dispersion (optics)}
{wiki}
{title2=$V_d=(n_d-1)/(n_F-n_C)$}

The Abbe number compares refractive strength with visible <optical dispersion>. The traditional $C,F,d$ reference lines are near $656.3,486.1,587.6$ nm, respectively. A large $V_d$ means weak relative dispersion.

= Abbe diagram
{c}
{parent=Abbe number}

An Abbe diagram plots <refractive index> $n_d$ against <Abbe number> $V_d$ to show available <optical glass> families. Many diagrams put increasing $V_d$ to the left. Ordinary <crown glasses> have larger $V_d$ than ordinary <flint glasses>, while modern compositions occupy a broader region.

= Prism
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Geometrical optics}
{wiki}

= Optical prism
{synonym}

= Minimum deviation
{parent=Prism (optics)}
{wiki}
{title2=$\gamma_{\min}$}

For a homogeneous <optical prism> in air, with apex angle $\phi$ and <refractive index> $n>1$, minimum deviation occurs at the symmetric ray path. Then both internal angles are $\phi/2$ and both external angles are $(\gamma_{\min}+\phi)/2$, so <Snell's law> gives
$$
n=\frac{\sin[(\gamma_{\min}+\phi)/2]}{\sin(\phi/2)}.
$$
Measuring the deviation therefore determines the refractive index at the chosen <wavelength>.

= Optical material
{parent=Optics}

= Silver chloride
{parent=Optical material}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{AgCl}$}

<Silver chloride> is a <chemical compound> with formula $\mathrm{AgCl}$. Its darkening under illumination supplied the chemical readout in the historical <ultraviolet> discovery described above. That response depends on the incident <optical spectrum> and the sample's condition; it does not provide an uncalibrated direct measurement of incident <power>. https://coolcosmos.ipac.caltech.edu/page/lesson_ritter_experiment[Caltech/IPAC, Ritter's silver-chloride detector]{ref}.

= Butadiene
{parent=Optical material}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathrm{C}_4\mathrm{H}_6$}

Here <butadiene> denotes the 1,3-butadiene <molecule>, with formula $\mathrm{C}_4\mathrm{H}_6$. It supplies a concrete <ultraviolet> absorption example: a <photon> can promote an <electron> between <molecular orbitals> with the appropriate <energy> separation. https://openstax.org/books/organic-chemistry/pages/14-7-ultraviolet-spectroscopy[John McMurry, ultraviolet absorption by butadiene]{ref}.

= Optical glass
{parent=Optical material}
{wiki}

= Crown glass
{disambiguate=optics}
{parent=Optical glass}
{wiki}

= Crown glass
{synonym}

= Flint glass
{parent=Optical glass}
{wiki}

= Atmospheric optics
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

= Atmospheric extinction
{parent=Atmospheric optics}

Atmospheric extinction attenuates starlight through absorption and scattering. It depends on <wavelength> and path length, and is described by an <optical depth> or an extinction coefficient in astronomical magnitudes per airmass.

= Atmospheric window
{parent=Atmospheric optics}
{wiki}

An atmospheric window is a wavelength interval of relatively high atmospheric transmission. <Infrared photometric bands> are largely chosen to lie in such windows.

= Atmospheric refraction
{parent=Atmospheric optics}
{wiki}

Atmospheric refraction bends a ray through the vertical <refractive index> gradient, shifting its apparent direction. Because of <optical dispersion>, the shift varies with <wavelength>.

= Atmospheric dispersion
{parent=Atmospheric refraction}

Atmospheric dispersion is the wavelength dependence of <atmospheric refraction>. It spreads a broadband stellar image along the vertical direction toward the zenith.

= Atmospheric turbulence
{parent=Atmospheric optics}

Atmospheric turbulence produces changing <refractive index> fluctuations. These alter <optical path lengths>, distorting <wavefronts> and causing <astronomical seeing> and <atmospheric scintillation>.

= Astronomical seeing
{parent=Atmospheric turbulence}
{wiki}

= Seeing
{synonym}

Astronomical seeing is the atmospheric blurring of a stellar image, conventionally expressed as the angular full width at half maximum of its long-exposure <point spread function>, usually in arcseconds. For a large aperture in the ideal Kolmogorov model, it is approximately $0.98\lambda/r_0$ radians, with <Fried parameter> $r_0$.

= Fried parameter
{c}
{parent=Astronomical seeing}
{wiki}
{title2=$r_0$}

The Fried parameter is the atmospheric coherence length for optical phase. In the standard inertial-range model, the phase structure function is $6.88(\rho/r_0)^{5/3}$. For unchanged atmospheric turbulence, $r_0\propto\lambda^{6/5}$. The https://www.eso.org/sci/facilities/paranal/decommissioned/naco/doc/VLT-MAN-ESO-14200-2761_v103.pdf[NACO manual, section 3.1] describes this scaling and its role in adaptive correction.

= Anisoplanatism
{parent=Astronomical seeing}

Anisoplanatism is the loss of correlation between <wavefront errors> along different viewing directions. Correcting a <guide star> therefore leaves a larger residual for a sufficiently separated target.

= Isoplanatic patch
{parent=Anisoplanatism}
{wiki}

= Isoplanatic angle
{parent=Isoplanatic patch}
{title2=$\theta_0$}

The isoplanatic angle is the characteristic angular separation over which a single-direction atmospheric correction remains useful. It depends on the altitude distribution of <atmospheric turbulence> as well as on <wavelength>.

= Scintillation
{disambiguate=astronomy}
{parent=Atmospheric turbulence}
{wiki}

= Atmospheric scintillation
{synonym}

Atmospheric scintillation is the fluctuation of received stellar intensity produced by propagation through <atmospheric turbulence>. A phase-only <deformable mirror> does not generally remove these amplitude fluctuations.

= Optical instrument
{parent=Optics}
{wiki}

= Focal ratio
{parent=Optical instrument}
{title2=$F=f/D$}

The <focal ratio>, or f-number, of a converging <optical instrument> focused at infinity is the <dimensionless quantity>
$$
N=F=\frac{f}{D},
$$
where $f>0$ is effective <focal length> and $D$ is the on-axis <entrance pupil> <diameter>. The notation f/4 means $N=4$, not $f=4$ in a particular unit. A $100\,\mathrm{mm}$ <focal length> with $D=25\,\mathrm{mm}$ gives f/4. Closing the <aperture stop> until $D=12.5\,\mathrm{mm}$ gives f/8 while retaining the same <focal length>. The mechanical opening need not equal $D$. https://www.edmundoptics.com/knowledge-center/application-notes/imaging/lens-iris-aperture-setting/[Gregory Hollows and Nicholas James, Edmund Optics, System Throughput, f-number, and Numerical Aperture]{ref}.

For a <thin lens> in air with a circular <aperture stop> at the <thin lens>, the converging bundle from a distant on-axis point has half-<angle> $\alpha$ satisfying
$$
\tan\alpha=\frac{D}{2f}=\frac{1}{2N},\qquad \alpha\simeq\frac{1}{2N}\quad\hbox{in radians}.
$$
The last step uses the <small-angle approximation>. For a compound <optical lens>, the cone is set by the image-side <optical pupil> and its distance from the receiving plane; the simple triangle is not an exact high-<angle> rule. At finite object distance a <thin lens> instead gives $v=f(1+|m|)$, by its imaging equation and <transverse magnification>, so its working cone corresponds to $v/D=N(1+|m|)$. Nominal <focal ratio> therefore does not alone specify close-range image <irradiance>. https://www.edmundoptics.com/knowledge-center/application-notes/imaging/lens-iris-aperture-setting/[Hollows and James, working f-number and image-space cone]{ref}.

Two useful scalings follow under stated conditions. For a fixed uniform incident beam and unchanged transmission, collected <radiant flux> scales as $D^2$. For an extended source with fixed <radiance>, the central image <irradiance> scales approximately as $N^{-2}$ in the <paraxial approximation>: the <solid angle> of the cone scales as $(D/f)^2$. For an ideal uniformly illuminated unobscured circular <entrance pupil>, the first dark ring of the <point spread function> has angular <radius> and <focal plane> <radius>
$$
\theta_1\simeq1.22\frac{\lambda}{D},\qquad r_1\simeq f\theta_1=1.22\lambda N.
$$
Here $\lambda$ is <wavelength>. These are first-zero radii, not diameters or a universal rule for resolving arbitrary objects. A proof outline evaluates the circular <aperture>'s <Fraunhofer diffraction> using its <Fourier transform>, then uses the first zero of the resulting <Bessel function>. https://farside.ph.utexas.edu/teaching/315/Waves/node98.html[Richard Fitzpatrick, University of Texas at Austin, Two-Dimensional Fourier Optics, circular-aperture first minimum]{ref}.

At $\lambda=0.55\,\mathrm{\mu m}$, the example's f/4 and f/8 first-ring radii are $2.684$ and $5.368\,\mathrm{\mu m}$. Closing the <aperture stop> halves $D$, quarters collected <radiant flux> and doubles this <diffraction> radius. It can also reduce <optical aberrations> by excluding outer <light rays>; actual sharpness reflects both effects. This explains why a smaller <aperture> need not produce a sharper image. https://www.edmundoptics.com/knowledge-center/application-notes/imaging/limitations-on-resolution-and-contrast-the-airy-disk/[Gregory Hollows and Nicholas James, Edmund Optics, The Airy Disk and Diffraction Limit]{ref}.

\Image[aperture-focal-ratio-field-view-cone-scaling.png]
{title=<Aperture> and <focal ratio>: a common <optical focus>, reduced collection and increased <diffraction> radius}
{description=Original analytical illustration. Left: equal-scale <thin lens> geometry with $f=100\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $D=25$ or $12.5\,\mathrm{mm}$. Right: collected <radiant flux> relative to f/4 is $(4/N)^2$, and first-ring <radius> relative to f/4 is $N/4$. The <wavelength> is $0.55\,\mathrm{\mu m}$; the <entrance pupil> is circular, uniformly illuminated and unobscured. These are calculated quantities, not measurements.}

A proposed <optical bench> check puts a fixed <collimator> before a converging <thin lens> and a <photodetector> large enough to collect the entire image behind it. Compare two centred <aperture stops> of known <diameters> while fixing source output, detector gain and integration time. The integrated signal tests the $D^2$ collection law; a spatially resolved <optical camera> at the <focal plane> can separately measure the <point spread function>. Repeat a dark reading and the first <aperture stop>, and check that neither the incoming beam nor detector clips either bundle. A resolved pinhole source, imperfect collimation, <optical aberrations> and detector pixel size can dominate the apparent spot width; a single peak reading does not measure total collected <radiant flux>.

= Optical camera
{parent=Optical instrument}

An <optical camera> images incident beams on a <photodetector>; a <spectrograph> <optical camera> maps a diffracted direction into a position in its <focal plane>.

= Optical slit
{parent=Optical instrument}

= Spectrograph slit
{synonym}

An <optical slit> selects a narrow region of a <focal plane>. In a slit-limited <spectrograph>, the width of its image divided by the <linear spectral dispersion> sets the spectral width.

= Focal plane
{parent=Optical instrument}

A focal plane is a plane in which an optical system forms images of distant directions. Aberrations can make the best-focus surface curved; a flat detector may then require a field-flattening optical element.

= High-contrast imaging
{parent=Optical instrument}

High-contrast imaging seeks faint objects near a bright source. Residual <point spread functions> and <speckle patterns> can dominate <photon shot noise>; <angular differential imaging> and <simultaneous spectral differential imaging> use source motion or spectral diversity to distinguish them.

= Differential-imaging self-subtraction
{parent=High-contrast imaging}

A companion or extended source contributes to the stellar reference used in differential imaging. Subtracting that reference removes part of the desired signal and distorts its shape, photometry or astrometry. Artificial-source injection and forward modelling can estimate the throughput loss.

= Simultaneous spectral differential imaging
{parent=High-contrast imaging}

= SSDI
{c}
{synonym}

Simultaneous spectral differential imaging compares images in nearby bands, rescaled so stellar <speckle patterns> approximately coincide. A companion has a different positional or spectral response. Simultaneity reduces temporal variation but does not remove chromatic and non-common-path errors.

= Angular differential imaging
{parent=High-contrast imaging}

= ADI
{c}
{synonym}

Angular differential imaging keeps the instrument pupil fixed while the sky rotates. A stellar reference <point spread function> is subtracted, then the residuals are derotated and combined. Insufficient rotation and imperfect reference selection cause <differential-imaging self-subtraction>.

= Fabry–Pérot interferometer
{c}
{parent=Optical instrument}

A Fabry–Pérot interferometer uses multiple reflections between two nearly parallel surfaces. Its transmission peaks select narrow <wavelength> intervals; scanning the spacing or incidence angle can build an imaging spectral cube.

= Lenslet array
{parent=Optical instrument}

A lenslet array is a set of small lenses that divides a field or a pupil into many optical samples. In an <integral field spectrograph> each field sample generates a microspectrum or feeds an <optical fiber>.

= Beam splitter
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Polarizing beam splitter
{parent=Beam splitter}

= Polarising beam splitter
{synonym}

A <polarizing beam splitter> sends two orthogonal <linear polarizations> to different output paths. Acting coherently on $\alpha|H\rangle+\beta|V\rangle$, it correlates path with <photon polarization> instead of measuring <photon polarization> unless a path is actually detected or discarded.

// Target: optics.bigb

= Mach-Zehnder interferometer
{parent=Beam splitter}
{c}
{wiki=Mach–Zehnder_interferometer}

A <Mach-Zehnder interferometer> splits an incident wave into two arms and recombines them at a second <beam splitter>. A relative <quantum phase> changes the interference at its output ports. With symmetric lossless 50:50 splitters, the ports can be calibrated to have detection <probabilities> $\cos^2(\theta/2)$ and $\sin^2(\theta/2)$.

// Target: optics.bigb

= Field of view
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

The <field of view> is the region of object directions sampled by an <optical instrument> in one pointing. State whether it is horizontal, vertical or diagonal, and whether it is a full <angle> or a half-<angle>. An object-plane width in millimetres also describes a <field of view>, but requires a specified object distance. Neither that width nor the angular <field of view> is the <aperture> <diameter> or the <diffraction-limited resolution>.

For a centred flat rectangular <photodetector> of width $w$ in the <focal plane>, with positive effective <focal length> $f$, infinity focus and ideal rectilinear imaging without <optical distortion>, the full horizontal <field of view> is
$$
\Theta_x=2\arctan\left(\frac{w}{2f}\right).
$$
The model maps an object direction $\theta$ to image height of magnitude $|y|=f|\tan\theta|$; set $|y|=w/2$ and use the <inverse tangent>. This retains exact <right triangle> geometry within the stipulated imaging model; a paraxial <focal length> alone does not guarantee that a real wide-field <optical lens> obeys it. With detector height $h$, replace $w$ by $h$ for the vertical <field of view>, and by $\sqrt{w^2+h^2}$ for the diagonal <field of view>. The <small-angle approximation> gives $\Theta_x\simeq w/f$ in radians. https://www.edmundoptics.com/knowledge-center/application-notes/imaging/understanding-focal-length-and-field-of-view/[Gregory Hollows and Nicholas James, Edmund Optics, Understanding Focal Length and Field of View]{ref}.

For an original worked example, a $36\,\mathrm{mm}$ detector width gives $\Theta_x\approx39.60^\circ$ at $f=50\,\mathrm{mm}$, and $20.41^\circ$ at $f=100\,\mathrm{mm}$. Keeping $D=25\,\mathrm{mm}$ makes these f/2 and f/4 respectively: the <field of view> changes because $f$ changes. By contrast, closing $D$ from $25$ to $12.5\,\mathrm{mm}$ at $f=100\,\mathrm{mm}$ changes f/4 to f/8 while retaining $20.41^\circ$ in this model. The clean separation is useful: <entrance pupil> <area> controls collection, <focal ratio> controls the paraxial cone, and $w/f$ controls angular image coverage. Edge clipping, <optical distortion>, <field curvature> and <optical aberrations> can restrict the practically usable <field of view>.

\Image[aperture-focal-ratio-field-view-sensor-geometry.png]
{title=<Field of view>: a fixed detector width subtends a smaller <angle> at longer <focal length>}
{description=Original ideal rectilinear model. Left: central <light rays> cross the <thin lens> centre and reach the two detector edges, with equal coordinate scales in both panels; the opening is $25\,\mathrm{mm}$ in each. Right: calculated full horizontal <field of view> for $w=36\,\mathrm{mm}$, with the $50$ and $100\,\mathrm{mm}$ examples marked. The detector lies in the <focal plane>, the object is at infinity, and <optical distortion> is absent. No plotted quantity is measured data.}

A proposed angular readout on an <optical bench> illuminates a small asymmetric target at the <optical focus> of a <collimator>, providing a bundle with a known direction. Rotate the <optical camera> about the centre of its <entrance pupil>, reading a graduated rotation stage, and record the target image position on its fixed <photodetector>. Centre the target to establish zero; turn left and right until its centre reaches each detector edge. The difference between stage readings measures the full horizontal <field of view>. Repeat with a smaller <aperture stop>: unchanged edge directions but reduced signal support the distinction between collection and angular coverage. Keep the same active detector width, infinity focus, illumination, integration time and gain; approach each reading from the same direction to check mechanical backlash. A finite target blurs the edge criterion, detector thresholds can imitate lost coverage, and a wide bundle must illuminate the whole <entrance pupil> at every tested <angle>. These checks distinguish a geometrical boundary from insufficient signal. The analytical diagram is the prediction for this protocol, not a record of its execution.

= Optical telescope
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Altazimuth mount
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

= Alt-az mount
{synonym}

An <altazimuth mount> points an <optical telescope> using <altitude> and <azimuth> rotations. A mount restricted to elevations no greater than the <zenith> must change its <azimuth> by approximately half a turn when a target crosses the <zenith>.

= Zenith blind spot
{parent=Altazimuth mount}

A <zenith blind spot> is a region in which an <altazimuth mount> cannot follow a target because the required axis motion exceeds a drive constraint. A finite slew across an exact <zenith> crossing gives a lost-track interval; a full forbidden footprint additionally depends on the target trajectory, acceleration and the control strategy. It need not be circular.

= Giant Magellan Telescope
{c}
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

The Giant Magellan Telescope design combines seven 8.4-metre primary mirrors, including off-axis segments. It was a planned extremely large telescope in the 2018 examination context.

= Thirty Meter Telescope
{c}
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

The Thirty Meter Telescope design uses a thirty-metre primary with 492 hexagonal segments. It was among the planned extremely large telescopes in the 2018 examination context.

= W. M. Keck Observatory
{c}
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

= Keck telescope
{c}
{synonym}

The two Keck telescopes each use a ten-metre primary made from 36 hexagonal segments.

= Large Binocular Telescope
{c}
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

The Large Binocular Telescope has two 8.4-metre primary mirrors with lightweight honeycomb structures.

= Segmented mirror
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

A segmented mirror assembles smaller mirrors into a large aperture. Sensors and actuators maintain position and figure; coherent imaging additionally requires <mirror segment phasing>.

= Mirror segment phasing
{parent=Segmented mirror}

Phasing adjusts relative piston and tilt so adjacent segments form a continuous optical wavefront. Near normal reflection a surface piston changes <optical path length> by twice that displacement.

= Lightweight telescope mirror
{parent=Optical telescope}

A lightweight mirror uses a thin optical face backed by ribs or a honeycomb structure. The deep backing provides rigidity with less mass than a solid disk and facilitates thermal equilibration.

= Active optics
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

Active optics uses controlled support forces and alignment to correct slow changes in telescope figure, particularly gravity and thermal deformation. It permits thin large primary mirrors. <Adaptive optics> instead corrects rapid <atmospheric turbulence>.

= Schmidt camera
{c}
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

= Schmidt telescope
{c}
{synonym}

= Classical Schmidt telescope
{synonym}

A Schmidt camera combines a spherical primary mirror with a thin aspheric corrector at the mirror’s centre of curvature. The corrector compensates <spherical aberration>; the symmetric stop location gives a wide useful field. The native focal surface is curved and lies inside the instrument.

= Reflecting telescope
{parent=Optical telescope}
{wiki}

A reflecting telescope forms an image using curved mirrors. Its primary and secondary shapes, spacing and aperture stop determine its <optical aberrations>, access to the focus, and obstruction.

= Gregorian telescope
{c}
{parent=Reflecting telescope}
{wiki}

A Gregorian telescope has a concave parabolic primary and a concave ellipsoidal secondary beyond the primary focus. Light crosses the intermediate focus, reaches the secondary, and returns through the primary to the final focus. The ellipsoid’s two foci are the intermediate and final focus.

= Cassegrain reflector
{c}
{parent=Reflecting telescope}
{wiki}

A Cassegrain arrangement places a secondary before the primary focus and returns light through a hole in the primary to a focus behind it. In the classical design the primary is parabolic and the secondary convex hyperbolic. The compact arrangement has a long effective <focal length>; its classical implementation retains off-axis <coma>.

= Ritchey–Chrétien telescope
{c}
{parent=Cassegrain reflector}

= Ritchey–Chrétien reflector
{c}
{synonym}

A Ritchey–Chrétien telescope has hyperbolic primary and secondary mirrors chosen to cancel third-order <spherical aberration> and <coma>. It retains <astigmatism> and <field curvature>; wide-field instruments may require additional correction.

= Classical Cassegrain reflector
{parent=Cassegrain reflector}

A classical Cassegrain reflector uses a concave parabolic primary and a convex hyperbolic secondary before the prime focus. The secondary returns the converging beam through a hole in the primary. It corrects on-axis <spherical aberration> but retains off-axis <coma>.

= Newtonian telescope
{c}
{parent=Reflecting telescope}
{wiki}

= Newtonian reflector
{c}
{synonym}

A Newtonian telescope uses a concave parabolic primary and a flat diagonal secondary to send the converging beam to a side focus. The primary avoids on-axis <spherical aberration>, but has off-axis <coma>.

= Diffraction limit of a telescope
{parent=Optical telescope}

The angular <diffraction-limited resolution> of a telescope scales as $\lambda/D$, with <wavelength> $\lambda$ and illuminated diameter $D$. Larger diameter resolves smaller angular structure, while longer wavelength makes atmospheric phase correction easier.

= Entrance pupil
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Optical microscope
{parent=Optical instrument}

An <optical microscope> uses <optical lens> elements to obtain a magnified image of a small object or an intermediate image. A calibrated eyepiece can measure image dimensions; its own <diffraction> and <optical aberrations> limit the readout.

= Phase-contrast microscopy
{parent=Optical microscope}
{wiki}

= Phase-contrast microscope
{parent=Phase-contrast microscopy}

An <optical microscope> configured for <phase-contrast microscopy>.

= Ronchi ruling
{c}
{parent=Optical instrument}

A <Ronchi ruling> is a grating of alternating transparent and opaque parallel bands. A known band spacing can calibrate the scale of an <optical microscope> image.

= Collimator
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

A collimator turns light from a source near its focus into a nearly parallel beam. A slit of width $x$ produces an angular width approximately $x/f$, where $f$ is the <focal length>.

= Spectrometer
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Fourier transform spectroscopy
{c}
{parent=Spectrometer}
{wiki}

Fourier transform spectroscopy measures an interferogram as a function of <optical path length> difference. Its <Fourier transform> recovers the spectrum. An imaging implementation obtains an interferogram at every detector pixel, but a scanned cube is not simultaneous at all wavelengths.

= Spectrograph
{parent=Spectrometer}

A spectrograph records the <optical spectrum> dispersed by an instrument. Its <spectral resolving power> describes which nearby wavelengths it separates.

= Spectrophotometry
{parent=Spectrograph}

<Spectrophotometry> measures received <light> as a function of <wavelength>, accounting for the instrument's <wavelength>-dependent response.

= Spectral resolution element
{parent=Spectrograph}

= SRE
{c}
{synonym}

A <spectral resolution element> is a spectral interval at the adopted instrumental resolution. At <spectral resolving power> $R$, its width is $\Delta\lambda=\lambda/R$. The number of <photodetector> <detector pixels> sampling it is distinct from $R$.

= Linear spectral dispersion
{parent=Spectrograph}
{title2=$q=dx/d\lambda$}

<Linear spectral dispersion> is the derivative of the <photodetector> coordinate in the dispersion direction with respect to <wavelength>. Near the <optical axis>, $q=f_{\rm cam}\,d\beta/d\lambda$.

= Integral field spectrograph
{parent=Spectrograph}

An integral field spectrograph records a spectrum for each of many spatial elements across a two-dimensional field, producing a <spectral data cube>. Field reformatters include <lenslet arrays>, <optical fibers> and <image slicers>.

= Integral-field spectrograph volume scaling
{parent=Integral field spectrograph}

With fixed <spectral resolving power>, <wavelength> interval, <diffraction grating> geometry, <photodetector> sampling and <collimator> <focal ratio>, a beam <diameter> scales as the <spaxel> width $\theta$, but the <optical camera> <focal length> stays fixed. A simple on-axis beam-envelope model therefore gives <collimator> and disperser volumes proportional to $\theta^3$ and <optical camera> volume proportional to $\theta^2$. With a fixed number of <spaxels> per <photodetector>, replication multiplies these volumes by $M\propto\theta^{-2}$. A fixed <photodetector> field adds transverse clearances and changes the small-$\theta$ limit; these exponents are not universal mechanical-volume laws.

= Spaxel
{parent=Integral field spectrograph}

A <spaxel> is one spatial sampling element in a <spectral data cube>. For a square field of angular side $\beta$ sampled at angular side $\theta$, the number of <spaxels> is $(\beta/\theta)^2$, before edge rounding.

= Image slicer
{parent=Integral field spectrograph}

An image slicer divides a two-dimensional image into narrow strips and optically rearranges them into a long pseudo-slit. A <spectrograph> then disperses each strip while preserving its position along the slit.

= Spectral data cube
{parent=Integral field spectrograph}
{title2=$I(x,y,\lambda)$}

A spectral data cube stores intensity $I(x,y,\lambda)$ with two spatial coordinates and one spectral coordinate. The third coordinate is <wavelength>, not an additional spatial dimension.

= Spectral resolving power
{parent=Spectrograph}
{title2=$R=\lambda/\Delta\lambda$}

Spectral resolving power is $R=\lambda/\Delta\lambda$, where $\Delta\lambda$ is the wavelength separation just resolved under the specified instrumental and source conditions.

= Cross-dispersed echelle spectrograph
{parent=Spectrograph}

A cross-dispersed echelle spectrograph uses an <echelle grating> for strong dispersion and a <cross-disperser> in the perpendicular direction to separate overlapping <diffraction orders>. It records many orders simultaneously in an echellogram.

= Cross-disperser
{parent=Cross-dispersed echelle spectrograph}

A cross-disperser is a second dispersing element, often an <optical prism> or <diffraction grating>, oriented to separate <optical spectra> perpendicular to their principal dispersion direction.

= Echellogram
{parent=Cross-dispersed echelle spectrograph}

An echellogram is the two-dimensional detector pattern of separated <diffraction orders> from a <cross-dispersed echelle spectrograph>.

= Multi-object spectrograph
{parent=Spectrograph}

A multi-object spectrograph records <optical spectra> of many selected objects simultaneously. The main designs are <multi-slit spectrographs> and <fiber-fed spectrographs>. The https://www.eso.org/sci/facilities/paranal/instruments/multiobject.html[ESO instrumentation overview] describes both arrangements.

= Multi-slit spectrograph
{parent=Multi-object spectrograph}

A multi-slit spectrograph puts separate slits at target positions in the focal plane. Each slit transmits the target plus nearby sky; dispersion places its spatially resolved <optical spectrum> on the detector.

= Fiber-fed spectrograph
{parent=Multi-object spectrograph}

A fiber-fed spectrograph places <optical fibers> on selected targets and reformats their output into a common entrance slit. Dedicated sky fibers sample <sky brightness>. Fiber coupling, transmission, and output beam spreading affect throughput and resolution.

= Optical fiber
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Optical fibre
{synonym}

= Focal-ratio degradation
{parent=Optical fiber}

Focal-ratio degradation is the broadening of the output angular cone of an <optical fiber> relative to its input cone. It can make a spectrograph collimator lose transmitted light or require larger optics. Stress, bends, and mode mixing in the fiber can contribute.

= Adaptive optics
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

Adaptive optics measures changing <wavefront errors> and corrects them using a <deformable mirror>. A <wavefront sensor> supplies measurements to a fast feedback controller; a <guide star> provides the reference. It improves <astronomical seeing> toward <diffraction-limited resolution>.

= Deformable mirror
{parent=Adaptive optics}
{wiki}

A deformable mirror changes its optical surface using an array of actuators. For reflection near normal incidence, surface displacement changes <optical path length> by twice that displacement.

= Deformable-mirror Fourier-mode counting
{parent=Deformable mirror}

For actuator pitch $D/N$, full-cycle <spatial frequency> components are limited to $N/2$ by sampling. Integer lattice counting in a circular <frequency> disk gives approximately $\pi N^2/4$ signed points; conjugate pairing halves the number of pairs, and two real <wave phase> quadratures per pair restore approximately $\pi N^2/4$ real coefficients. Counting only positive-quadrant <frequency> representatives gives $\pi N^2/16$, but it omits other orientations or quadratures unless a restricted convention is specified. Area approximations have boundary corrections and do not give exact integer ranks. A square sampling cutoff and a circular illuminated actuator footprint are distinct geometric restrictions.

= Deformable-mirror control radius
{parent=Deformable mirror}
{title2=$\theta_{\max}=N\lambda/(2D)$}

With $N$ actuators across diameter $D$, the projected pitch is approximately $D/N$. The <Nyquist spatial frequency> is $N/(2D)$, so the largest correctable angular speckle displacement along an actuator row is $N\lambda/(2D)$. A square actuator array gives a square ideal spatial-frequency region. See the https://arxiv.org/abs/astro-ph/0510597[Bordé–Traub wavefront-control derivation].

= Wavefront sensor
{parent=Adaptive optics}
{wiki}

A wavefront sensor measures information from which a <wavefront error> can be reconstructed. For example, a <Shack–Hartmann wavefront sensor> measures local wavefront slopes using lenslets.

= Shack–Hartmann wavefront sensor
{c}
{parent=Wavefront sensor}
{wiki}

= Guide star
{disambiguate=adaptive optics}
{parent=Adaptive optics}
{wiki}

= Guide star
{synonym}

A guide star provides photons for a <wavefront sensor>. Its brightness and angular separation from the target limit <adaptive optics> sky coverage.

= Laser guide star
{parent=Guide star (adaptive optics)}
{wiki}

A laser guide star is an artificial atmospheric beacon for <adaptive optics>. It improves sky coverage but samples a finite-distance cone rather than the full incoming stellar beam. Conventional systems still require a natural reference for absolute image motion.

= Adaptive-optics sky coverage
{parent=Adaptive optics}

Adaptive-optics sky coverage is the fraction of targets for which a sufficiently bright nearby <guide star> allows useful correction. <Anisoplanatism> limits the offset, and photon statistics limit the sensing speed. A <laser guide star> improves coverage but does not remove all low-order and finite-distance limitations.

= Optical attenuator
{parent=Optical instrument}

= Monochromator
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

A <monochromator> selects a narrow, adjustable <wavelength> interval from an <optical spectrum>. Its finite spectral bandwidth matters when calibrating a <photodetector>'s <spectral responsivity> in <calibrated radiant-flux measurement>.

= Optical power meter
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

An <optical power meter> measures collected <radiant flux> using a calibrated detector and readout. With a <photodiode>, its conversion to <watts> depends on <spectral responsivity>, as in <calibrated radiant-flux measurement>.

= Photodetector
{parent=Optical instrument}
{wiki}

= Spectral responsivity
{parent=Photodetector}
{title2=$R(\lambda)=I/P$}

For a <photodiode> in its linear regime, <spectral responsivity> is the dark-subtracted <electric current> per incident <radiant flux> in a narrow band at <wavelength> $\lambda$, measured in $\mathrm{A\,W^{-1}}$. More generally a <photodetector>'s readout can be electrical voltage or another quantity, with corresponding units. A broad-band readout weights the incoming <optical spectrum> by the detector's <spectral responsivity>; it is not the unweighted total incident <power>. https://www.nist.gov/programs-projects/spectral-responsivity-measurements-and-spatial-spectral-power-responsivity[NIST, Spectral responsivity measurements: wavelength-dependent sensitivity and spatial characterization]{ref}.

= Detector bias frame
{parent=Photodetector}

= Bias image
{synonym}

A <photodetector> bias frame is an exposure of effectively zero integration <time> used to estimate the electronic offset and <read noise>. For independent identical bias reads, the <variance> of their difference is twice the single-read <variance>; subtracting an estimated mean bias removes the offset but adds the uncertainty of that estimate.

= Flat-field correction
{parent=Photodetector}
{wiki}

= Flatfield correction
{synonym}

<Flat-field correction> compensates stable multiplicative variations in <photodetector> response using calibrated illumination. Identically illuminated exposure ratios cancel the stable response in their mean, but their <photon shot noise> remains signal dependent.

= Analogue-to-digital unit
{parent=Photodetector}

= ADU
{c}
{synonym}

= Data number
{synonym}

An analogue-to-digital unit is a unit of a digitized <photodetector> output after electronic conversion. Its relation to collected <electrons> is set by the <detector conversion gain>; an additive electronic offset must be removed before interpreting a count as a <light> signal.

= Detector conversion gain
{parent=Photodetector}
{title2=$g$}

= Detector gain
{synonym}

<Detector conversion gain> is the number of collected <electrons> per <analogue-to-digital unit>. With independent <Poisson distribution> photoelectron counts, the shot-noise <variance> in count units is the mean count divided by the gain. Detected-photon and <electron> conventions agree only when each detected <photon> contributes one <electron>.

= Gain estimation from flat-field ratios
{parent=Detector conversion gain}

Let independent exposure sums $A,B$ have mean $N$ <ADU> at each <detector pixel>, with pure <Poisson distribution> noise and fixed gain $g$. The <delta method> gives $A/B\simeq1+(A-N)/N-(B-N)/N$, so $\operatorname{Var}(A/B)\simeq2/(gN)$ and $g\simeq2/(NE^2)$. For nonuniform <detector pixel> means $N_i$, an unweighted spatial ratio <variance> is instead $(2/g)\langle1/N_i\rangle$. This high-count approximation requires linear unsaturated data, bias subtraction and negligible <read noise>.

= Bias of pooled flat-field ratio gain
{parent=Gain estimation from flat-field ratios}
{title2=$g_{\rm naive}/g=1/(\langle N\rangle\langle1/N\rangle)$}

For stable positive <detector pixel> means $N_i$ and uniform <detector conversion gain> $g$, high-count independent <Poisson distribution> noise gives $\operatorname{Var}(A_i/B_i)\simeq2/(gN_i)$. Thus substituting a global mean in the equal-signal formula yields $g_{\rm naive}=g/[\langle N_i\rangle\langle1/N_i\rangle]\le g$, by the <Jensen inequality>. Equality holds for uniform means. The <flat-field correction> pattern cancels from the ratio mean but not from the signal-dependent noise. Local signal bins, or a <variance> fit after normalization of differences by the square root of their local mean, avoid this leading bias.

= Optical detector pixel
{parent=Photodetector}

= Detector pixel
{synonym}

An optical <photodetector> <detector pixel> is a spatially sampled photosensitive element. Counts from distinct <detector pixels> have independent <photon shot noise> in the ideal model, but charge transfer, electrical coupling and resampling can produce <covariance>.

= Photodiode
{parent=Photodetector}
{wiki}

A photodiode is a <semiconductor> diode that converts absorbed photons into electrical carriers. A reverse-biased detector separates the carriers and collects charge; its <quantum efficiency> and <dark current> determine sensitivity.

= Dark current
{disambiguate=physics}
{parent=Photodetector}
{wiki}

= Dark current
{synonym}

Dark current is charge produced in a detector without illumination, often by thermally excited carriers. Cooling reduces it, although cooling requirements also depend on the wavelength range and thermal background.

= Correlated double sampling
{parent=Photodetector}
{wiki}

Correlated double sampling differences a signal reading and its associated reset or baseline reading. It suppresses correlated offsets and reset noise, while the noise of the two measurements must still be propagated.

= Hybrid infrared detector
{parent=Photodetector}

A hybrid infrared detector bonds a photosensitive <semiconductor> array to a separate silicon readout circuit. Each pixel has its own electrical connection; charge need not be transferred across the array as in a <charge-coupled device>.

= Detector reference pixel
{parent=Hybrid infrared detector}

A detector reference pixel has no illuminated photosensitive connection and samples readout offsets. Reference-pixel subtraction can suppress common electronic drifts but does not measure the sky background or all pixel-dependent errors.

= Nondestructive detector readout
{parent=Hybrid infrared detector}

A nondestructive read samples a pixel’s accumulated signal without resetting it. Repeated samples permit <Fowler sampling> and <up-the-ramp sampling>, and can help identify saturation and cosmic-ray charge steps.

= Up-the-ramp sampling
{parent=Nondestructive detector readout}

Up-the-ramp sampling measures accumulated charge repeatedly through an exposure and estimates flux from its slope. Cosmic-ray steps, saturation and nonlinearity must be identified or modelled.

= Fowler sampling
{c}
{parent=Nondestructive detector readout}

Fowler sampling averages several nondestructive reads near the start and end of an integration and differences the two averages. For independent equal read noise, averaging lowers the read-noise variance; correlated noise limits the improvement.

= H2RG detector
{c}
{parent=Hybrid infrared detector}

= HAWAII-2RG
{c}
{synonym}

The HAWAII-2RG architecture has a $2048\times2048$ grid with an 18-micrometre pitch. For infrared work its readout circuit is normally bonded to a <mercury cadmium telluride> absorber. Reference pixels track electronic offsets and an independently addressed guide window can be read alongside the science array.

= Background subtraction
{parent=Photodetector}

Background subtraction estimates a source contribution by removing a sky or detector-background measurement. An independent background estimate adds <photon shot noise>; a mismatched background adds <bias of an estimator>.

= Systematic-error signal-to-noise ceiling
{parent=Background subtraction}
{title2=$Z_\infty$}

Let $X\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(Q+B)$ and $Y\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(fB)$ be independent. Then $X-Y$ has source-estimation bias $(1-f)B$ and variance $Q+(1+f)B$. Its accuracy ratio $Q/\sqrt{\mathbb E[(X-Y-Q)^2]}$ tends to $R_Q/(|1-f|R_B)$ when $Q=R_QT$, $B=R_BT$ and $T$ grows. This uses <mean squared error>, not variance alone, and assumes a fixed uncorrected mismatch.

= Read noise
{parent=Photodetector}

Read noise is uncertainty added by measuring detector charge or voltage. It is often expressed as an equivalent number of input electrons per pixel per read.

= Photon shot noise
{parent=Photodetector}

Independent photon arrivals have a <Poisson distribution>: a mean count $\mu$ has variance $\mu$ and standard deviation $\sqrt\mu$. Subtraction of independent counting measurements adds their variances.

= Quantum efficiency
{parent=Photodetector}
{title2=$\eta$}
{wiki}

Quantum efficiency is the mean fraction of incident photons producing collected signal carriers. Noise-equivalent quantum efficiency can be lower when amplification adds fluctuations; this does not imply a corresponding loss of actual photon absorption.

= Charge-coupled device
{parent=Photodetector}
{wiki}

= CCD
{c}
{synonym}

A charge-coupled device stores photoelectrons in potential wells and transfers the charge through clocked stages to an output amplifier. The transfer differs from independently addressed pixels in a <hybrid infrared detector>.

= Electron-multiplying CCD
{parent=Charge-coupled device}
{title2=$g$}

= EMCCD
{c}
{synonym}

An electron-multiplying CCD adds a high-field multiplication register before the output amplifier. <Impact ionization> generates a large mean gain, reducing input-referred <read noise> but adding <multiplication excess noise>.

= Multiplication excess noise
{parent=Electron-multiplying CCD}
{title2=$F$}

Random multiplication increases the noise relative to deterministic gain. If the output from one input electron has mean $g$ and variance $g^2$, a <Poisson distribution> of input electrons with mean $\mu$ produces output variance $2g^2\mu$, by the <law of total variance>. The high-gain analog-mode excess-noise factor is $\sqrt2$.

= Impact ionization
{parent=Electron-multiplying CCD}
{wiki}

An energetic charge carrier creates an additional electron–hole pair. In a multiplication register this is stochastic, so the output charge fluctuates even when the input electron number is fixed.

= Signal-to-noise ratio in photon counting
{parent=Photodetector}

For source counts $S$ and independent background counts $B$ with <Poisson distributions>, the ideal counting <standard deviation> is $\sqrt{S+B}$, so the signal-to-noise ratio is $S/\sqrt{S+B}$. Detector noise and uncertainty in the estimated background add further variance. A larger sky aperture raises $B$, while better throughput raises $S$; both affect the faintness limit of a <spectrograph>.
